NCLEXPN Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B: taking and recording routine vital signs on a stable client is a task within the training and scope of a UAP and does not require nursing judgment. Assessing a new client's admission history requires professional judgment and belongs to a licensed nurse. Teaching a client about a new medication requires nursing knowledge the UAP is not trained to provide. Evaluating a client's response to pain medication requires clinical judgment that only a licensed nurse can perform.
  2. The correct answer is C: a client reporting new onset chest pain and shortness of breath may be experiencing a life threatening cardiac or respiratory event and must be assessed immediately using the airway, breathing, circulation priority framework. A client with a healing surgical incision that itches is a lower priority comfort concern. A client due for a scheduled dressing change can wait until the more urgent client is stabilized. A client requesting a warm blanket has a comfort need, not a physiological emergency.
  3. The correct answer is D: obtaining informed consent and explaining the risks, benefits, and alternatives of a procedure is the legal responsibility of the surgeon or provider performing it, so the nurse should notify the provider of the client's questions before the consent form is signed. Providing the explanation herself would place the nurse outside her legal scope of practice. Telling the client to sign and ask questions afterward does not satisfy informed consent requirements. Refusing to discuss the topic at all leaves the client's concern unaddressed.
  4. The correct answer is D: an incident report is an internal quality improvement and risk management document and is not part of the client's permanent medical record, so it should never be referenced in the chart. Copying the report into the nurses' notes would improperly merge the two documents. The report is completed by the staff member who discovered or was involved in the event, not only the charge nurse. It is reviewed by risk management, not destroyed after the physician signs it.
  5. The correct answer is B: when a concern is not addressed appropriately at the immediate level, the nurse has a professional and legal duty to follow the facility's chain of command and notify the next level of supervision so the client's safety is protected. Documenting the conversation alone does not ensure the client receives needed care. Waiting for the next shift could delay treatment of a worsening condition. Contacting the family before escalating internally bypasses the facility's own safety process.
  6. The correct answer is C: the Assessment component of SBAR is the nurse's clinical interpretation of the data, such as identifying that the client's vital signs are consistent with early sepsis. The client's admitting diagnosis and comorbidities belong in the Background component. A request for the provider to see the client belongs in the Recommendation component. The client's current oxygen saturation reading alone is objective data that belongs in the Background or Situation component, not the interpretation.
  7. The correct answer is D: reading the order back to the provider word for word confirms accuracy before the medication is given and is required by facility policy and safe medication practice. Administering the medication immediately skips a required verification step. Waiting for a written order before doing anything could delay urgently needed treatment when a telephone order is appropriate. Asking a colleague to countersign does not replace the mandatory read back to the prescriber.
  8. The correct answer is B: a competent adult client has the legal and ethical right to refuse any treatment, including medication, and the nurse's role is to document the refusal and the education provided, not to force compliance. Administering the medication against the client's wishes violates the client's autonomy and constitutes battery. Reporting the client to security is not an appropriate response to a lawful refusal. Withholding all further medications punishes the client rather than respecting an informed decision.
  9. The correct answer is D: autonomy is the ethical principle that respects a client's right to make decisions about their own care, and completing an advance directive is a direct exercise of that right. Beneficence refers to acting in the client's best interest, not the client's own decision making. Nonmaleficence refers to avoiding harm rather than self determination. Justice refers to fair distribution of healthcare resources, not an individual's personal choice.
  10. The correct answer is C: veracity is the ethical duty to tell the truth, and giving the client clear, accurate information about the diagnosis directly reflects this principle. Fidelity refers to keeping promises and being faithful to professional commitments, not honesty specifically. Justice refers to fair treatment and resource allocation. Nonmaleficence refers to avoiding harm, which is a related but separate concept from truth telling.
  11. The correct answer is D: a valid DNR order means the healthcare team should not initiate cardiopulmonary resuscitation, and the nurse's responsibility is to provide comfort measures while respecting the client's documented wishes. Beginning chest compressions and calling a code directly contradicts the order. Calling the family before acting delays appropriate comfort care. Performing rescue breathing alone still violates the DNR directive since any resuscitative effort is excluded.
  12. The correct answer is B: nurses are mandated reporters, and suspected abuse or exploitation of a vulnerable adult must be reported to the appropriate adult protective services agency regardless of the nurse's certainty about the facts. Waiting for proof before reporting could allow ongoing harm to continue. Discussing the concern with the family caregiver first could place the client at greater risk of retaliation. Documenting the concern without reporting it does not fulfill the nurse's legal reporting duty.
  13. The correct answer is D: Good Samaritan laws generally protect a healthcare provider who voluntarily renders emergency care in good faith and within the scope of their training from civil liability for ordinary negligence. The protection does not apply once the nurse is paid or acting within a work assignment, since that removes the voluntary, off duty nature of the act. The protection also does not extend to acts of gross negligence or willful misconduct. It applies regardless of whether the injured person later consents in writing, since consent is implied in an emergency.
  14. The correct answer is C: sharing relevant clinical information with another provider who is directly involved in the client's ongoing treatment is a permitted disclosure for treatment purposes under HIPAA. Discussing a client's diagnosis with a family member who is not involved in care and has not been authorized violates confidentiality. Posting details about a client's condition on a personal social media account is a clear HIPAA violation. Looking up a coworker's chart out of curiosity is an unauthorized access violation even though the nurse works at the facility.
  15. The correct answer is B: physical restraints are a last resort under legal and ethical guidelines, so the nurse must first exhaust reasonable less restrictive alternatives, such as moving the client closer to the nurses station for closer observation, and obtain a provider order if a restraint truly becomes necessary. Applying a restraint without first trying less restrictive measures violates client rights. Restraining the client without a provider order is not legally permitted except in a narrowly defined emergency. Sedating the client solely for staff convenience is a form of chemical restraint used inappropriately.
  16. The correct answer is C: negligence describes a failure to act with the degree of care a reasonably prudent nurse would use in a similar situation, and failing to verify identity before administering medication is a breach of that standard even though no harm occurred this time. Malpractice specifically requires that the breach of duty actually caused demonstrable harm to the client, which did not happen here. Assault involves the threat of harmful contact, not a medication error. Battery involves actual unauthorized physical contact with intent, which does not describe this scenario.
  17. The correct answer is D: a durable power of attorney for healthcare is the legal document that formally names a specific person, called a healthcare proxy, to make medical decisions on the client's behalf when the client cannot. A living will states general treatment preferences but does not name a decision maker. A do not resuscitate order addresses only resuscitation status, not broader decision making authority. A financial power of attorney authorizes decisions about money and property, not medical care.
  18. The correct answers are A, C, and D: assisting with ambulation, recording intake and output, and repositioning a stable client every two hours are all routine tasks within a UAP's training that do not require nursing judgment. Assessing the client's surgical incision for signs of infection requires clinical judgment reserved for a licensed nurse. Teaching the client about home wound care also requires licensed nursing knowledge and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
  19. The correct answers are A, B, and D: a missing narcotic count, an unlocked medication cart left unattended, and a client who eloped from the unit all represent serious safety and security breaches that must be reported immediately. A client who ate seventy five percent of a meal is a normal, reassuring finding. A linen cart parked in the hallway during a busy shift is a minor housekeeping issue, not an urgent safety concern requiring immediate escalation.
  20. The correct answers are A, C, and D: administering oral and intramuscular medications, performing wound care and dressing changes, and reinforcing teaching that has already been initiated by a registered nurse are all within the standard scope of practice for an LPN. Independently developing the initial nursing plan of care requires the comprehensive assessment and judgment reserved for a registered nurse. Administering a first dose of a blood product through a central line is typically restricted to a registered nurse due to the high risk of a life threatening transfusion reaction.
  21. The correct answers are A, B, and E: unexplained bruising in various stages of healing, a caregiver who refuses to allow the nurse to speak privately with the client, and evidence that the client's medications and food are consistently missing all raise concern for possible abuse or neglect and must be reported. A client who is well groomed and appropriately dressed is a reassuring finding. A caregiver who asks detailed questions about the plan of care shows engagement, not a warning sign.
  22. The correct answers are A, B, and C: physical therapy for mobility and strength, occupational therapy for activities of daily living, and speech language pathology for a swallowing evaluation are all appropriate given the client's residual weakness and swallowing difficulty. A referral to a dietitian would only be necessary if additional nutritional concerns beyond swallowing safety were present, which is not indicated here. A referral to a genetic counselor is not relevant to recovery from a stroke.
  23. The correct answer is D: a durable power of attorney for healthcare names a specific proxy to make medical decisions on the client's behalf. A living will documents general treatment preferences but does not name a decision maker. An incident report is an internal quality document unrelated to decision making authority. A HIPAA authorization only permits release of health information, not decision making power.
  24. The correct answer is B: veracity is the duty to tell the truth, and proactively informing a client about possible side effects reflects honest, complete communication. Justice refers to the fair distribution of care and resources, not truth telling. Beneficence refers to acting for the client's benefit generally, which is related but distinct from the specific act of truthful disclosure. Fidelity refers to keeping professional promises and commitments rather than honesty about risk.
  25. The correct answer is A, the standard SBAR order of situation, background, assessment, and recommendation: this sequence moves logically from what is happening now, to relevant history, to the nurse's clinical interpretation, and finally to what is being requested. Option B places the recommendation before the assessment has even been shared, which leaves the listener without the reasoning behind the request. Option C places background before situation, reversing the intended flow of a handoff. Option D omits the assessment component entirely and jumps straight from background to recommendation.
  26. The correct answer is A: the nurse must first ensure the client's immediate safety and assess for injury, then notify the registered nurse and provider of the change in condition, then document the objective findings in the medical record, and finally complete the internal incident report. Option B documents before the provider has even been notified of the event. Option C completes the incident report before the client has been assessed, which endangers client safety. Option D notifies the provider before assessing the client, skipping the most urgent first step.
  27. The correct answers are A and B: an unlocked medication cart left unattended in the hallway and a missing count of a controlled substance are both serious safety and security breaches that require immediate notification of the supervisor. A client who ambulated in the hallway with steady gait is a normal, reassuring finding. A family member who brought outside food that was later shared with the client's permission is a routine, non urgent event.
  28. The correct answers are Blank 1: A and Blank 2: A: the surgeon or performing provider is legally responsible for obtaining informed consent by explaining the risks, benefits, and alternatives, while the practical nurse's role is limited to witnessing the client's signature and confirming the client appears to understand. The charge nurse and the client's family have no legal role in obtaining informed consent for a procedure.
  29. The correct answer is B: a root cause analysis is a systematic quality improvement process used to identify the underlying system failures that allowed a sentinel event to occur, so similar errors can be prevented in the future. Its purpose is not to determine an individual staff member's punishment, since a punitive focus discourages honest reporting of future errors. It is not limited to a private conversation with only the nurse who administered the medication, since a true system analysis involves the broader care team and processes. It also does not conclude once the client has recovered, since the goal is process improvement, not simply confirming the client's clinical outcome.
  30. The correct answer is A: airborne precautions require a fitted N95 or higher level respirator because the causative organism travels in droplet nuclei small enough to remain suspended in air and bypass a surgical mask. A surgical face mask blocks larger droplets but does not filter airborne particles of this size. Sterile gloves protect against contact transmission, not airborne transmission. A face shield protects the eyes from splash but does not filter inhaled air.
  31. The correct answer is B: MRSA is transmitted primarily through direct contact, so contact precautions using a gown and gloves are required to prevent transmission to other clients and staff. Airborne precautions with an N95 respirator are used for organisms like tuberculosis and measles that spread through fine airborne particles. Droplet precautions are used for organisms like influenza that spread through larger respiratory droplets. Standard precautions alone are insufficient once a known contact organism has been identified.
  32. The correct answer is C: hand hygiene is performed as the final step after all contaminated equipment has been removed, since the hands are considered contaminated throughout the doffing process until this last cleansing step. Gloves are removed first because they are the most heavily contaminated item. The gown is removed next since it also carries contamination from client contact. A properly fitted mask is removed before hand hygiene but after the gown, not last.
  33. The correct answer is A: the R in RACE stands for rescue, meaning the nurse must first remove any client from immediate danger before doing anything else. Activating the alarm comes next, after ensuring the immediate area is safe for the client. Confining the fire by closing doors happens after the alarm is activated. Extinguishing the fire is attempted last and only if it is small and safe to do so.
  34. The correct answer is D: checking two client identifiers, such as the client's full name and date of birth, against the medication administration record confirms the right client before any medication is given. Checking the medication label against the order three times confirms the right drug, not the right client. Verifying the prescribed dose against the client's weight confirms the right dose. Confirming the scheduled administration time confirms the right time, a separate check entirely.
  35. The correct answer is B: a client taking a sedative hypnotic medication who also has orthostatic hypotension has both an altered level of alertness and a physiologic drop in blood pressure on standing, a combination that significantly increases fall risk. Wearing non skid socks is a protective measure, not a risk factor. Having a bed alarm activated is also a protective safety measure. Using a rolling walker correctly with supervision reduces fall risk rather than increasing it.
  36. The correct answer is C: the immediate priority when a gas leak is suspected is to evacuate clients and staff from the immediate danger zone before doing anything else, since the risk of explosion or toxic exposure is severe. Calling maintenance is appropriate but only after people are moved to safety. Opening windows could worsen an explosion risk if done incorrectly and does not address the immediate danger to people. Documenting the odor in the chart is not an appropriate first response to an active safety hazard.
  37. The correct answer is D: alcohol based hand sanitizer is not effective against spore forming organisms such as Clostridioides difficile, so soap and water must be used instead, making this statement incorrect and in need of correction. Washing hands for at least twenty seconds is accurate guidance. Using alcohol based sanitizer when hands are not visibly soiled is correct standard practice. Washing hands before and after every client contact is also correct and reflects appropriate infection control technique.
  38. The correct answer is A: using a mechanical lift device for a client who cannot bear any weight protects both the client from falling and the nurse from a musculoskeletal injury, and is the recommended standard for this level of dependency. Attempting a manual transfer with one assistant places both the nurse and the client at high risk for injury. Asking the client to help pull up on the nurse's neck is an unsafe technique that can cause injury to both people. Sliding the client without any assistive device increases friction injury risk and does not address the inability to bear weight.
  39. The correct answer is B: droplet precautions require anyone entering the room, including visitors, to wear a surgical mask to prevent exposure to large respiratory droplets, so the nurse should provide one before allowing entry. Allowing the visitor to enter without protection places them at risk of infection. Telling the visitor they cannot see the client at all is unnecessarily restrictive when proper protective equipment can safely allow the visit. Only requiring the client to wear a mask does not protect the visitor from droplets in the air of the room.
  40. The correct answers are A, B, and D: standard precautions include performing hand hygiene before and after client contact, wearing gloves when contact with blood or body fluids is anticipated, and safely disposing of sharps in a puncture resistant container. Standard precautions apply to the care of every client, not only those with a known infection. Recapping used needles by hand is an unsafe practice that increases the risk of needlestick injury and is not part of standard precautions.
  41. The correct answers are A, B, and C: a history of a previous fall, the use of a diuretic medication that increases urinary urgency, and impaired vision are all recognized risk factors that increase the likelihood of a fall. A client who is fully oriented to person, place, and time has intact cognitive function, which is a protective factor rather than a risk. A client who ambulates independently with a steady gait and no assistive device is also at lower fall risk, not higher.
  42. The correct answers are A, C, and D: bending at the knees rather than the waist, keeping a wide base of support, and using a mechanical or friction reducing device for a dependent client all reduce the risk of musculoskeletal injury to the nurse. Twisting the spine while lifting a heavy load increases injury risk rather than reducing it. Holding a heavy load far away from the body increases strain on the lower back instead of protecting it.
  43. The correct answers are A, B, and D: performing hand hygiene interrupts the mode of transmission, safely disposing of contaminated dressings removes a reservoir for pathogens, and administering a recommended vaccine strengthens the susceptible host's immune defenses. Leaving a used urinary catheter bag uncapped on the floor creates a reservoir and a portal of exit for pathogens rather than breaking the chain. Allowing staff to work while symptomatic with a contagious illness maintains an active reservoir and source of transmission instead of interrupting it.
  44. The correct answer is A: airborne precautions require negative pressure rooms and fitted N95 respirators because organisms like measles spread through fine droplet nuclei that remain suspended in air for extended periods. Droplet precautions use a surgical mask and do not require negative pressure airflow. Contact precautions use a gown and gloves for organisms spread by direct touch. Standard precautions alone are insufficient once an airborne organism is suspected.
  45. The correct answer is A: hand hygiene is always performed first, followed by the gown to cover clothing, then the mask if indicated, and finally the gloves, which are put on last so they can cover the gown's cuffs and remain the outermost, most protective barrier. Option B puts the gloves on before the gown, which fails to seal the gown cuff underneath. Option C skips hand hygiene entirely before donning equipment. Option D puts the mask on before the gown, which is out of the standard sequence.
  46. The correct answers are A and B: an oxygen tubing draped across the walking path and a bed left in the highest position with the side rails down both create an immediate fall and entanglement hazard that must be corrected right away. A call light placed within the client's reach is an appropriate safety measure, not a hazard. A nightlight left on in the bathroom is also a helpful safety feature that reduces fall risk during nighttime use.
  47. The correct answers are Row 1: Appropriate, Row 2: Breach, Row 3: Appropriate, Row 4: Breach, Row 5: Appropriate: performing hand hygiene after removing gloves and disposing of a used needle in a sharps container without recapping are both correct technique, and wearing a mask correctly over the nose and mouth is also appropriate. Reusing a single pair of gloves between two different clients and recapping a used needle with both hands are both clear breaches of infection control technique that increase the risk of transmission or injury.
  48. The correct answer is B: sitting independently without support is a gross motor milestone typically achieved by six to eight months of age, so a healthy nine month old should have this skill. Walking independently without holding on typically occurs closer to twelve to fifteen months. Speaking in two word phrases is a language milestone expected around eighteen to twenty four months. Riding a tricycle requires coordination that develops around age three.
  49. The correct answer is C: a client with a normal pre pregnancy body mass index is generally advised to gain twenty five to thirty five pounds over the course of the pregnancy to support fetal growth while minimizing maternal complications. A gain of less than fifteen pounds is more appropriate only for a client who begins pregnancy significantly overweight. A gain of fifteen to twenty pounds is too low for a client starting at a normal weight. A gain of more than forty pounds increases the risk of gestational complications and is not the recommended target.
  50. The correct answer is A: storing all household chemicals and medications in a locked cabinet out of a toddler's reach and sight is the most effective way to prevent accidental poisoning at this developmental stage. Keeping cleaning supplies under the kitchen sink without a lock leaves them accessible to a curious, mobile toddler. Storing medications in a purse on the floor still places them within a toddler's reach. Transferring medications to unlabeled containers removes important safety information and increases the risk of a dangerous ingestion.
  51. The correct answer is B: current guidelines recommend that routine colorectal cancer screening begin at age forty five for a client at average risk, allowing earlier detection of precancerous changes. Beginning at age thirty is earlier than recommended for someone without additional risk factors. Waiting until age sixty five delays screening well past the recommended starting point. Screening only after symptoms appear misses the entire purpose of preventive, asymptomatic screening.
  52. The correct answer is D: placing a healthy infant on their back on a firm, flat surface for every sleep is the single most effective evidence based practice to reduce the risk of sudden infant death syndrome. Placing the infant on their stomach to sleep is a known risk factor rather than a protective practice. Using soft bedding and pillows in the crib increases suffocation risk. Placing the infant in bed with the parents for every sleep, known as bed sharing, is also associated with increased risk and is not recommended.
  53. The correct answer is A: current guidelines recommend that average risk clients begin Pap testing at age twenty one and generally repeat the test every three years if results remain normal. Testing only once in a lifetime would miss the ongoing surveillance needed to detect precancerous cellular changes over time. Waiting until age forty to begin screening misses more than a decade of recommended surveillance. Testing every ten years is far less frequent than current evidence based guidance supports.
  54. The correct answer is C: according to Erikson, adolescence is the stage of identity versus role confusion, during which a teenager explores different values, peer groups, and beliefs to develop a stable sense of self. Trusting caregivers to meet basic needs describes the infant stage of trust versus mistrust. Developing a sense of accomplishment through completing tasks describes the school age stage of industry versus inferiority. Forming a close, committed intimate relationship describes the young adult stage of intimacy versus isolation.
  55. The correct answer is D: a mild fever is a common and expected reaction after many vaccines and is not by itself a reason to withhold future scheduled vaccines, so this statement reflects a misunderstanding requiring correction. Following the recommended immunization schedule is correct guidance. Reporting a severe allergic reaction after a previous vaccine to the provider is appropriate and important. Bringing the immunization record to every well child visit is also correct practice that helps keep vaccinations on schedule.
  56. The correct answers are A, C, and D: placing the infant on the back for every sleep, keeping the crib free of soft toys and loose bedding, and offering a pacifier at naptime and bedtime are all evidence based practices that reduce the risk of sudden infant death syndrome. Placing the infant on the stomach for daytime naps only is still a risk factor regardless of the time of day. Using a crib bumper pad is a known suffocation and entrapment hazard and should be avoided.
  57. The correct answers are A, B, and D: cigarette smoking, a sedentary lifestyle, and an elevated low density lipoprotein cholesterol level are all modifiable risk factors the client can address through lifestyle change or treatment. A family history of early cardiovascular disease is a nonmodifiable genetic risk factor. The client's biological sex is also a nonmodifiable factor that cannot be changed through intervention.
  58. The correct answers are A, B, and C: removing loose throw rugs, improving lighting in hallways and stairwells, and having vision checked regularly all directly reduce environmental and sensory fall risk factors in the home. Wearing loose fitting slippers without backs actually increases the risk of tripping and should be discouraged. Discontinuing all prescribed medications without provider guidance is unsafe and could worsen the client's underlying conditions rather than prevent falls.
  59. The correct answer is C: industry versus inferiority is Erikson's stage for the school age child, during which the child develops a sense of competence and accomplishment through completing tasks and comparing achievements with peers. Trust versus mistrust describes the infant stage. Identity versus role confusion describes adolescence. Generativity versus stagnation describes middle adulthood, not the school age years.
  60. The correct answers are Blank 1: B and Blank 2: A: rolling from front to back is typically achieved by around six months, and walking independently while holding on to furniture, sometimes called cruising, followed shortly by independent steps, is typically emerging around twelve months. Riding a tricycle requires coordination that develops closer to age three. Speaking in full sentences is a language milestone expected well beyond the first year of life.
  61. The correct answer is A: denial is the grief stage in which a person refuses to accept the reality of a loss, and questioning the accuracy of a diagnosis is a classic example. Anger typically involves frustration directed outward, often with statements blaming others. Bargaining involves attempting to negotiate or delay the loss, often through promises. Acceptance involves coming to terms with the reality of the situation, which this client has not yet reached.
  62. The correct answer is B: remaining with the client and using a calm, reassuring approach directly addresses the client's acute anxiety and helps establish a sense of safety before further intervention. Leaving the client alone to allow the anxiety to pass on its own could worsen feelings of isolation and fear. Telling the client to simply calm down dismisses the client's experience and rarely reduces anxiety. Immediately administering an as needed anxiolytic without first attempting a calming presence skips a lower risk, equally effective initial intervention.
  63. The correct answer is C: rationalization involves creating a logical sounding but incorrect explanation, such as believing an illness is a punishment, to make an unacceptable situation feel more understandable. Denial would involve refusing to accept the surgery happened at all, which is not the case here. Projection involves attributing one's own unacceptable feelings to someone else. Regression involves reverting to an earlier, less mature stage of behavior, which is not described in this scenario.
  64. The correct answer is D: visual hallucinations combined with a tremor and elevated blood pressure suggest the client may be progressing toward delirium tremens, a life threatening complication of alcohol withdrawal that requires urgent intervention. Mild anxiety and irritability are common, expected findings in early withdrawal. A slightly elevated heart rate alone without other severe findings is also an expected, monitored finding. Difficulty sleeping is uncomfortable but not an emergent, life threatening symptom on its own.
  65. The correct answer is A: directly and calmly asking the client about the specifics of the plan and access to means is the priority action, since it allows the nurse to accurately assess the level of risk and initiate appropriate safety precautions immediately. Changing the subject to something more positive ignores a disclosed safety risk and could increase the client's sense of isolation. Waiting until the end of the shift to report the statement dangerously delays urgent intervention. Promising to keep the statement confidential is inappropriate, since safety concerns of this severity must be reported to the healthcare team.
  66. The correct answer is B: acknowledging that the voices seem frightening to the client while calmly stating that the nurse does not hear the voices validates the client's feelings without reinforcing the false belief as true. Agreeing that the food may be poisoned reinforces the client's delusion, which is not therapeutic. Telling the client the belief is ridiculous is dismissive and can damage the therapeutic relationship. Ignoring the statement entirely misses an opportunity to build trust and assess the client's safety.
  67. The correct answer is C: an increase in appetite and engagement with meals often signals improving mood and energy in a client with depression, since decreased appetite is a common depressive symptom. Continued social withdrawal from unit activities suggests the depression has not yet improved. Persistent feelings of worthlessness are a core depressive symptom that would indicate ongoing illness. A flat affect with minimal eye contact also reflects an unchanged or worsening depressive state rather than improvement.
  68. The correct answer is A: speaking in a calm, low, steady voice while maintaining a safe physical distance is the first line, least restrictive intervention for managing escalating behavior and helps prevent further escalation. Immediately calling for physical restraints skips the required less restrictive interventions and is an inappropriate first response. Matching the client's raised tone can escalate the situation further rather than calm it. Leaving the area without addressing the client's distress at all misses an opportunity for early, effective de escalation.
  69. The correct answer is D: a heart rate of thirty eight beats per minute reflects severe bradycardia, a dangerous cardiac complication of prolonged starvation that can progress to a life threatening dysrhythmia and requires immediate attention. Preoccupation with calorie counting is an expected psychological symptom of the disorder that needs ongoing therapeutic intervention rather than emergency action. Mild constipation is uncomfortable but not immediately life threatening. Distorted body image is a core psychological feature of the disorder that requires long term treatment, not an emergent response.
  70. The correct answers are A, B, and D: a previous suicide attempt, giving away prized possessions, and a recent significant loss such as a divorce are all recognized warning signs that increase suicide risk. A strong, supportive social network is a protective factor that lowers risk. Active participation in ongoing outpatient therapy is also a protective factor rather than a risk factor.
  71. The correct answers are A, C, and D: reflecting the client's feelings, using silence to allow the client time to process emotions, and offering to sit with the client both use therapeutic techniques that encourage the client to express feelings openly. Telling the client everything happens for a reason imposes the nurse's own belief system rather than exploring the client's feelings. Changing the subject to avoid an uncomfortable conversation blocks therapeutic communication instead of supporting it.
  72. The correct answers are A, B, and C: tremors, elevated blood pressure, and visual or tactile hallucinations are all recognized signs of worsening alcohol withdrawal that can progress to a life threatening state if untreated. A heart rate that is trending toward the client's normal baseline is a reassuring sign, not a warning sign. Calm, cooperative behavior is also a reassuring finding rather than an indicator of worsening withdrawal.
  73. The correct answers are A, B, and D: staying with the client, speaking in short simple sentences, and guiding the client through slow, deep breathing are all appropriate interventions that help reduce the intensity of a panic attack. Asking the client detailed, complex questions about the trigger during the peak of the attack can overwhelm a client who is already struggling to process information. Leaving the client alone to manage the panic attack independently removes needed support during an acute episode.
  74. The correct answer is B: projection is the defense mechanism in which a person attributes their own unacceptable thoughts or feelings to someone else. Denial involves refusing to acknowledge a reality altogether. Rationalization involves creating a logical sounding excuse for a behavior or feeling. Regression involves reverting to an earlier, less mature stage of behavior, none of which describe attributing one's own feeling to another person.
  75. The correct answers are A and B: a client stating they have a specific plan and access to a firearm, and a client giving away personal belongings both represent serious, specific warning signs of suicide risk that must be reported immediately. A client stating they feel tired after a long week is a common, nonspecific complaint. A client stating they are looking forward to a family visit next month reflects future oriented thinking, which is generally a reassuring sign rather than a risk indicator.
  76. The correct answers are Blank 1: A and Blank 2: B: guilt filled statements about what could have been done differently reflect the bargaining stage, in which a person mentally revisits the events leading to the loss, while calmly planning for the future reflects acceptance, in which the person has come to terms with the loss. Denial involves refusing to believe the loss occurred, and anger involves outward frustration, neither of which matches these two statements.
  77. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: a statement expressing a wish for the pain to stop combined with a history of a prior attempt are the two most concerning cues, since a prior attempt is one of the strongest known predictors of a future attempt. The priority action is to implement one to one continuous observation and notify the registered nurse and provider immediately so the client's immediate safety can be secured. The two outcomes that show the intervention worked are the client remaining free from self harm and the client verbalizing a willingness to use a safety plan, since both directly reflect whether the immediate risk has been reduced.
  78. The correct answer is C: crossing the operative leg over the midline of the body places the hip in adduction and internal rotation, a combination of movements that significantly increases the risk of prosthesis dislocation after hip replacement surgery. Keeping the leg in a neutral, straight alignment is a safe, recommended position. Using an abduction pillow between the legs actively prevents adduction and protects the joint. Elevating the head of the bed to a moderate low Fowler position is generally allowed as long as hip flexion stays within safe limits.
  79. The correct answer is A: repositioning the client at least every two hours relieves sustained pressure over bony prominences and is the most effective, evidence based intervention to prevent pressure injury formation. Massaging reddened areas over a bony prominence can actually cause further tissue damage and is no longer recommended. Keeping the head of the bed elevated above forty five degrees for long periods increases shearing force on the sacrum. Applying powder to intact skin does not address the underlying cause of prolonged pressure.
  80. The correct answer is B: a comprehensive pain assessment, including location, quality, and aggravating factors, must be completed before administering any medication so the nurse and provider have accurate information to guide safe, effective treatment. Administering the maximum prescribed dose without first assessing the pain in detail could mask an evolving surgical emergency. Telling the client the pain will improve with rest dismisses a significant reported symptom without appropriate evaluation. Waiting until the next scheduled rounds unnecessarily delays care for a client reporting severe pain.
  81. The correct answer is D: reaching across an open sterile field with an unsterile arm contaminates the field because anything passing over a sterile area is considered contaminated, even without direct contact. Keeping sterile gloved hands above waist level maintains sterility. Opening the sterile kit away from the body so the inner surface faces up is correct technique. Placing the sterile drape on a clean, dry surface before opening the kit is also an appropriate preparatory step.
  82. The correct answer is A: applying the ice pack for no more than twenty minutes at a time with a barrier such as a towel between the ice and skin prevents cold related tissue and nerve injury while still providing an effective anti inflammatory benefit. Leaving the ice pack in place continuously for over an hour significantly increases the risk of frostbite and nerve damage. Placing the ice directly against bare skin without any barrier also increases the risk of a cold injury. Applying heat instead of ice during the first twenty four hours after an acute sprain can increase swelling rather than reduce it.
  83. The correct answer is B: positioning the unconscious client in a side lying position allows secretions and fluid used during oral care to drain out of the mouth rather than pool in the back of the throat, which significantly reduces aspiration risk. Positioning the client flat on the back increases the likelihood that fluid will pool near the airway. Using a large volume of liquid mouthwash increases the amount of fluid available to be aspirated. Performing oral care quickly without attention to positioning ignores the single most important safety measure for this client.
  84. The correct answer is C: a client who is actively vomiting shortly before surgery is at significant risk for aspiration under anesthesia and this finding must be reported before transport so the surgical team can be informed and the plan adjusted if needed. A client reporting mild hunger is an expected, low risk finding related to being NPO. A client asking when the surgery will begin reflects normal anticipatory anxiety, not a safety concern. A client who slept only a few hours the night before is a common finding that does not require notification prior to transport.
  85. The correct answer is D: white rice is low in fiber and residue, making it an appropriate choice for a client following a low residue diet during bowel healing. Raw vegetables are high in fiber and residue and are not appropriate for this diet. Whole grain bread is also high in fiber and would increase bowel stimulation. Popcorn contains hulls and significant roughage that should be avoided on a low residue diet.
  86. The correct answer is A: establishing a consistent sleep and wake schedule, part of good sleep hygiene, is the first line, evidence based nonpharmacologic intervention recommended for chronic insomnia before other measures are tried. Encouraging a large meal close to bedtime can actually interfere with sleep onset. Suggesting vigorous exercise immediately before bed can increase alertness and delay sleep rather than promote it. Recommending daytime napping for several hours can disrupt the nighttime sleep drive and worsen insomnia.
  87. The correct answers are A, B, and D: applying heat to the affected area, encouraging proper body mechanics, and teaching relaxation and guided imagery techniques are all appropriate nonpharmacologic comfort measures for chronic low back pain. Encouraging complete bed rest for several weeks is no longer recommended, since prolonged inactivity can worsen deconditioning and pain. Recommending the client sleep on a soft, sagging mattress fails to provide the spinal support needed to reduce pain.
  88. The correct answers are A, B, and D: a steady gait during a supervised attempt, vital signs within the client's normal baseline, and the client reporting no dizziness when standing all support that it is safe to proceed with independent ambulation. A blood pressure drop of thirty points when moving from lying to standing indicates orthostatic hypotension, which increases fall risk and should delay independent ambulation. A client reporting significant leg weakness also indicates the client is not yet ready to ambulate without assistance.
  89. The correct answer is A: pain that radiates along the path of the sciatic nerve, from the lower back through the posterior thigh and calf, is classic sciatic, or radicular, pain. Visceral pain arises from internal organs and is typically diffuse and poorly localized. Somatic superficial pain arises from the skin and is sharp and well localized, not radiating down a limb. Phantom limb pain occurs only after an amputation.
  90. The correct answer is A: the nurse should first raise the bed to a safe working height, then lower the head of the bed as tolerated, then position a second staff member or a friction reducing device, and finally reposition the client using a coordinated lift on a count of three. Option B repositions the client before the bed height and equipment are set up, increasing injury risk to staff. Option C lowers the head of the bed before raising the bed to a working height, an inefficient and less safe order. Option D positions the equipment before the bed is raised to a safe working height.
  91. The correct answers are Row 1: Appropriate, Row 2: Not Appropriate, Row 3: Appropriate, Row 4: Not Appropriate, Row 5: Appropriate: white toast, plain chicken, and clear broth are all low in fiber and residue and are appropriate. Raw celery and bran cereal are both high in fiber and roughage and are not appropriate on a low residue diet during bowel healing.
  92. The correct answer is C: the international normalized ratio (INR) directly measures the anticoagulant effect of warfarin and is the standard laboratory value used to guide dose adjustments and monitor therapeutic effectiveness. Serum potassium is unrelated to warfarin's mechanism of action. Blood glucose reflects glycemic control and has no relationship to anticoagulation. Serum creatinine reflects kidney function and does not measure warfarin's therapeutic effect.
  93. The correct answer is C: beta blockers reduce heart rate, so the apical pulse must be checked before administration and the dose held per protocol if the heart rate is too low to prevent symptomatic bradycardia. Blood glucose is important for medications like insulin but is not the priority pre administration check for a beta blocker. Body temperature is unrelated to this medication's mechanism of action. Bowel sounds are not affected by beta blockade and are not a relevant pre administration assessment for this drug class.
  94. The correct answer is B: red man syndrome is a well known, rate related infusion reaction to vancomycin, causing flushing and a rash on the upper body and neck, and is managed by slowing the infusion rate rather than stopping the drug entirely. A true anaphylactic reaction typically involves airway compromise and hypotension, which are not described here. A common cold is unrelated to an intravenous medication infusion. Normal saline sensitivity is not a recognized clinical reaction and does not explain these findings.
  95. The correct answer is D: withholding the insulin dose and treating the current hypoglycemia is the priority, since administering insulin to a client whose glucose is already low would dangerously worsen the hypoglycemia. Administering the full prescribed dose as ordered ignores the client's current dangerously low glucose level. Administering only half the prescribed dose still lowers an already low blood glucose further. Delaying breakfast without treating the hypoglycemia first leaves the client at continued risk while glucose remains low.
  96. The correct answer is C: an apical pulse below sixty beats per minute in an adult is a classic parameter for withholding digoxin, since the medication further slows heart rate and could precipitate dangerous bradycardia. A blood pressure of one hundred eighteen over seventy six millimeters of mercury is within normal limits. A respiratory rate of sixteen breaths per minute is also normal and unrelated to this medication's hold parameters. An oxygen saturation of ninety seven percent is a normal, reassuring finding.
  97. The correct answer is A: loop diuretics such as furosemide commonly cause potassium loss through increased urinary excretion, making hypokalemia the most important electrolyte imbalance to monitor. Hyperkalemia is more commonly associated with potassium sparing diuretics or renal failure, not loop diuretics. Hypercalcemia is not a typical adverse effect of this drug class. Hypernatremia is also not a typical finding, since loop diuretics generally promote sodium loss along with water.
  98. The correct answer is B: respiratory depression is the most serious, potentially life threatening adverse effect of opioid analgesics like morphine, making the respiratory rate the priority parameter to assess before and after each dose. Level of thirst is not a relevant safety parameter for this medication class.
  99. The correct answer is D: holding the next dose and notifying the provider promptly allows a serum lithium level to be obtained and the dose adjusted before further toxicity develops, which is essential since lithium has a narrow therapeutic range. Administering the next scheduled dose as ordered could worsen a potentially serious toxicity. Encouraging the client to restrict fluids is incorrect, since adequate fluid intake actually helps prevent lithium toxicity. Telling the client the symptoms are expected and require no action ignores signs of a potentially dangerous adverse reaction.
  100. The correct answer is C: a persistent dry cough is a well known adverse effect of ACE inhibitors, and stopping the medication without first notifying the provider could leave the client's blood pressure uncontrolled, so this statement shows a need for further teaching. Reporting facial or lip swelling immediately is correct, since this could indicate a rare but dangerous angioedema reaction. Rising slowly from a sitting to standing position is appropriate teaching to reduce orthostatic hypotension. Avoiding potassium supplements without provider approval is also correct, since ACE inhibitors can raise potassium levels.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A: activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) is the laboratory value used to monitor and titrate a continuous heparin infusion to a therapeutic range. INR is used to monitor warfarin, an oral anticoagulant with a different mechanism of action. Platelet count is monitored to detect a rare adverse reaction called heparin induced thrombocytopenia but does not measure the therapeutic anticoagulant effect. Hemoglobin A1c reflects long term blood glucose control and is unrelated to anticoagulation.
  2. The correct answer is B: aged cheese contains tyramine, and combining it with an MAOI can trigger a hypertensive crisis, making it essential to avoid this food while taking this medication class.   4.
