Case Study CJ-101

A 78-year-old client with a history of heart failure is admitted with a three day history of worsening shortness of breath and swelling in both legs. Vital signs on admission are temperature 36.8 degrees Celsius, heart rate 112/min and irregular, respiratory rate 26/min, blood pressure 148/92 mmHg, and oxygen saturation 89% on room air. The client reports sleeping on three pillows to breathe comfortably and has gained six pounds over the past week.

  1. The correct answer is A: an oxygen saturation of 89% on room air reflects inadequate oxygenation and is the priority finding, since impaired gas exchange can rapidly become life threatening if not addressed. The irregular heart rate is significant and needs monitoring but is a lower immediate priority than correcting hypoxemia. The six pound weight gain reflects fluid retention that developed over a week, not an acute emergency. Sleeping on three pillows is an important symptom of orthopnea but is not itself the most urgent physiological finding.
  2. The correct answer is B: the irregular rhythm with no clearly discernible P waves is consistent with atrial fibrillation, a common dysrhythmia in clients with underlying heart failure that can worsen cardiac output and contribute to fluid overload. A normal sinus rhythm would show a regular rate with a clear P wave before each QRS complex, which does not match this strip. Complete heart block presents with P waves that are entirely dissociated from the QRS complexes, a different pattern than shown here. Ventricular tachycardia presents as a fast, wide complex rhythm, which is not what this strip demonstrates.
  3. The correct answers are A, B, and D: impaired gas exchange related to fluid in the lungs, excess fluid volume related to worsening heart failure, and decreased cardiac output related to the new atrial fibrillation are all directly supported by the client's admission findings and require immediate attention. There is no evidence in the scenario of a skin integrity problem. There is also no evidence presented of an infectious process, since the client's temperature is within normal limits.
  4. The correct answers are Blank 1: A and Blank 2: B: a goal of achieving an oxygen saturation of at least 92% is realistic and directly addresses the client's hypoxemia, and placing the client in a high Fowler position helps maximize lung expansion and improve oxygenation while other treatments take effect. A saturation goal of only 80% would still represent an unsafe, hypoxic state. Placing the client flat on the back would worsen breathing in a client with fluid in the lungs rather than help it.
  5. The correct answer is C: applying supplemental oxygen addresses the client's immediate hypoxemia and should be initiated first, since correcting oxygenation takes priority over administering a medication that will take longer to produce its diuretic effect. Administering the furosemide is important but treats fluid overload, a slightly less immediate threat than the client's current low oxygen saturation. Weighing the client is useful baseline data but is not the most urgent action given the current oxygen saturation. Encouraging the client to ambulate in the hallway is inappropriate for a client in acute respiratory distress.
  6. The correct answers are Row 1: Improving, Row 2: Improving, Row 3: Not Improving, Row 4: Improving, Row 5: Not Improving: an oxygen saturation that has risen to 94%, a respiratory rate that has decreased toward normal, and increased urine output following furosemide all show the client is responding well to treatment. A heart rate that remains irregular at 110/min shows the atrial fibrillation has not yet resolved, and lung sounds that remain coarse throughout both lung fields show fluid is still present, so both indicate the client is not yet improving in those specific areas.
  7. The correct answer is B: a blood pressure this severely elevated combined with headache and visual changes is consistent with a hypertensive emergency, which can rapidly cause life threatening organ damage and requires immediate intervention. A migraine headache alone would not typically present with a blood pressure this critically elevated. Tension headache is a much lower acuity concern and does not explain this degree of hypertension. Dehydration would more typically present with low, not critically high, blood pressure.
  8. The correct answer is B: a temperature trend that steadily decreases from a febrile peak toward the normal range over the monitored hours indicates the client is responding well to the antibiotic therapy. A rising trend would instead suggest a worsening infection, which does not match the pattern shown. A flat, unchanging trend at a febrile level would suggest the treatment is not yet effective, which also does not match the downward pattern shown. A single spike in the middle of an otherwise improving trend does not by itself indicate treatment failure.
  9. The correct answer is D: a wide complex, very fast rhythm consistent with ventricular tachycardia is a life threatening finding, so the nurse's first action must be to activate the facility's emergency response and notify the provider immediately. Administering an oral pain medication does not address a life threatening dysrhythmia. Encouraging the client to rest quietly delays urgent recognition of a medical emergency. Documenting the rhythm and continuing routine monitoring is unsafe given the severity of this finding.
  10. The correct answer is A: bradycardia describes a heart rate below sixty beats per minute in an adult. Tachycardia describes a heart rate above one hundred beats per minute, the opposite finding. Atrial fibrillation describes an irregular rhythm rather than a specific rate. Asystole describes the complete absence of any cardiac electrical activity, not a slow but present rhythm.
  11. The correct answer is A: the nurse should first assess the client's airway, breathing, and circulation, then obtain vital signs and apply supplemental oxygen, then notify the registered nurse and provider, and finally prepare for a twelve lead electrocardiogram as ordered. Option B notifies the provider before the client has even been assessed. Option C obtains the electrocardiogram before oxygen has been applied to an already hypoxic client. Option D applies oxygen before completing the initial airway, breathing, and circulation assessment.
  12. The correct answers are A and B: an oxygen saturation of 84% on the client's usual oxygen setting and new onset confusion together suggest worsening hypoxemia severe enough to affect brain function, requiring immediate reporting. Scattered expiratory wheezes are a common, expected finding for this chronic condition. A productive cough with white sputum is also a typical, non urgent finding for a client with this diagnosis.
  13. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: a potassium of 6.8 mEq/L with peaked T waves on the rhythm strip together indicate severe hyperkalemia with early cardiac changes, a life threatening combination that can progress to a fatal dysrhythmia. The priority action is to notify the registered nurse and provider immediately so treatment to stabilize the cardiac membrane and lower the potassium level can begin urgently. The two parameters that show the intervention worked are the potassium level trending down toward normal and the T waves normalizing on the rhythm strip, since both directly reflect resolution of the hyperkalemia and its cardiac effects.
  14. The correct answers are Row 1: Expected, Row 2: Unexpected, Row 3: Expected, Row 4: Unexpected, Row 5: Expected: moderate serosanguineous drainage on the residual limb dressing and phantom limb sensation are both expected findings in the early postoperative period, and a pain level controlled by the prescribed analgesic is also expected. A saturated dressing with bright red blood suggests active bleeding and is unexpected, and a residual limb that is cool and dusky suggests compromised circulation and is also unexpected.
Case Study CJ-B-COOR-1

An LPN is the team leader for four unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a medical-surgical unit at the start of the day shift. The assignment includes eight clients: one client six hours post-total hip replacement, one client with a new tracheostomy requiring frequent suctioning, one client who needs a fingerstick blood glucose before breakfast, and one client with new left-sided weakness from a stroke who requires assistance with first-time ambulation. One UAP has only two weeks of experience on the unit.

  1. The correct answer is A: a fingerstick blood glucose is a standard technical task that does not require nursing assessment or judgment and can safely be performed by any trained UAP without additional instruction. Suctioning a new tracheostomy is an invasive skilled task requiring licensed oversight. Assisting a stroke client with first-time ambulation requires initial nursing judgment about readiness and fall risk. Repositioning using an abduction pillow requires understanding of hip precautions and should be taught specifically before delegation.
  2. The correct answer is C: a client attempting first-time ambulation after a new stroke has an unpredictable outcome and fall risk that requires the LPN's own direct assessment before the task is assigned to a newly oriented UAP. A routine fingerstick blood glucose is a predictable, low-risk task. A tracheostomy client already stable on the ventilator has a predictable status for monitoring purposes. A postoperative hip client resting quietly in bed is currently stable and does not require immediate reassessment.
  3. The correct answer is C: dizziness on standing after a new stroke indicates orthostatic hypotension, which places the client at high risk for a fall, making this the priority nursing problem at this time. Impaired verbal communication is not supported by the finding described. Deficient knowledge of stroke prevention is a longer-term teaching concern, not the immediate priority. Risk for impaired skin integrity is not related to the dizziness reported during ambulation.
  4. The correct answer is B: having the client sit down and reassessing vital signs before continuing addresses the immediate safety concern of possible orthostatic hypotension before further activity is attempted. Continuing to ambulate independently ignores a warning sign of instability. Stopping ambulation for the shift without notifying the RN fails to communicate a clinically significant finding. Increasing the ambulation distance would further stress an already unstable cardiovascular response.
  5. The correct answer is B: a confirmed 22 mmHg systolic drop on standing is a significant finding of orthostatic hypotension that must be reported to the registered nurse before any further ambulation is attempted. Waiting until the end of the shift to document delays needed intervention. Attempting ambulation again in an hour without reporting the finding ignores a safety concern. Leaving a repeat blood pressure check contingent on the client's request is not an appropriate safety practice.
  6. The correct answer is D: successfully ambulating 10 feet with a stable blood pressure and no dizziness demonstrates that the interventions addressing orthostatic hypotension were effective. A client refusing to attempt ambulation again does not demonstrate resolution of the problem. An unchanged blood pressure reading only from supine does not show the client's response to standing. Completing ambulation without documenting vital signs provides no evidence the intervention worked and reflects incomplete monitoring.
Case Study CJ-B-COOR-2

At the start of the shift, an LPN receives hand-off report on four clients from the outgoing nurse: a client with a temperature of 38.9 degrees Celsius and productive cough two days post-abdominal surgery, a client requesting pain medication that is due in 20 minutes, a client scheduled for discharge teaching this morning, and a client who reports new onset chest pain and shortness of breath five minutes ago.

  1. The correct answer is B: new onset chest pain and shortness of breath reported just five minutes ago represents a potential life-threatening emergency and requires the LPN's immediate attention ahead of the other reported needs. A temperature of 38.9 degrees Celsius, while notable, is not immediately life-threatening. Pain medication due in 20 minutes and scheduled discharge teaching are both non-urgent, time-flexible tasks.
  2. The correct answer is C: an oxygen saturation of 89% combined with diaphoresis suggests significant hypoxemia and physiologic stress, both concerning signs of a possible life-threatening cardiopulmonary complication. Incisional pain rated 2 of 10 is a mild, expected postoperative finding. Mild anxiety about discharge is a psychosocial concern, not a physiologic emergency. A decreased appetite after surgery is a common, low-acuity finding unrelated to the chest pain presentation.
  3. The correct answer is D: new chest pain, shortness of breath, and hypoxemia in a postoperative client are classic warning signs of a pulmonary embolism, a potentially fatal complication of immobility after surgery. Constipation would not cause these cardiopulmonary findings. A urinary tract infection typically presents with urinary symptoms, not chest pain and hypoxemia. A superficial wound infection would present with localized redness or drainage, not respiratory distress.
  4. The correct answer is A: applying supplemental oxygen and remaining with the client while notifying the registered nurse addresses the immediate hypoxemia and ensures the client is monitored while urgent evaluation is arranged. Ambulating the client would worsen oxygen demand and risk collapse. Beginning discharge teaching ignores the acute emergency entirely. Lowering the head of the bed flat would worsen, not relieve, dyspnea in this situation.
  5. The correct answer is C: delegating routine vital sign checks on the stable febrile client to a UAP allows the LPN to remain with the client in acute distress while other care needs are still safely addressed. Leaving the unit for paperwork abandons both the client in distress and the other assigned clients. Administering scheduled pain medication before assessing the emergent client misprioritizes care. Postponing all other client care entirely is unnecessary since lower-acuity tasks can be delegated.
  6. The correct answer is D: an oxygen saturation of 96% with even, unlabored respirations indicates the client's respiratory status has improved and stabilized after intervention. A client who is asleep and cannot be aroused is a concerning finding, not evidence of stabilization. A respiratory rate that increased to 32 per minute shows worsening, not improving, respiratory status. Continued severe chest pain indicates the underlying problem has not resolved.
Case Study CJ-B-COOR-3

An LPN is assisting with preoperative preparation for a client scheduled for a bowel resection in two hours. While reviewing the chart, the LPN notices the surgical consent form has not been signed. The client tells the LPN, 'I still have some questions about what the surgeon is actually going to do and what my other options might be.'

  1. The correct answer is A: the client's statement that questions remain about the procedure and its alternatives is significant because it signals that informed consent has not yet been properly established, which is a barrier to proceeding safely with surgery. Asking about the scheduled time, requesting a blanket, and asking about family waiting areas are routine, non-clinical questions that do not affect the validity of consent.
  2. The correct answer is B: because the client still has unanswered questions about the procedure and its alternatives, the client's understanding is incomplete, which means consent obtained at this point would not meet the legal standard of being fully informed. The client has not yet given valid informed consent, so option A is incorrect. Nothing in the statement indicates the client is refusing surgery or requesting a different surgeon.
  3. The correct answer is C: the surgeon performing the procedure is legally and professionally responsible for explaining the procedure, risks, benefits, and alternatives as part of obtaining informed consent. Unlicensed assistive personnel have no role in providing clinical information for consent. The LPN can witness a signature but cannot independently provide the clinical explanation required for informed consent. The admitting clerk handles administrative paperwork, not clinical explanations.
  4. The correct answer is D: notifying the registered nurse and surgeon that the client has unanswered questions ensures the correct team member returns to complete the informed consent discussion before the client signs. Having the client sign first and explaining afterward reverses the required order of informed consent. Asking a family member to answer clinical questions is not appropriate, since the surgeon must provide that information. Proceeding with preparation while disregarding the client's statement ignores a legitimate consent concern.
  5. The correct answer is A: once the surgeon has answered the client's questions and the client verbalizes understanding, the LPN's appropriate role is to witness the client's own signature on the consent form. The LPN cannot sign the consent form on the client's behalf, since only the client or their legal representative may consent. Telling the client signing is no longer necessary is inaccurate, since a signed consent is still required. Witnessing a legal signature is a task appropriate for licensed staff, not delegated to unlicensed personnel.
  6. The correct answer is C: a client statement expressing understanding of the procedure, risks, and alternatives demonstrates that informed consent was obtained appropriately. A statement that the client signed only because told to do so suggests coerced rather than informed consent. A statement of continued lack of understanding shows consent is still not valid. A statement that the client felt too rushed to ask questions also indicates the consent process was inadequate.
Case Study CJ-B-COOR-4

An LPN reviews a new telephone order transcribed by another nurse for a client with chronic kidney disease: furosemide 80 mg IV push now. The LPN recalls that IV push medication administration is outside the LPN's scope of practice in this state, and also notes the client's most recent potassium level, resulted an hour ago, was 6.8 mEq/L, which was not addressed in the new order.

  1. The correct answer is C: a potassium level of 6.8 mEq/L that has not been addressed by the new order is dangerously elevated and poses an immediate risk of a life-threatening cardiac dysrhythmia, making it the most concerning finding. The route of administration being IV rather than oral is a scope-of-practice issue but not itself a clinical danger sign. The diagnosis of chronic kidney disease is background information already known and expected. Receiving the order by telephone is a routine communication method, not a safety concern by itself.
  2. The correct answer is D: a potassium of 6.8 mEq/L combined with a diuretic order that does not address the hyperkalemia means the client remains at significant risk for a dangerous cardiac dysrhythmia until the potassium level is properly treated. Furosemide does not reliably or rapidly correct severe hyperkalemia, so assuming low risk is incorrect. Nothing in the scenario indicates the client's kidney function has normalized. A client with this potassium level requires, not eliminates, the need for cardiac monitoring.
  3. The correct answer is A: verifying that the critically elevated potassium level has been reported to the registered nurse or provider is the priority concern, since untreated severe hyperkalemia poses an immediate risk to the client's life. Confirming transcription accuracy is a secondary concern once patient safety is addressed. Whether the client prefers oral medications is not relevant to this urgent laboratory finding. Reviewing the visitor list has no bearing on this clinical safety issue.
  4. The correct answer is C: the LPN should decline to administer the IV push dose since it exceeds LPN scope of practice, and should also notify the registered nurse of the unaddressed critical potassium level so it can be treated appropriately. Administering the medication anyway would violate scope of practice and ignore the unaddressed lab value. Asking unlicensed personnel to give the medication is not appropriate, since medication administration of this kind is a licensed nursing responsibility. Delaying any action risks a life-threatening cardiac event from the untreated hyperkalemia.
  5. The correct answer is A: after appropriately notifying the registered nurse of both concerns, the LPN should document the actions taken and continue with other assigned client care while the registered nurse follows up with the provider. Giving the IV push dose regardless would still exceed the LPN's scope of practice and ignore the unresolved potassium concern. Telling the client the medication will not be given without explanation could cause unnecessary alarm before the situation is clarified. Discarding the order without documentation fails to create an accurate record of the safety concern raised.
  6. The correct answer is B: the client's safety is appropriately addressed when the critical potassium level is rechecked and treated and the medication is given, if still indicated, by a nurse practicing within their legal scope. Having the LPN give the IV push dose as originally ordered would still be an unsafe, out-of-scope action. Ignoring the order entirely with no follow-up leaves the client's needs unmet. No further laboratory monitoring would be an inappropriate response to a critical, unresolved potassium level.
Case Study CJ-B-COOR-5

An LPN is assisting the case manager with discharge planning for an 82-year-old client being discharged home after treatment for pneumonia. The client lives alone, uses a cane, and will need home oxygen therapy and daily wound care for a stage 2 pressure injury on the coccyx. The client's daughter states she works full time and can only visit on weekends.

  1. The correct answer is C: the client's combination of living alone, needing daily wound care, and having limited family support is the most clinically significant factor for discharge planning and should be reported to the case manager. A preference for a television channel is unrelated to discharge safety. The admission date is administrative information already available in the chart. An insurance card number is administrative billing information, not a safety-related discharge factor.
  2. The correct answer is D: the combination of living alone, needing daily skilled wound care, and having limited family support strongly suggests this client is likely to need a home health referral for skilled nursing visits after discharge. Assuming no additional services are needed ignores real safety gaps. Discharging with no further planning would leave complex needs unaddressed. Using a cane does not eliminate fall risk, especially for an older adult recovering from an acute illness.
  3. The correct answer is A: given the client's daily wound care needs and limited support at home, risk for impaired skin integrity and inadequate wound care management is the priority problem for the case management team to address. Risk for excessive weight gain is not supported by anything in the scenario. Risk for social isolation from too many visitors is the opposite of the client's actual situation, which involves limited support. Risk for medication overuse from an oversupply of caregivers is not consistent with the described limited family availability.
  4. The correct answer is B: a home health nursing referral directly addresses the client's ongoing needs for skilled wound care and monitoring of home oxygen therapy after discharge. A personal chef service does not address the client's wound care or oxygen monitoring needs. A referral to a fitness center is not appropriate for a client recovering from pneumonia with an active pressure injury. Cosmetic dermatology is unrelated to the clinical wound care this client requires.
  5. The correct answer is D: providing the daughter with discharge teaching and the home health agency's contact information allows her to meaningfully support her mother's care during weekend visits, consistent with the family's actual availability. Excluding the daughter from information reduces her ability to help. Instructing her to quit her job is not an appropriate or realistic nursing intervention. Telling the daughter no further involvement is needed disregards a valuable support resource for the client.
  6. The correct answer is A: a statement confirming that the home health nurse is checking the wound and that the oxygen equipment is working well indicates the discharge plan and referrals are functioning effectively. A statement that no one has checked on the client suggests the referral did not result in actual follow-up care. Running out of oxygen with no one to call indicates a failure in equipment or education planning. A wound that has worsened with no monitoring indicates the wound care referral did not achieve its intended outcome.
  7. The correct answer is B: right circumstance requires the nurse to consider the client's clinical stability and predictability of outcome before delegating a task, and a hemodynamically stable client four hours after a routine procedure meets that standard. Right task concerns whether the activity itself, such as vital signs, is delegable in general. Right person concerns the specific UAP's training and competency, not the client's condition. Right direction and communication concerns how clearly the LPN explains the task, not the client's stability.
  8. The correct answer is C: the assessment component of SBAR is where the nurse reports current clinical data and trends, such as a declining blood pressure over time, that describe the client's present condition. The situation component briefly states who the client is and the immediate reason for the report. The background component provides relevant history, such as diagnosis or recent procedures. The recommendation component states what the reporting nurse believes should happen next.
  9. The correct answer is A: most state nurse practice acts reserve the initial comprehensive assessment and development of the individualized plan of care to the registered nurse, since this requires independent clinical judgment beyond the LPN scope of practice. The LPN contributes ongoing data collection and implements the established plan but does not independently create it. Unlicensed assistive personnel are not licensed to assess or plan care. The unit secretary manages clerical tasks, not clinical assessment.
  10. The correct answer is D: clients have a legal right under federal privacy regulations to request and obtain a copy of their own health information, typically through a written request to the health information management department. Telling the client records are inaccessible to them is incorrect and misinforms the client of a protected right. A family member does not need to request the record instead of the client. A court order is not required for a client to access their own information.
  11. The correct answer is B: new stridor and increased work of breathing signal a potential airway compromise, which takes priority over stable, non-urgent needs according to the airway, breathing, and circulation framework. A scheduled dressing change and a routine medication due later are not immediately life-threatening. A question about visiting hours is not a clinical priority at all.
  12. The correct answer is A: a living will documents the client's specific wishes about future treatment, while a durable power of attorney for health care names a designated person to make decisions on the client's behalf when the client cannot. These are two distinct advance directive documents, not the same document under different names. A living will addresses care while the client is alive but unable to communicate, not after death. A health care power of attorney only acts when the client lacks capacity, not while the client can still decide.
  13. The correct answer is D: restraints are a last resort, so the nurse must first attempt and document less restrictive alternatives such as increased observation, a bed alarm, or relocating the client, before a restraint order is sought. Applying restraints immediately skips the required least-restrictive-alternative step. Sedating the client is a chemical restraint decision that also requires an order and careful justification. Asking a family member to physically hold the client is unsafe and not an appropriate nursing intervention.
  14. The correct answer is C: nurses are mandated reporters, and findings such as bruising in multiple stages of healing combined with a fearful reaction to the caregiver require the nurse to report suspected elder abuse to adult protective services. Waiting to mention it only to the physician later delays a required, time-sensitive report. Remaining silent unless the client directly discloses abuse ignores the nurse's independent reporting duty. Confronting the caregiver directly could endanger the client and is not the nurse's role.
  15. The correct answer is A: valid informed consent requires the provider to explain the nature of the procedure, its risks and benefits, and any reasonable alternatives so the client can make a voluntary, informed decision. Knowing only the surgeon's name does not meet the legal standard for informed consent. The cost of the procedure and the expected length of stay are useful planning details but are not the required elements of informed consent.
  16. The correct answer is C: when a nurse believes an assignment is unsafe, the appropriate action is to raise the concern through the established chain of command, typically starting with the charge nurse or nursing supervisor, so the assignment can be reviewed and adjusted. Silently accepting an unsafe assignment does not protect client safety. Abandoning the shift without following proper procedure could constitute client abandonment. Asking a family member to provide nursing care is not an appropriate substitute for licensed staffing.
  17. The correct answer is B: an incident report is an internal document used for quality improvement and risk management and is kept separate from the client's medical record. The objective facts of the fall itself must still be documented directly in the client's chart. The incident report does not replace chart documentation of the event and client status. The report should contain objective facts only, not opinions or statements of fault.
  18. The correct answer is D: reading the order back to the provider and receiving confirmation before ending the call is the recommended safety practice for verbal and telephone orders to catch transcription errors before they reach the client. Implementing the order without verification increases the risk of a medication error. An unlicensed assistive personnel cannot verify a clinical order since that is outside their role. Waiting until the next visit could dangerously delay a needed medication.
  19. The correct answer is D: Good Samaritan laws are generally designed to protect a health care provider who voluntarily renders emergency care in good faith and without gross negligence from civil liability for the outcome. The nurse is not automatically liable simply for providing reasonable emergency assistance. Active malpractice insurance is not a legal requirement to render emergency first aid at an accident scene. State licensure requirements for routine practice do not typically bar a nurse from providing emergency bystander assistance.
  20. The correct answer is B: assisting a stable client with routine hygiene needs is a task that does not require nursing assessment or judgment and is within the scope of a UAP. Evaluating a client's response to medication requires nursing assessment and clinical judgment. Determining a priority nursing diagnosis is a licensed nursing responsibility. Teaching a client to use new equipment such as an insulin pen requires nursing knowledge and cannot be delegated to unlicensed personnel.
  21. The correct answer is C: confidentiality protections apply to any information that could reasonably identify a client, not just the client's name, so unique details about a case should not be shared publicly even in general terms. Omitting the name alone does not guarantee the client cannot be identified from other details. A private group of nurses is still an unauthorized disclosure outside the client's care team. A coworker cannot grant permission to disclose another client's protected health information.
  22. The correct answer is A: a newly admitted client with severe, unaddressed acute pain requires prompt assessment because the underlying cause has not yet been evaluated, making this the higher priority over a stable, comfortable client. Completing routine charting can wait until the more urgent need is addressed. Delaying assessment until the next shift or scheduled rounding time could allow a serious condition to go unrecognized.
  23. The correct answer is C: a social worker is trained to connect clients with financial assistance programs, medication assistance resources, and community services, making this the most appropriate referral for a client worried about affording medications. Physical therapy addresses mobility and functional needs, not financial concerns. A dietitian addresses nutrition needs. Pastoral care addresses spiritual and emotional support, not financial resources.
  24. The correct answer is D: a do-not-resuscitate order specifically addresses whether cardiopulmonary resuscitation will be attempted if the client's heart or breathing stops, and it does not limit other appropriate treatments such as antibiotics or pain management. Withholding all further treatment for the infection misapplies the scope of the order. Discontinuing all medications is not implied by a resuscitation-only order. Withholding pain medication would be inappropriate and unrelated to the order's actual scope.
  25. The correct answer is A: failing to follow the established reposition schedule, an accepted standard of care, represents a breach of duty, one of the required elements of a malpractice claim. A duty was clearly owed to the client as the assigned nurse, so this does not demonstrate an absence of duty. Causation would need to be separately shown by linking the missed repositioning directly to the pressure injury. The presence of a new pressure injury represents actual damage, not an absence of damages.
  26. The correct answer is B: a legally emancipated minor has the legal capacity to consent to their own medical treatment, similar to an adult, so parental consent is not required. Delaying treatment to locate a parent is unnecessary and could harm the client. Contacting child protective services is not indicated simply because the client is an emancipated minor seeking care. A court order is not required for routine treatment of an emancipated minor who can legally consent.
  27. The correct answer is B: a client in restraints requires frequent, regularly scheduled assessment of circulation, skin integrity, and whether the restraint is still needed, per facility policy and regulatory requirements. Checking only once every 24 hours is far too infrequent to ensure safety. Restraints must be reassessed regularly, not left in place indefinitely without evaluation. Decisions about continuing or discontinuing a restraint require licensed nursing judgment, not delegation to unlicensed personnel.
  28. The correct answer is D: utilization review is the process of evaluating whether the level and length of care meet established medical necessity criteria, often tied to insurance coverage and reimbursement. Medication reconciliation involves comparing medication lists across care transitions, not reviewing coverage criteria. Informed consent verification confirms a client understood and agreed to a procedure. Incident reporting documents unexpected events, not ongoing coverage decisions.
  29. The correct answer is C: in most states, once a registered nurse has initiated a blood transfusion, the LPN may monitor the client for signs of a transfusion reaction, which is within the LPN scope of practice. Obtaining informed consent for a blood product is typically a provider or registered nurse responsibility, not an independent LPN task. Initiating a blood transfusion independently generally exceeds LPN scope of practice in most states. Blood typing must be confirmed through laboratory testing, not determined informally.
  30. The correct answer is A: a competent adult client has the right to leave against medical advice, but the nurse should first notify the registered nurse and provider so the client can be informed of the risks before making a final decision. Physically blocking or detaining a client who has decision-making capacity could constitute false imprisonment. Telling the client leaving is never allowed misrepresents the client's legal right to refuse treatment and leave.
  31. The correct answer is A: a new, acute, severe symptom such as crushing chest pain represents a potentially life-threatening emergency and takes priority over a stable, well-controlled chronic condition. Order of call light activation does not override clinical urgency when a life-threatening symptom is present. Discharge timing is not a valid basis for prioritizing which client is assessed first when acute distress is present.
  32. The correct answer is B: a just culture balances individual accountability with an examination of system factors that may have contributed to the error, encouraging honest reporting rather than blame alone. Automatic termination discourages future honest reporting and does not reflect a just culture approach. Ignoring the event, even without harm, misses an opportunity to prevent a similar error. Discouraging error reporting undermines patient safety and quality improvement efforts.
  33. The correct answer is D: when a competent client specifically requests that health information be directed to a family member, honoring and documenting that request respects both the client's cultural values and their right to make decisions about their own information. Refusing to share information under any circumstances disregards the client's own expressed wish. Sharing information without confirming the client wants this ignores the need for the client's own authorization. Ignoring the client's request altogether disrespects client autonomy.
  34. The correct answer is C: a qualified medical interpreter should be used for informed consent discussions to ensure accurate communication of the procedure, risks, benefits, and alternatives. Using a family member as an interpreter risks inaccurate or incomplete translation of medical information and can compromise privacy. Having the client sign before understanding the information does not meet the standard for informed consent. An unsupervised translation application is not considered a reliable substitute for a qualified medical interpreter for consent.
  35. The correct answer is D: a client who requires 24-hour skilled nursing care and rehabilitation beyond what can be safely provided at home is appropriately discharged to a skilled nursing facility rather than sent home. Discharging home without additional services would leave the client without needed 24-hour support. Outpatient therapy alone does not provide the round-the-clock nursing care this client requires. A single weekly home health visit is far less than the 24-hour care level needed.
  36. The correct answer is C: bedside hand-off that includes the client and allows both nurses to verify current orders, pending tasks, and the client's status together supports accuracy and continuity of care. Conducting the report entirely away from the client removes an opportunity to verify information and engage the client. Skipping verification of identity and status increases the risk of a missed or transferred error. Relying only on the written record without a verbal exchange misses the chance to clarify questions in real time.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A: a client with decision-making capacity has the legal right to refuse treatment, and the nurse's role is to provide education, respect that decision, and document both the refusal and the education given. Administering medication covertly without the client's knowledge violates autonomy and informed consent. Telling the client refusal is not permitted misrepresents their legal right. Withholding all other care as a consequence of refusal is punitive and inappropriate.
  2. The correct answer is B: nurses have a professional and ethical obligation to report unsafe or fraudulent practices, such as falsified documentation, through the appropriate facility reporting channel to protect client safety. Remaining silent allows the unsafe practice to continue and puts clients at risk. Adopting the same unsafe behavior compounds the problem and violates professional standards. A public confrontation in front of clients is inappropriate and does not follow proper reporting procedure.
  3. The correct answer is C: right supervision and communication require the delegating nurse to give clear instructions and follow up to evaluate whether the task was completed appropriately and the client's status. Assuming completion without follow-up abandons the nurse's supervisory responsibility. Leaving the unit entirely removes the ability to supervise or be available for questions. Allowing the UAP to independently determine the frequency of skilled skin care removes necessary licensed nursing oversight.
  4. The correct answer is A: a hospice referral is appropriate for a client with a terminal prognosis of six months or less who wishes to shift the goal of care from curative treatment to comfort. Suggesting aggressive chemotherapy contradicts the client's stated preference for comfort-focused care. A repeat diagnostic workup does not align with a comfort-focused plan of care. Occupational therapy for return to work is inconsistent with a terminal prognosis and comfort-focused goals.
  5. The correct answer is B: when a client lacks decision-making capacity, has no advance directive, and no one has existing legal authority to decide on their behalf, a court proceeding to appoint a legal guardian is typically required. A nurse cannot automatically assume legal decision-making authority for a client. A facility administrator does not have independent legal authority to make medical decisions for an incapacitated client. Treatment cannot simply be withheld without a proper decision-making process in place.
  6. The correct answer is D: sudden severe shortness of breath is a potential airway and breathing emergency and must be addressed immediately, ahead of non-urgent requests. Refilling a water pitcher, asking about meal timing, and locating a remote control are all low-priority comfort requests that can safely wait. None of these other requests involve a change in clinical status that could be life-threatening.
  7. The correct answer is B: a POLST form translates a client's current wishes into specific, medically actionable physician orders and is designed to guide immediate treatment decisions, including in emergencies, more directly than a general advance directive. An older advance directive is not automatically prioritized simply because it was signed first, especially if wishes have since been clarified. Both documents can apply, but the POLST provides actionable orders in an emergency. Family preference does not override the client's own documented wishes reflected in the POLST.
  8. The correct answer is D: a sentinel event is an unexpected occurrence involving death or serious physical or psychological injury that triggers a formal investigation, often a root cause analysis, to identify system factors and prevent recurrence. A routine quality audit is a planned, ongoing review process, not a response to a single serious event. A staff performance evaluation assesses an individual employee, not the systemic causes of an event. A scheduled competency check is a planned skills assessment unrelated to investigating a specific adverse event.
  9. The correct answer is A: completing an accurate, updated medication reconciliation that accompanies the client during transfer is essential to prevent omissions, duplications, or errors in the client's ongoing medication therapy. Sending belongings without a medication list creates a significant safety gap. Deferring the information to a later pharmacy contact could delay time-sensitive medication administration. Assuming the receiving facility already has current information is unsafe and often inaccurate.
  10. The correct answer is C: drawing the privacy curtain and speaking quietly protects the client's dignity and confidentiality during the assessment without requiring an unnecessary delay in care. Proceeding without any change to the environment does not protect the client's privacy. Postponing the assessment indefinitely could delay needed care unnecessarily. Moving clients to a public hallway would further compromise privacy rather than protect it.
  11. The correct answer is D: the LPN's valuable contribution to an interdisciplinary care conference is sharing direct, ongoing observations of the client's daily status and response to interventions, information gathered through frequent contact with the client. Diagnosing is outside LPN scope of practice. Discharge placement decisions are made collaboratively by the team, not solely by the LPN. Prescribing medications is outside the LPN's legal scope of practice.
  12. The correct answer is A: according to Maslow's hierarchy, physiological needs such as adequate oxygenation and breathing take priority over higher-level psychosocial needs like anxiety, esteem, or self-actualization. Anxiety, while important to address, is a psychosocial need that ranks below basic physiological survival needs. Esteem and self-actualization needs are the highest levels of the hierarchy and are addressed only after more basic needs are met.
  13. The correct answer is C: an assignment refers to distributing work that falls within the LPN's own scope of practice, which the LPN then performs directly, distinct from delegation, which involves transferring a task to someone else, such as a UAP. Delegation specifically involves handing off a task to another person, which is not what is described here. Supervision refers to overseeing someone else's performance of a delegated task, not the LPN's own direct care. There is no such thing as a transfer of licensure authority between staff members.
  14. The correct answer is B: when floated to an unfamiliar unit, the nurse should report as assigned but communicate specific skill limitations to the charge nurse so the assignment can be adjusted to tasks within the nurse's competency, with orientation support as needed. Leaving the facility without proper notice could be considered client abandonment. Silently accepting an assignment beyond one's competency risks client safety. Performing unfamiliar tasks without disclosing limitations endangers clients and is professionally inappropriate.
  15. The correct answer is A: a durable power of attorney for health care legally designates a specific person to make health care decisions when the client can no longer do so, and that named individual has the authority to provide consent. Any available family member does not automatically have this authority if a specific person was legally designated. A provider generally still needs appropriate consent before proceeding with treatment, except in true emergencies. A case manager coordinates care but does not hold legal decision-making authority.
  16. The correct answer is C: a cognitively intact client has the right to refuse treatment, and the nurse's responsibility is to provide ongoing education, document the refusal and teaching provided, and respect the client's autonomous decision. Performing the procedure without consent violates the client's right to refuse. Reporting a competent adult's informed refusal as neglect is not appropriate, since the client retains decision-making capacity. Discharging the client solely for declining one treatment is not an appropriate or proportionate response.
  17. The correct answer is D: standard protocol requires that a trained representative from the designated organ procurement organization, not the bedside nurse, approach the family about organ donation, since this requires specialized training in the request process. The bedside LPN's role is to notify the appropriate parties, not to make the donation request directly. Neither the unit clerk nor unlicensed assistive personnel have a clinical or trained role in this sensitive conversation.
  18. The correct answer is B: appropriate documentation is objective, includes the client's own words when relevant, and records the actions taken, such as notifying the provider and providing education about the refusal. Describing the client as difficult or uncooperative is a subjective, judgmental statement that does not belong in the medical record. Speculating about why the client refused, such as assuming forgetfulness, is not objective documentation. Stating nothing unusual to report fails to document a clinically significant refusal at all.
  19. The correct answer is C: physical therapy specializes in evaluating and improving a client's mobility, strength, and safe transfer techniques, making it the most appropriate referral for new weakness affecting movement. Pastoral care addresses spiritual and emotional needs, not physical mobility. A dietitian addresses nutritional needs, not mobility limitations. A financial counselor addresses cost and insurance concerns, not physical rehabilitation needs.
  20. The correct answer is B: a client's advance directive, such as a living will, should be placed in the medical record and communicated to the health care team so it can guide care decisions if needed. Discarding the document disregards the client's legally documented wishes. Keeping it only among personal belongings means the care team may not know it exists when a decision is needed. Advance directives are generally valid across care settings, so telling the client otherwise is inaccurate.
  21. The correct answer is D: clients have the right to file a formal grievance, and the nurse should provide accurate information about the facility's established grievance process. Telling the client no process exists is inaccurate and denies the client their right to be heard. Suggesting the client only discuss the issue with family does not address the client's right to a formal complaint process. Dismissing complaints as not taken seriously is inappropriate and misrepresents the facility's obligations.
  22. The correct answer is A: sudden, severe abdominal pain with a rigid abdomen suggests a potential acute surgical emergency, such as a perforation or hemorrhage, and requires immediate assessment ahead of routine, stable care needs. Routine vital signs on a stable postpartum client can be safely delayed briefly. Order of admission and presence of family are not valid clinical criteria for prioritization when a possible emergency is present.
  23. The correct answer is D: an error that was caught before reaching the client and causing harm is considered a near miss, and reporting it, even without harm, helps identify system weaknesses and prevent future errors. A sentinel event involves actual serious harm or death, which did not occur here. Treating the event as routine and unreportable misses a valuable opportunity for prevention. A malpractice claim requires actual harm and a breach of duty, neither of which is described here.
  24. The correct answer is A: sudden one-sided numbness and weakness are classic signs of a possible stroke, a time-sensitive emergency that requires immediate assessment and notification of the registered nurse. Completing a scheduled but non-urgent medication first would dangerously delay recognition of a possible stroke. Asking the client to wait risks a poorer outcome from delayed treatment. Assessing new neurological symptoms requires nursing judgment and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
  25. The correct answer is B: arranging consistent access to a qualified medical interpreter supports accurate communication and safe, coordinated care throughout the client's stay, not just for a single conversation. Relying on gestures alone risks significant miscommunication about medical needs. Documenting a family-performed task as though the nurse completed it would be inaccurate and falsified documentation.

  81. The correct answer is D: veracity is the ethical principle that requires nurses to be honest and truthful with clients about their care. Justice refers to treating clients fairly and equitably.   82. The correct answer is C: a telephone order is a verbal order given by a provider over the phone that the nurse reads back to confirm accuracy before implementing it.   83. The correct answer is B: an advance directive is a legal document that outlines a client's wishes for future medical care if they become unable to communicate those wishes themselves. An incident report documents an unusual event such as a fall or medication error, not future care wishes. A nursing care plan outlines the client's current goals and interventions, not legal future care preferences. An informed consent form authorizes a specific procedure and is signed at the time care is provided, not in advance for future situations.
  84. The correct answer is D: handoff communication is the structured transfer of client care information and responsibility from one nurse to another, such as at shift change. Delegation refers to assigning a specific task to another team member, not transferring overall responsibility for a client. Triage refers to prioritizing clients based on acuity, most often in emergency settings. Case management refers to coordinating a client's services across providers and settings over time.
  85. The correct answer is B: when an LPN believes an order may be unsafe, the appropriate first step is to clarify the order directly with the provider who wrote it. Asking a family member for an opinion is not appropriate since family members do not have the clinical expertise to clarify a medical order.

Paternalism describes making decisions for a client rather than respecting the client's own choices, which is the opposite of autonomy.

Option A places the signature before the client has a chance to ask questions. Option B places the question-and-answer step before the explanation itself has occurred. Option D places the witnessed signature before the client has actually signed the form.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B, the order that follows recognizing the finding first, then notifying, then implementing ordered interventions, then reassessing: the nurse must identify and validate an abnormal finding before anyone can act on it, and interventions should not begin until they have been ordered by the provider after notification. Option A places notification before the finding has even been recognized. Option C implements interventions before the provider has been notified and had a chance to order them. Option D reassesses before the ordered interventions have actually been implemented.
  2. The correct answer is D, the order that follows assessing needs first, coordinating referrals based on that assessment, providing teaching, and finally confirming follow-up: this order ensures referrals reflect an accurate picture of the client's needs and that teaching precedes the final confirmation of the discharge plan. Option A confirms follow-up appointments before teaching has been provided. Option B coordinates referrals before the client's needs have actually been assessed. Option C provides teaching before referrals have been coordinated, which may miss services the teaching should address.
  3. The correct answer is C, the order that follows asking whether a directive exists, obtaining a copy, confirming the code status order matches it, and communicating that status to the team: the nurse cannot obtain a copy of a directive without first confirming one exists, and the code status order cannot be verified against the directive until a copy is available. Option A confirms the order matches before a copy has been obtained. Option B obtains a copy before confirming whether a directive exists at all. Option D communicates the code status before actually confirming it matches the directive.
  4. The correct answer is D, the order that follows assessing the client first, notifying the registered nurse or provider, implementing any ordered interventions, and completing the incident report last: the client's immediate safety must be assessed before anything else, and interventions cannot be implemented until they have been ordered following notification. Option A completes the incident report before the ordered interventions have even been implemented. Option B notifies before the client has been assessed for adverse effects. Option C implements interventions before the provider has been notified and had a chance to order them.
  5. The correct answer is A, the order that follows assessing decision-making capacity first, explaining the risks, notifying the provider or registered nurse, and finally offering the against medical advice form: the client's capacity should be assessed before further discussion, and the form should only be offered after the provider has been notified. Option B explains the risks before capacity has been assessed. Option C notifies the provider before the risks have been explained to the client. Option D offers the form before the provider has actually been notified of the client's intent.
  6. The correct answer is B, the order that follows the SBAR framework as situation, background, assessment, and recommendation: starting with the current situation orients the receiving nurse before background details are added, and the recommendation logically follows the assessment findings. Option A begins with background instead of the situation prompting the transfer. Option C presents assessment findings before the relevant background information. Option D states the recommendation before the current assessment findings have been shared.
  7. The correct answers are A, B, and C: a significant blood pressure drop, new chest tightness even though it resolved, and minimal urine output over several hours are all abnormal findings that should be communicated to the oncoming registered nurse as a priority for further follow-up. Intermittent sleep, fluids infusing at the ordered rate, standby-assisted ambulation, and medications due at a scheduled time are all routine, expected details that do not require priority reporting.
  8. The correct answers are A, B, and C: increasing redness, warmth, and tenderness at the incision, a fever of 38.9 degrees Celsius, and new sudden shortness of breath are all concerning findings that require notification of the registered nurse or provider. Minimal drainage with a well-healing incision, tolerating a regular diet, walking short distances daily, and a scheduled follow-up appointment are all reassuring, expected findings that do not require this level of escalation.
  9. The correct answers are A, B, and C: a heart rate of 132/min with a low blood pressure, new numbness and tingling in a casted extremity, and a lack of urination for over 8 hours despite adequate fluid intake are all abnormal findings the UAP's report must be brought to the registered nurse's attention. Eating most of a meal, ambulating without difficulty, routine linen changes, and a family visit are all normal, non-urgent details from the UAP's report.
  10. The correct answers are A, B, and C: living alone with significant stair difficulty, needing wound irrigation the client cannot yet perform independently, and having no identified support person at home are all findings that indicate the client needs additional referral support before discharge. Being alert, oriented, and eager to go home, having well-controlled pain, confirmed transportation, and understanding the medication schedule are all reassuring findings that do not by themselves indicate a need for additional referral.
  11. The correct answers are A, B, and C: a code status order that does not match the advance directive, no copy of the directive actually present in the chart, and uncertainty about whether the client's wishes have changed are all situations that indicate the documentation is incomplete or needs clarification before the procedure. A signed directive on file, current contact information for the healthcare power of attorney, and a provider's note referencing the directive are all reassuring findings that suggest the documentation is otherwise in order.
  12. The correct answers are A, B, and C: a client who states they do not understand why the procedure is being done, a signature obtained immediately after a sedating medication, and questions that were apparently never answered by the provider all suggest the consent process may not have been completed appropriately. A signed and dated form, provider documentation of discussing risks and alternatives, and a witnessed signature alone do not guarantee the client's understanding was genuinely confirmed before signing.
  13. The correct answers are Row 1: Appropriate, Row 2: Not Appropriate, Row 3: Appropriate, Row 4: Not Appropriate, Row 5: Appropriate: ambulating a client with an already-assessed gait belt, recording routine vital signs, and feeding a client already assessed as able to swallow safely are all standardized tasks appropriate for a UAP. Administering an intramuscular injection and teaching a client about medication side effects both require licensed nursing judgment and are not appropriate to delegate to a UAP.
  14. The correct answers are Row 1: Immediately, Row 2: Next Scheduled Follow-up, Row 3: Immediately, Row 4: Next Scheduled Follow-up, Row 5: Immediately: a rapid weight gain, new significant swelling with worsening shortness of breath, and waking up gasping for air are all signs of worsening heart failure that require immediate notification. Gradually improving energy and a minor dietary question can both be safely addressed at the next scheduled follow-up.
  15. The correct answers are Row 1: Currently Has, Row 2: Currently Lacks, Row 3: Currently Has, Row 4: Currently Lacks, Row 5: Currently Has: a client who can accurately explain the procedure, ask thoughtful questions and state a clear choice, or describe the alternatives discussed all demonstrate current decision-making capacity. A client who is disoriented to place and cannot recall being informed, or who is unresponsive and unable to communicate any preference, currently lacks the capacity needed to provide informed consent.
  16. The correct answers are Row 1: Within LPN Scope, Row 2: Requires RN, Row 3: Within LPN Scope, Row 4: Requires RN, Row 5: Within LPN Scope: reinforcing teaching already provided, administering a scheduled subcutaneous medication, and monitoring and documenting stable vital signs are all within LPN scope of practice. Completing the initial comprehensive nursing assessment and developing and initiating the client's individualized plan of care both require the clinical judgment reserved for a registered nurse.
  17. The correct answers are Row 1: Is Ready, Row 2: Is Not Yet Ready, Row 3: Is Ready, Row 4: Is Not Yet Ready, Row 5: Is Ready: correctly repeating back the medication schedule, having a confirmed follow-up appointment, and having stable vital signs for 24 hours all support that the client is ready for discharge. An inability to demonstrate a new dressing technique independently and a lack of arranged transportation both indicate the client is not yet ready for a safe discharge.
  18. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: sudden shortness of breath along with an oxygen saturation of 89% together suggest an acute cardiopulmonary complication, so the priority action is to stop the activity, position the client safely, and notify the registered nurse immediately rather than continuing or delaying. An oxygen saturation of 89% is frank hypoxemia and a more urgent, specific abnormal finding than the heart rate of 128/min, which is a nonspecific tachycardia that can also result from pain, anxiety, or hypoxia itself. The parameters that show whether the action worked are the oxygen saturation trending back toward baseline and the heart rate returning toward the client's baseline range, since both directly reflect improved oxygenation and reduced cardiovascular strain.
  19. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: an assignment that includes a task outside the nurse's competency along with multiple unstable clients assigned simultaneously signals a genuinely unsafe workload, so the priority action is to voice the concern to the charge nurse or supervisor before accepting the assignment as given. The parameters that confirm the concern was resolved are the assignment actually being adjusted to remove tasks outside the nurse's scope and competency, and the nurse then being able to safely monitor all remaining assigned clients.
  20. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: unexplained bruising in various stages of healing together with fearfulness in the caregiver's presence are classic warning signs of elder abuse, so the priority action is to report the suspicion to the appropriate protective services agency rather than addressing it informally or waiting. The parameters that confirm the situation has been appropriately addressed are the client being assessed and confirmed safe from further harm and the protective services agency actually being notified and investigating.
  21. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: an inability to explain why the procedure is being done combined with an inability to recall the previously explained risks both raise genuine concern about the client's current decision-making capacity, so the priority action is to notify the provider or registered nurse so capacity and understanding can be reassessed before proceeding. The parameters that confirm this concern has been resolved are the client being able to accurately explain the procedure, risks, and alternatives, and the provider confirming the client currently has the capacity to consent.
Case Study CJ-B-SAFE-1

A 58-year-old client with a peripherally inserted central catheter (PICC) for long-term antibiotic therapy is being cared for on a medical unit. During the morning assessment, the practical nurse notes the PICC insertion site on the client's upper arm is red, warm, and mildly swollen, with a small amount of purulent drainage. The client reports the arm feels sore and states a chill this morning. The client's temperature is 38.6 degrees Celsius, heart rate is 104/min, and blood pressure is 118/74 mmHg. The client remains alert and oriented and denies pain elsewhere.

  1. The correct answer is A: purulent drainage and redness at the PICC insertion site is the priority finding because it is the most specific, objective sign pointing toward a localized infection at the catheter site. The client reporting soreness is a subjective symptom that supports but does not confirm the concern by itself. A blood pressure of 118/74 mmHg is within normal limits. Remaining alert and oriented is a reassuring finding, not a concerning one.
  2. The correct answer is B: purulent drainage, redness, and swelling at the PICC site combined with fever and chills together point toward a localized catheter-related infection that could progress to systemic involvement. These findings are not a normal expected response to antibiotic therapy. There is no indication described that suggests an allergic reaction to the dressing material specifically. The client's presentation, including fever and purulent drainage, is not consistent with simple dehydration.
  3. The correct answer is A: given the site infection findings along with fever and tachycardia, the priority concern is that this could progress to a catheter-related bloodstream infection, a potentially serious systemic complication. A pressure injury is not supported by the assessment findings described. Client dissatisfaction with the arm's appearance is not a clinical priority compared to a possible infection. A delayed antibiotic dose is a logistical concern, not the priority clinical complication to consider here.
  4. The correct answer is B: notifying the registered nurse or provider of the PICC site findings and vital signs is the appropriate first planned action so that further assessment, cultures, and treatment can be ordered promptly. Continuing the antibiotic infusion without addressing the site findings delays needed intervention. Applying a warm compress without further assessment does not address the underlying concern for infection. Removing a PICC catheter independently without a provider order and notification is not an appropriate first action for the nurse to take.
  5. The correct answer is C: obtaining blood cultures and a wound culture as ordered by the provider is an appropriate action for the practical nurse to implement once the provider has been notified and interventions have been ordered, since these cultures help identify the causative organism and guide treatment. Discontinuing antibiotic therapy without a provider order is outside the nurse's scope and could worsen the client's infection. Applying a new dressing without documenting the site's appearance fails to track the wound's progression. Reassuring the client that no further action is needed is inaccurate and could delay necessary treatment.
  6. The correct answer is D: a temperature trending toward normal along with diminishing redness at the insertion site indicates the infection is responding to treatment. A heart rate that continues to rise above baseline suggests the client's condition is worsening rather than improving. Increased swelling and drainage at the site would indicate the infection is progressing, not resolving. A new onset of chills suggests an ongoing or worsening systemic infectious process rather than improvement.
Case Study CJ-B-SAFE-2

An 82-year-old client is admitted to the medical-surgical unit after a fall at home. The client has osteoporosis, takes a scheduled diuretic, and received a first dose of a new sedative-hypnotic for sleep at bedtime last night. This morning the practical nurse finds the client sitting on the edge of the bed attempting to stand alone, appearing unsteady, and stating she needs to reach the bathroom urgently. The bed exit alarm is present but was not turned on, and the call light is clipped to the bed rail within the client's reach.

  1. The correct answer is B: a client who is unsteady, attempting to stand alone, and feeling urgency to void is actively engaging in the exact combination of behaviors most likely to cause an immediate fall. The call light being within reach is a reassuring finding, not a risk. Osteoporosis increases the severity of injury from a fall but is not the active event happening this morning. The bed alarm not being turned on is a system gap, but it is the client's unsteady attempt to stand that is most urgent.
  2. The correct answer is D: a diuretic increases urine output and urgency, a new sedative-hypnotic can cause residual drowsiness and impaired balance, and together these produce both the drive to rush to the bathroom and the physical impairment that makes a fall likely. Osteoporosis and admission alone do not explain the sudden urgency and unsteadiness seen this morning. The alarm status is an equipment issue, not a physiologic cause. Age and sex alone are nonspecific and do not explain today's behavior.
  3. The correct answer is A: the combination of sedation, unsteady mobility, and urgent need to void places this client at immediate risk for a fall-related injury, which takes priority over comfort or knowledge concerns. Impaired urinary elimination describes the diuretic's expected effect, not the safety threat itself. Disturbed sleep pattern is not evidenced by the data given. Deficient knowledge has not been assessed and is a lower priority than an active physical safety risk.
  4. The correct answer is C: activating the bed alarm alerts staff if the client attempts to rise unsupervised, and scheduled toileting proactively addresses the urgency caused by the diuretic before it becomes urgent. A practical nurse cannot discontinue a prescribed medication independently. Instructing an unsteady, sedated client to ambulate alone increases rather than reduces fall risk. Removing the call light eliminates the client's main way to safely request help.
  5. The correct answer is A: with the client already unsteady and attempting to move, the immediate priority is to physically stay with her and provide safe assistance to prevent a fall right now. Leaving the room to get equipment abandons an unsteady client mid-attempt. Telling her to wait does not address that she is already out of bed and moving. Documentation is important but must never delay an immediate safety intervention.
  6. The correct answer is B: consistently using the call light and waiting for staff before attempting to rise shows the client has adopted the safe behavior the interventions were designed to promote. An increased diuretic dose would raise, not lower, fall risk. A comment about the sedative's effectiveness does not reflect fall prevention outcomes. Disconnecting the bed alarm removes a key safety measure and would indicate a failure of the plan, not success.
Case Study CJ-B-SAFE-3

A client has had an indwelling urinary catheter in place for 5 days following surgical repair of a hip fracture. The practical nurse notes the drainage bag is positioned above the level of the bladder and is resting on the floor beside the bed. The urine in the tubing is cloudy and dark amber, and the client reports new suprapubic discomfort. The client's temperature is 38.4 degrees Celsius, increased from 37.0 degrees Celsius recorded the previous day.

  1. The correct answer is C: fever, cloudy dark amber urine, and new suprapubic discomfort together are classic signs of a urinary tract infection and are the findings the nurse must recognize as clinically significant. Catheter duration is a risk factor, not a sign of current infection. The type of surgery does not itself indicate infection. A transparent drainage bag is a normal, expected feature of the equipment.
  2. The correct answer is A: a drainage bag positioned above the bladder allows urine to flow backward into the bladder, and a bag resting on the floor introduces contamination, both of which directly increase infection risk. Age alone is a nonspecific risk factor, not a cause of this event. Anesthesia type has no direct bearing on catheter-associated infection. Discomfort alone is a symptom, not a contributing cause.
  3. The correct answer is D: the improperly positioned drainage system combined with new fever and cloudy urine makes infection risk the priority nursing problem to act on now. Impaired mobility is an ongoing postoperative concern but is not urgent today. Acute incisional pain is not supported by the data given, which describes suprapubic rather than incisional discomfort. Deficient fluid volume is not supported by any assessment finding presented.
  4. The correct answer is B: repositioning the bag below the bladder and off the floor corrects the exact hazard identified and prevents further backward flow and contamination. Disconnecting the closed drainage system unnecessarily breaks its sterility and increases infection risk. Catheter irrigation requires a specific order and is not the first action for this finding. Clamping the tubing can cause urinary retention and is not an appropriate independent action.
  5. The correct answer is A: the nurse should immediately correct the equipment hazard and report the new fever and abnormal urine so the registered nurse or provider can evaluate for infection without delay. Increasing fluids may be helpful later but does not replace prompt reporting of a possible infection. Removing an indwelling catheter without an order is outside the practical nurse's scope. Waiting until the next scheduled assessment delays recognition of a developing infection.
  6. The correct answer is C: a temperature returning toward baseline along with urine clearing to a normal color both show that treatment of the infection and correction of the drainage problem are working. Worsening discomfort, persistently cloudy urine, and a further rise in temperature would all indicate the infection is progressing rather than resolving, meaning the plan is not yet effective.
  7. The correct answer is C: Clostridioides difficile forms spores that alcohol-based hand rub does not reliably kill, so soap-and-water hand washing with friction and rinsing is required. Alcohol-based rub alone leaves spores on the hands. A dry paper towel does not remove or kill organisms. Lotion followed by hand rub still fails to address spores and adds no protective benefit here.
  8. The correct answer is A: gloves are the last item put on during standard donning sequence so that the hands, which will touch the most surfaces, are protected right up until room entry. Eye protection is put on earlier in the sequence, after the mask. A second gown provides no additional protection and is not part of standard donning. Shoe covers are not part of contact and droplet precautions unless specifically indicated.
  9. The correct answer is D: gloves are removed first because they are the most contaminated item and removing them first prevents transferring organisms to the gown, mask, or the nurse's own hands. The gown is removed after the gloves. The mask and eye protection are removed even later, after hand hygiene, since they protect the face and are handled by their clean ties or straps.
  10. The correct answer is B: standard precautions are applied to every client encounter because infection status is often unknown, and they form the baseline of infection prevention for all care. Limiting them to confirmed infections or only visibly soiled items misses the many situations where organisms are present without obvious signs.
