Standard precautions do not replace transmission-based precautions; they are used together when a specific organism requires additional measures.
  11. The correct answer is A: tuberculosis is spread by airborne droplet nuclei, so a negative-pressure room with the door closed prevents contaminated air from escaping into the hallway. A positive-pressure room is used for protective isolation of immunocompromised clients, the opposite of what is needed here. A curtained semi-private room does not contain airborne organisms. A surgical mask on the client alone does not control room air movement.
  12. The correct answer is B: Neisseria meningitidis is spread through large respiratory droplets produced by coughing, talking, or close contact, which requires droplet precautions. Airborne precautions are reserved for organisms that travel in smaller particles over longer distances, such as tuberculosis. Contact precautions alone do not address the respiratory route of transmission. Protective isolation is used to shield an immunocompromised client, not to contain an infectious one.
  13. The correct answer is C: MRSA in uncontained wound drainage spreads through direct or indirect contact, so gowns and gloves for contact precautions are required in addition to standard precautions. Droplet and airborne precautions address respiratory transmission, which is not the route of concern for this wound. Standard precautions alone are insufficient once drainage cannot be contained.
  14. The correct answer is D: an N95 respirator only forms an effective seal against airborne particles when the wearer has been fit-tested for that exact model and size, since faces and respirator shapes vary. A coworker's size is not a substitute for personal fit testing. Respirators are not reused indefinitely and must be evaluated for damage or contamination. Labeling a respirator with the client's name has no bearing on its protective seal.
  15. The correct answer is B: an uncapped needle placed directly into a puncture-resistant sharps container at the point of use is the safest practice and prevents accidental needlestick injury. Recapping with both hands is a well-documented cause of needlestick injuries and should never be done. Regular trash does not protect staff or waste handlers from sharps. Bending a needle also increases the risk of an accidental puncture.
  16. The correct answer is A: immediately washing the puncture site with soap and water reduces the organism load at the exposure site and is the first step before any other action. Reporting to the supervisor and seeking evaluation should follow promptly, but washing comes first. Applying ointment and continuing work delays proper evaluation of an exposure. Squeezing the site is not recommended and does not reduce infection risk.
  17. The correct answer is D: using two identifiers, such as name and date of birth, checked against the medication administration record is the standard method to confirm the right client before giving medication. Room number can change and is not an acceptable identifier. A door sign can be outdated or incorrect. A family member's statement does not confirm the client's own identity directly.
  18. The correct answer is B: RACE stands for Rescue, Alarm, Confine, and Extinguish, and rescuing anyone in immediate danger always comes first regardless of whether the alarm has already sounded. Attempting to extinguish a fire before ensuring people are safe risks harm to both client and nurse. Confining the fire is done after people are rescued. Activating the alarm is important but does not take priority over an occupant in immediate danger.
  19. The correct answer is A: PASS stands for Pull, Aim, Squeeze, Sweep, and pulling the pin is required before the extinguisher can be activated at all. Aiming at the base of the flames is the second step, not the first. Squeezing the handle before pulling the pin will not release any agent. Sweeping side to side is the final step, performed only after the extinguisher is already discharging.
  20. The correct answer is C: a frayed cord is an electrical and fire hazard, so the equipment must be taken out of service and reported for repair or replacement rather than used further. Taping over damaged wiring does not restore electrical safety. Continuing to use damaged equipment risks shock or fire even if the infusion is nearly finished. Changing outlets does not correct the hazard within the cord itself.
  21. The correct answer is B: less restrictive alternatives, such as covering or padding the site and increasing supervision, must be tried and documented before a restraint is applied, since restraints are always a last resort. Applying a restraint immediately skips required alternatives. A signed consent form does not substitute for trying alternatives first. Sedation for the purpose of restricting movement is itself considered a chemical restraint and requires the same careful process.
  22. The correct answer is D: circulation, skin integrity, and the need for continued restraint must be assessed frequently, typically at least every 2 hours or as specified by facility policy, to prevent complications. Once per shift is far too infrequent to catch early circulatory compromise. Waiting for the client to request assessment is unsafe since a confused client may not report problems. Once daily monitoring is grossly inadequate for a client in restraints.
  23. The correct answer is A: lowering the client safely to the floor and positioning on one side protects from a fall and helps maintain an open airway by allowing secretions to drain. Restraining the extremities during a seizure can cause fractures or other injury and does not stop the seizure. Inserting any object into the mouth during a seizure can cause airway obstruction or dental injury and is never recommended. Leaving the client unattended risks injury from an unwitnessed fall.
  24. The correct answer is C: padded side rails and a low bed position reduce the risk of injury if a seizure occurs, especially a fall from bed. A high bed position increases the distance and severity of a potential fall. Removing all bedding provides no protective benefit and reduces comfort unnecessarily. Strict bed rest is not required for seizure precautions and unnecessarily limits the client's activity.
  25. The correct answer is B: mechanical lifts and friction-reducing devices used with sufficient staff protect both the client and the nurse from injury during a manual handling task. Lifting alone while bending at the waist places excessive strain on the nurse's lower back. Pulling by the arms risks shoulder injury to the client. Holding the breath does not improve safety and can raise intrathoracic pressure unnecessarily.
  26. The correct answer is D: a conscious adult with a completely obstructed airway who cannot speak or cough needs immediate abdominal thrusts to attempt to dislodge the obstruction. Back blows alone are less effective for a complete obstruction in this situation and are typically combined with, not substituted for, thrusts. Giving water can worsen the obstruction. Leaving to call for help before attempting to clear the airway delays a lifesaving intervention.
  27. The correct answer is C: an unresponsive client who is not breathing normally requires the nurse to call for help and start chest compressions immediately, since early compressions are critical to survival. Checking blood glucose delays a life-saving intervention and is not the priority in cardiac arrest. Documentation must wait until the emergency is addressed. Waiting for another staff member to arrive before beginning compressions wastes critical time.
  28. The correct answer is A: the nurse must first assess the client's condition and promptly notify the registered nurse or provider so appropriate monitoring or treatment can begin without delay. Waiting for symptoms to appear before reporting risks the client's safety. Correcting documentation without notifying the appropriate staff leaves the client unmonitored for potential harm. Discussing the error with family should not occur before the client has been assessed and the appropriate staff notified.
  29. The correct answer is B: high-alert medications such as heparin require an independent double check by a second qualified nurse to catch calculation or preparation errors before they reach the client. Skipping verification because the order looks clear removes an important safety barrier. Rounding a dose for convenience can cause a dangerous dosing error. Verification of a high-alert medication cannot be delegated to unlicensed personnel.
  30. The correct answer is D: carefully reading the complete label and comparing it against the medication administration record is the most reliable way to avoid confusing look-alike, sound-alike medications. Package shape can be similar across different drugs and is not a safe identifier. Accepting a medication from a coworker without independently checking the label bypasses a critical safety step. Relying on memory of storage location is unreliable, since stock and placement can change.
  31. The correct answer is A: using non-latex gloves and supplies eliminates the client's exposure to the allergen and is the correct action once a latex allergy is documented. Using standard latex gloves exposes the client to a known allergen regardless of perceived risk level. A barrier cream does not prevent an allergic reaction to latex contact. Delaying necessary care to reverify an already documented allergy is unnecessary and can compromise wound care.
  32. The correct answer is C: a severely neutropenic client has minimal ability to fight infection, so visitors who are ill or have recently received a live vaccine pose a real risk and should be excluded. Clothing type has no bearing on infection risk. Hand hygiene is required for every visitor, not optional. Fresh flowers and fruit can carry mold or bacteria and are typically restricted for neutropenic clients.
  33. The correct answer is D: a loose, moist dressing no longer maintains a sterile barrier over the insertion site and significantly increases the risk of a catheter-related infection, so it must be reported and changed promptly. A dressing changed on the facility's scheduled interval is expected and not concerning by itself. A clean, dry, intact site is a normal reassuring finding. A temperature of 37.0 degrees Celsius is within normal limits.
  34. The correct answer is B: increasing redness, warmth, and purulent (pus-like) drainage are classic signs of a developing surgical site infection and must be reported. A well-approximated incision with mild bruising is an expected postoperative finding. Serosanguineous drainage on the day of surgery is normal and expected, not a warning sign. Mild tenderness that responds to analgesic is an expected part of normal postoperative recovery.
  35. The correct answer is A: routine perineal hygiene reduces bacterial colonization near the insertion site, and keeping the bag below the bladder prevents backward flow of contaminated urine, both key infection-prevention measures. Disconnecting the closed drainage system unnecessarily introduces organisms into a normally sterile circuit. Routine antiseptic irrigation is not recommended and can actually increase infection risk. Letting the bag rest on the floor exposes it to contamination.
  36. The correct answer is C: chemotherapy-contaminated linens are hazardous waste and must be placed in a specially labeled container to protect staff and the environment from exposure. Regular laundry bags do not provide the containment or handling required for hazardous waste. Rinsing contaminated linens in a sink spreads the hazardous substance into the plumbing and exposes the nurse. Standard trash disposal does not meet the safe-handling requirements for chemotherapy waste.
  37. The correct answer is B: limiting time near the source and maximizing distance are core radiation safety principles that reduce staff and visitor exposure. Spending unrestricted time at the bedside increases cumulative radiation exposure. A surgical mask offers no protection against radiation. Positioning visitors close to the client increases their exposure rather than protecting them.
  38. The correct answer is A: open flames of any kind, including candles, must never be used where oxygen is in use because oxygen supports combustion regardless of tubing length, so this statement shows a dangerous misunderstanding. Keeping the tank away from open flames, posting a no-smoking sign, and storing tanks upright and secured are all correct oxygen safety practices that require no further teaching.
  39. The correct answer is C: lowering the bed and locking the wheels reduces fall risk and prevents the bed from moving unexpectedly, and this basic safety measure should be performed for every client. Leaving the bed elevated increases the distance and severity of a potential fall. Unlocked wheels can allow the bed to shift during transfer or repositioning. Raising all four rails without an assessed need can be considered an unnecessary restraint.
  40. The correct answer is A: confirming the correct sling size and secure attachment before initiating the lift is essential to prevent the client from falling or being injured during the transfer. Whether the client has eaten has no bearing on the safety of the lift equipment. The chair should be positioned close to the transfer path, not across the room. Family presence is not a required safety check for using a mechanical lift.
  41. The correct answer is D: a client who is breathing with a strong pulse but cannot walk is classified as delayed, or yellow, since the injury is significant but not immediately life-threatening. Red is reserved for clients with immediate, life-threatening problems such as an unmanaged airway. Green is used for minor injuries in clients who are able to walk. Black is used for clients who are deceased or not expected to survive given available resources.
  42. The correct answer is B: a reservoir is a place where an organism can live and multiply, and standing water in equipment is a well-known example. Hands that have just been cleaned represent a controlled, not a reservoir, state. Intact skin is a natural barrier against infection, not a reservoir. The route of exit from an infected person describes the portal of exit, a different link in the chain.
  43. The correct answer is A: reaching across a sterile field risks contaminating it with clothing, skin particles, or unsterile arm surfaces passing over the field, so this action breaks sterile technique. Keeping sterile items in view, treating the outer 1 inch of the field as contaminated, and opening supplies away from the body are all correct principles that maintain sterility.
  44. The correct answer is D: placing the client near the nurses' station with frequent monitoring is a least-restrictive measure that directly addresses elopement risk while preserving the client's mobility and dignity. A four-point restraint is highly restrictive and inappropriate before less restrictive options are tried. Checking on the client only once per shift is far too infrequent for an elopement risk. Leaving the door unlocked without other safeguards increases the risk of the client leaving unnoticed.
  45. The correct answer is B: poison control specialists can give guidance specific to the substance ingested, and contacting them immediately is the priority before any other action is taken. Inducing vomiting can cause additional harm with many caustic or petroleum-based products and should never be done without professional guidance. Giving milk is not universally safe and may be contraindicated depending on the substance. Waiting for symptoms delays care for a potentially serious ingestion.
  46. The correct answer is A: the client wears a surgical mask during transport for source control to contain respiratory secretions. Staff escorting a masked client through the hallway do not need to wear an N95 respirator during the transport itself; the N95 is reserved for staff entering the client's negative-pressure airborne isolation room. An N95 respirator on the client is not appropriate, since its purpose is to protect the wearer, not contain the client's droplets. Transporting without any mask leaves hallways and other areas unprotected. Necessary tests are not canceled; transport is arranged with appropriate precautions instead.
  47. The correct answer is C: like C. difficile, norovirus is not reliably killed by alcohol-based hand rub, so soap-and-water hand washing is required to physically remove the virus from the hands. Relying on alcohol rub exclusively leaves norovirus particles on the hands. Waiting until leaving the facility misses many opportunities to prevent transmission during care. Wearing gloves does not eliminate the need for hand hygiene after glove removal.
  48. The correct answer is D: single-dose vials are intended for one client and one use only, and any unused medication must be discarded to prevent cross-contamination between clients. Saving the remainder for another client, storing an opened single-dose vial in shared stock, and reusing a needle for a second client are all unsafe injection practices that can transmit infection between clients.
  49. The correct answer is A: reusable equipment such as a blood pressure cuff must be disinfected according to facility policy between clients to prevent transmission of organisms. Plain water does not effectively disinfect a surface. Visible cleanliness does not indicate the absence of microorganisms. Storing shared equipment in one client's drawer risks cross-contamination and equipment mix-up between clients.
  50. The correct answer is B: loose throw rugs are a well-documented tripping hazard, and for a client with unsteady gait removing or securing them is the priority to prevent a fall. A nightlight, grab bars, and a bedside phone are all positive safety measures that reduce, rather than increase, fall or emergency response risk.
  51. The correct answer is C: gowns and gloves for contact precautions prevent the organism from being carried on staff clothing or hands to other clients or surfaces, which directly answers the client's question honestly and accurately. This response is inaccurate because contact precautions are specific to certain organisms, not required for all clients. VRE can spread through skin and environmental contact even without an open wound. Isolation for VRE is typically based on culture results and facility policy, not on fever resolution alone.
  52. The correct answer is A: the "recommendation" portion of SBAR states a specific suggested action for the oncoming nurse to take, such as rechecking a vital sign at a defined interval. The client's age and admitting diagnosis belong in the "situation" or "background" portion. A specific temperature reading belongs in the "assessment" portion. Past medical history such as hypertension and diabetes belongs in the "background" portion, not the recommendation.
  53. The correct answer is D: every medication error must be reported and documented according to facility policy regardless of perceived severity, since transparent reporting supports client safety and helps identify system issues. Saying nothing hides information the healthcare team needs for monitoring. Documenting only if the client notices makes reporting inconsistent and unreliable. Waiting for a repeat error before reporting delays needed system improvements and puts future clients at risk.
  54. The correct answer is B: hand hygiene immediately before entering the room of a severely immunosuppressed client is the single most effective action to reduce the organism load the nurse might otherwise introduce. A gown is used based on anticipated contact, not automatically for every entry. Requesting additional cleaning is not the immediate priority action for the nurse at this moment. Reviewing vital signs is useful but does not reduce the immediate infection risk of room entry.
  55. The correct answer is C: a retractable or shielded safety mechanism automatically covers the needle after use, directly reducing the risk of an accidental needlestick injury to staff. Needle length has no bearing on injury prevention. A brightly colored hub may aid visibility but does not prevent injury. Faster packaging opening improves convenience but does not address needlestick safety.
  56. The correct answer is A: evacuating the most mobile clients first allows them to move independently with minimal staff assistance, freeing staff to focus on wheelchair-dependent and then the most resource-intensive bedridden clients last. Evacuating bedridden clients first is inefficient since they require the most staff and time per client. Proximity to the nurses' station is not the determining factor for evacuation order. Random evacuation without a mobility-based plan increases confusion and risk during an emergency.
  57. The correct answers are A, B, and E: wearing a surgical mask within close range, placing the client in a private room or cohorting with another client with the same infection, and consistent hand hygiene are the correct components of droplet precautions. An N95 respirator is reserved for airborne, not droplet, organisms. The door does not need to remain closed for droplet precautions since large droplets do not remain suspended in air. Allowing an infectious, actively coughing client to walk freely in common areas risks spreading droplets to others and should not be permitted.
  58. The correct answers are A, B, D, and F: new fever with chills, increasing confusion, a rising heart rate paired with falling blood pressure, and cool clammy skin together suggest the body's response to an overwhelming infection and possible early sepsis. A stable, unchanged blood pressure and a urine output within the client's normal range are both reassuring findings that do not indicate a developing sepsis picture.
  59. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: a history of falling, medications that cause urgency and sedation, impaired vision, and orthostatic hypotension are all well-established fall risk factors that should raise a client's fall-risk score. A steady, independent gait and normal cognitive status are both reassuring findings that lower, rather than raise, fall risk.
  60. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: hand hygiene, aseptic dressing changes, scrubbing the hub before access, and changing tubing on schedule are all evidence-based practices that reduce CLABSI risk. Leaving a loose or damp dressing in place compromises the sterile barrier and increases infection risk rather than preventing it. Accessing the line without gloves fails to maintain aseptic technique.
  61. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: swelling, redness with warmth, blanched cool skin, and reported burning pain are all signs of infiltration or phlebitis that require the site to be reassessed and likely discontinued. Brisk capillary refill and a strong distal pulse are both normal, reassuring findings that do not indicate a problem with the intravenous site.
  62. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: using a gait belt, locking wheels before a transfer, using assistive devices for lateral moves, and obtaining adequate help beforehand are all recognized safe patient handling practices. Bending at the waist to lift places dangerous strain on the nurse's spine, and lifting more than half a client's weight alone is a well-documented cause of nurse injury and should always be avoided.
  63. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: hand hygiene is required before touching a client, after touching the client's surroundings, before a clean or aseptic procedure, and after any exposure risk to body fluids, forming the WHO's 5 moments framework. Restricting hand hygiene to only visibly soiled hands or only once at the start of a shift misses the vast majority of situations where transmission of organisms can occur.
  64. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: washing the site, reporting the exposure promptly, seeking timely professional evaluation, and documenting the source client if known are all required steps after a needlestick injury to allow for appropriate testing and, if needed, post-exposure prophylaxis. Delaying the report until the end of the shift wastes critical time for prophylaxis, and no needle can be assumed safe based on appearance alone.
  65. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: hives with itching, swelling of the lips or tongue, wheezing with difficulty breathing, and hypotension are all classic signs of anaphylaxis, which can occur rapidly after a new medication is given and requires immediate reporting and treatment. Stable, unchanged vital signs are reassuring rather than concerning. Anaphylaxis develops rapidly, typically within minutes, not gradually over several days.
  66. The correct answers are A, B, D, and F: a sporicidal disinfectant is required because C. difficile forms spores that ordinary disinfectants do not reliably kill, high-touch surfaces need focused cleaning, privacy curtains that may be contaminated should be laundered or discarded, and cleaning from least to most soiled prevents spreading organisms into cleaner areas. Alcohol-based wipes alone do not kill spores. The bathroom must be terminally cleaned regardless of earlier cleaning, since it is a high-contamination area.
  67. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: an evacuation plan, an extra medication supply, a backup power source for medical equipment, and accessible emergency contacts are all essential elements of disaster preparedness for a client with ongoing medical needs. Ignoring evacuation orders puts the client at serious risk regardless of how the client feels. Storing medical supplies loose and unorganized makes them harder to locate and grab quickly during an emergency.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answers are A, C, and E: a client endangering a life-sustaining device despite redirection, a client at risk of self-extubation, and a client with a physician order and documented ongoing risk of self-harm all meet appropriate criteria for restraint use after less restrictive measures have failed. A mildly anxious but cooperative client does not meet criteria. A client's simple preference against restraint is not itself a clinical indication, and restraint applied only for staff convenience is never appropriate.
  2. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: coughing or choking with swallowing, a wet or gurgly voice afterward, pocketing food, and a delayed swallow reflex are all signs of impaired swallowing that place the client at risk for aspiration. A strong, effective cough reflex and a normal, clear voice after swallowing are both reassuring findings that indicate intact swallowing function.
  3. The correct answers are A, B, D, and F: a newly identified allergy, a fall, a medication error, and a change in level of consciousness are all urgent safety findings that must be clearly communicated during hand-off so the oncoming nurse can continue appropriate monitoring. Stable, unchanged vital signs and a pending result with no abnormal values yet are routine information, not urgent safety flags.
  4. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: verifying client, procedure, and site, marking the surgical site, performing a full team time out, and confirming accurate consent are all required elements of the Universal Protocol used to prevent wrong-site surgery. Proceeding without a completed time out defeats the purpose of the safety check regardless of team confidence, and starting immediately without these verification steps is unsafe.
  5. The correct answers are A, C, D, and F: non-blanchable redness, an open area with tissue loss, warmth with induration, and reported tenderness over a bony prominence are all early or established signs of pressure injury that require prompt reporting. Skin that blanches normally is a healthy finding, and a consistent repositioning schedule being followed is a positive care measure, not a concerning finding.
  6. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: activating the bed alarm, keeping the bed low, ensuring nonskid footwear, and scheduled rounding are all core components of an evidence-based fall prevention bundle. Leaving the call light out of reach removes the client's main way to safely request help, and disabling alarms to reduce noise removes a critical safety warning system.
  7. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: sudden fever with chills, flank or back pain, hives with itching, and hypotension are all signs associated with an acute transfusion reaction and require the infusion to be stopped immediately and reported. Stable, unchanged vital signs are reassuring, and mild warmth limited to the intravenous site alone, without other systemic signs, is not indicative of a transfusion reaction.
  8. The correct answer is A: this organism forms spores that resist alcohol-based hand rub, so soap-and-water washing with friction and rinsing is required to physically remove them. MRSA, influenza, and respiratory syncytial virus are all effectively addressed by alcohol-based hand rub and do not require this special spore-focused precaution.
  9. The correct answer is B: varicella spreads through airborne particles and also through direct contact with the lesions, so both types of precautions are required together. Contact and droplet precautions alone would not address the airborne route. Droplet precautions alone misses both the airborne and contact routes of transmission. Standard precautions alone are not sufficient for a client with active varicella.
  10. The correct answer is C: gloves are the most contaminated item after client contact and are removed first to prevent transferring organisms to other equipment or the nurse's skin. The mask and eye protection are removed later, protected by their clean straps. The gown is removed after the gloves, not before.
  11. The correct answer is B: restraint orders for medical, non-behavioral reasons are typically reassessed and renewed at least once every 24 hours per facility policy, ensuring the ongoing need for the restraint is regularly evaluated. Renewal every 4 hours is closer to the shorter interval used for some behavioral restraint orders in certain settings. Waiting 72 hours or 7 days between reassessments would leave the client in a restraint far too long without adequate reevaluation.
  12. The correct answer is A: the RACE mnemonic stands for Rescue, Alarm, Confine, and Extinguish, in that order, guiding the nurse's actions from immediate rescue through attempting to extinguish a small, containable fire. Evacuate, Escape, and Enclose are not part of the standard RACE sequence used in healthcare fire response.
  13. The correct answer is C: replacing a sharps container once it reaches about three-quarters full prevents overfilling, which can cause a sharp to protrude and injure staff attempting to dispose of another item. Replacing at only one-quarter or one-half full wastes containers unnecessarily. Waiting until a container is completely full significantly increases the risk of a needlestick or sharps injury.
  14. The correct answers are Blank 1: A and Blank 2: B: uncontained wound drainage positive for MRSA requires contact precautions because the organism spreads through direct or indirect contact, and unlike airborne precautions, contact precautions do not require the door to remain closed since the organism does not travel through the air. Droplet or airborne precautions would be incorrect since MRSA in wound drainage is not spread by the respiratory route, and standard precautions alone are insufficient once drainage cannot be contained by a dressing.
  15. The correct answers are Blank 1: B and Blank 2: B: a new fever with chills and tachycardia after a central line insertion is a classic presentation of a catheter-related bloodstream infection, and the priority action is to notify the registered nurse or provider immediately so cultures and treatment can begin without delay. Local skin irritation, a normal postoperative response, and dehydration do not explain this sudden systemic change. Waiting hours to reassess, increasing the infusion rate, or applying a warm compress all delay recognition and treatment of a potentially serious bloodstream infection.
  16. The correct answers are Blank 1: B and Blank 2: A: influenza spreads primarily through respiratory droplets produced by coughing or sneezing, so covering coughs and performing frequent hand hygiene are the key measures to teach the family to reduce spread in the home. Airborne droplet nuclei describe a different, longer-range route used by organisms such as tuberculosis. Contaminated water and insect bites are not routes of influenza transmission. Showering once daily, wound care, and oral rinsing alone do not address the hand-to-surface transmission that hand hygiene specifically prevents.
  17. The correct answers are Blank 1: B and Blank 2: B: verification with two identifiers and the blood bank tag by two licensed staff members prevents a transfusion from being given to the wrong client, and remaining at the bedside for the first 15 minutes allows the nurse to detect an early transfusion reaction, since most severe reactions occur early in the infusion. Relying on the client's stated name alone, room number, or a verbal order alone does not meet the required independent double-identification process. Only 5 minutes is too brief to reliably capture an early reaction, while waiting 60 minutes or 2 hours delays recognition of a reaction that typically begins earlier.
  18. The correct answers are Blank 1: A and Blank 2: B: a gait belt provides a secure hand-hold that supports an unsteady client during a standing transfer, and locking the wheelchair wheels prevents it from rolling away during the transfer, both of which directly prevent a fall. A restraint vest and cervical collar are not appropriate or necessary equipment for this standard transfer. Leaving the wheels unlocked, removing them entirely, or locking only one side would all allow the wheelchair to move unexpectedly and increase fall risk.
  19. The correct answers are Blank 1: B and Blank 2: A: oxygen supports combustion, so tanks must be kept at least 10 feet from open flames, and posting a "no smoking, oxygen in use" sign near the entrance alerts visitors and emergency responders to the hazard. A distance of only 1 foot is far too close to be safe, while 50 or 100 feet is unnecessarily and impractically far for a home setting. Signs about pets, deliveries, or quiet are unrelated to the specific fire hazard that oxygen use creates.
  20. The correct answer is A, the standard donning sequence of hand hygiene, gown, N95 respirator with a seal check, eye protection, and gloves last: this order protects the most exposed surfaces last so the hands remain clean until the final step before room entry. Option B places gloves before hand hygiene and the rest of the equipment, which contaminates the gloves before any protective gear is on. Option C puts the respirator on before the gown, reversing the correct torso-then-face sequence. Option D puts eye protection on before the gown and respirator, out of the standard order.
  21. The correct answer is C, hand hygiene followed by gown, mask, eye protection, and gloves last: this sequence protects the cleanest areas first and lets the gloves cover the gown cuffs, sealing the barrier. Option A places the mask before the gown, risking contamination of the mask while tying the gown. Option B places gloves before the gown and omits hand hygiene first, breaking the barrier sequence. Option D delays the mask until after eye protection, which does not follow the standard donning order.
  22. The correct answer is A: gloves are removed first because they are the most contaminated item, followed by eye protection, then the gown, and the mask is removed last since it protects the airway until the wearer is farthest from the client. Option B removes the mask while the gloves are still contaminated, risking self-contamination of the face. Option C removes the gown before the gloves, contaminating bare forearms. Option D removes the mask before the gown, exposing the airway too early.
  23. The correct answer is D, following the RACE sequence of rescue, alarm, contain, and extinguish or evacuate: removing anyone in immediate danger comes first, then the alarm is sounded to summon help, doors are closed to contain smoke and fire, and the fire is extinguished if small or the area is evacuated. Option A delays rescuing clients until after the alarm. Option B contains the fire before rescuing clients in danger. Option C attempts to extinguish or evacuate before anyone in danger has been rescued or the alarm sounded.
  24. The correct answer is B: the chain begins with an infectious agent, which must have a reservoir to live and multiply, a portal of exit to leave that reservoir, a mode of transmission to travel to a new location, a portal of entry into a new host, and finally a susceptible host capable of becoming infected. Option A starts with the reservoir before an agent has been established. Option C places the portal of entry first, before the agent or reservoir exist. Option D places transmission before the organism has left its reservoir through a portal of exit.
  25. The correct answer is B: the nurse first calls for help and activates the emergency response system, checks for a pulse for no more than 10 seconds, begins chest compressions promptly if no pulse is found, opens the airway, and delivers two rescue breaths after every 30 compressions. Option A delays calling for help until after checking a pulse. Option C attempts rescue breaths before compressions or confirming responsiveness. Option D begins compressions before confirming the client is unresponsive and pulseless and before calling for help.
  26. The correct answers are A, B, and C: redness with purulent drainage at the insertion site, chills with a warm sensation, and a new fever of 39.1 degrees Celsius together suggest a catheter-related bloodstream infection and must be reported. A dressing changed 2 days ago per protocol is routine care, and a tube feeding infusing at the prescribed rate and pain-free ambulation are both unremarkable, expected findings.
  27. The correct answers are A, B, and C: separated wound edges with visible tissue, a sudden gush of clear pink fluid, and a sensation of the incision pulling apart with coughing are all signs of wound dehiscence, a surgical emergency requiring immediate reporting. A steady ambulating gait, active bowel sounds, and improved oral intake are all expected, reassuring findings during postoperative recovery.
  28. The correct answers are A, B, and C: frequent foul-smelling watery stools, new abdominal distension and tenderness, and diarrhea that began after antibiotic therapy together raise concern for Clostridioides difficile infection, which requires contact precautions and prompt reporting. Stable vital signs, tolerating breakfast, and normal skin turgor are reassuring findings that do not by themselves indicate an infectious process.
  29. The correct answers are Row 1: Droplet Precautions, Row 2: Airborne Precautions, Row 3: Contact Precautions, Row 4: Standard Precautions Only, Row 5: Droplet Precautions: influenza and pertussis spread through large respiratory droplets and require droplet precautions, suspected pulmonary tuberculosis spreads through airborne droplet nuclei and requires airborne precautions with a fitted respirator, and MRSA wound infection spreads by direct contact and requires contact precautions. A client recovering from surgery with no infection needs only standard precautions.
  30. The correct answers are Row 1: Expected, Row 2: Unexpected, Row 3: Expected, Row 4: Unexpected, Row 5: Expected: mild soreness, full symmetric movement without new pain, and a small stable bruise are common after a minor fall. New pupil inequality with decreased consciousness and a sudden severe headache with vomiting both suggest a possible intracranial injury and are unexpected findings that require immediate reporting.
  31. The correct answers are Row 1: Near miss, document per facility policy, Row 2: Sentinel event, report immediately to risk management, Row 3: Sentinel event, report immediately to risk management, Row 4: Near miss, document per facility policy, Row 5: Sentinel event, report immediately to risk management: a sentinel event is an unexpected occurrence resulting in serious harm or death, such as a wrong-type transfusion, a retained surgical item, or a fall causing a hip fracture. A near miss is an error caught before it reached the client, such as one stopped before administration or an order clarified before it was given.
  32. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: B, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: C, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: B: facial swelling with wheezing and a significant blood pressure drop after starting an intravenous antibiotic are hallmark signs of anaphylaxis, so the priority action is to stop the infusion immediately, notify the registered nurse, and prepare for emergency treatment rather than slowing, continuing, or delaying the response. Resolution of the swelling, wheezing, and hives along with the blood pressure returning toward baseline confirm the reaction is improving.
  33. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: B, Priority Action: C, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: B: the universal choking sign of clutching the throat with an inability to cough, speak, or make sound, along with dusky lips, indicates complete airway obstruction, so the priority action is to perform abdominal thrusts until the object is expelled or the client becomes unresponsive. A client who regains the ability to cough forcefully and speak, with lip color returning to normal, has successfully cleared the obstruction.
Case Study CJ-B-HEAL-1

A 26-year-old client at 26 weeks gestation attends a prenatal visit. A 1-hour glucose challenge test result is 158 mg/dL (normal is less than 140 mg/dL). The client reports increased thirst and frequent urination over the past 2 weeks. Prepregnancy body mass index was 31 kg/m2. Vital signs are within normal limits, and fetal heart rate is 148/min with a reactive pattern.

  1. The correct answer is A: a 1-hour glucose challenge result of 158 mg/dL exceeds the normal cutoff of less than 140 mg/dL and is the finding most significant for recognizing gestational diabetes risk. A fetal heart rate of 148/min and a reactive pattern are both normal, reassuring findings. The client's age of 26 years alone is not a risk indicator.
  2. The correct answer is A: an elevated glucose challenge result together with increased thirst, frequent urination, and a prepregnancy body mass index in the obese range are a recognized cluster of findings that support gestational diabetes. A reactive fetal heart rate and normal vital signs do not by themselves indicate glucose intolerance. Age and gestational week alone, or urination without other findings, do not form a supporting pattern.
  3. The correct answer is C: the elevated glucose challenge result combined with classic hyperglycemia symptoms of increased thirst and urination and a risk factor of prepregnancy obesity make gestational diabetes mellitus the priority concern. A urinary tract infection, preterm labor, and iron deficiency anemia are not supported by the findings described in this visit.
  4. The correct answer is D: an abnormal 1-hour glucose challenge result is followed by a 3-hour oral glucose tolerance test to confirm the diagnosis of gestational diabetes, so the nurse should anticipate this next step being ordered. Scheduling routine follow-up with no further testing or telling the client the result is normal both ignore an abnormal finding. Increasing carbohydrate intake would worsen hyperglycemia and is not appropriate.
  5. The correct answer is C: the client should fast as instructed and follow the specific preparation guidelines provided, since accurate results depend on correct test preparation. Discontinuing prenatal vitamins is unnecessary and not indicated. Completely restricting carbohydrates could cause an inaccurate result and is not the correct preparation. Skipping the test if symptoms improve would leave a possible diagnosis of gestational diabetes unconfirmed.
  6. The correct answer is B: fasting and postprandial glucose values remaining within the target range indicate the client's blood glucose is well controlled. Continued thirst and urination would suggest ongoing hyperglycemia rather than control. Prepregnancy weight change is not a measure of current glucose control, and a fetal heart rate of 170/min is above the expected range and is an unrelated, concerning finding.
Case Study CJ-B-HEAL-2

A parent brings a 4-month-old infant to a well-child visit. The infant was born at term with no complications. The parent reports the infant can roll from stomach to back, reaches for objects, and babbles, but is not yet holding the head steady when pulled to a sitting position. The infant's weight has increased from 7 pounds 8 ounces at birth to 12 pounds. Immunizations are up to date.

  1. The correct answer is A: head lag when pulled to a sitting position is most significant because most infants have developed sufficient neck control to hold the head steady by 4 months of age. Weight gain within an expected range, babbling, and reaching for objects are all appropriate findings for this age and are not signs of delay.
  2. The correct answer is A: by 4 months of age, most infants have developed enough neck and trunk control to hold the head steady when pulled to sitting, so persistent head lag at this age is significant for gross motor development. Head lag is not an expected finding through 6 months. Weight gain reflects nutritional status, not neurological development, and babbling assesses language development, not motor skills.
  3. The correct answer is B: head lag when pulled to sitting is a gross motor finding, making gross motor development the priority area of concern for this infant. Language development is supported by the infant's babbling, and social development and fine motor development are not the domains affected by the finding described.
  4. The correct answer is C: persistent head lag at 4 months warrants referral for further developmental evaluation as directed by the registered nurse or provider so the cause can be identified early. Reassuring the parent without further evaluation and waiting until 9 months both delay identification of a possible delay. Limiting tummy time would reduce, not support, the activity that strengthens neck and trunk muscles.
  5. The correct answer is D: supervised tummy time several times a day strengthens the neck and trunk muscles needed for head control and supports gross motor development. Keeping the infant supine at all times, delaying tummy time until 6 months, and restricting handling to feeding times all reduce the opportunities the infant needs to build motor strength.
  6. The correct answer is C: holding the head steady with only minimal lag when pulled to sitting shows the infant's neck and trunk strength has improved, indicating progress in gross motor development. Continued head lag with no improvement would indicate the concern remains unresolved. Weight gain and increased babbling reflect nutritional and language progress, not gross motor development.
  7. The correct answer is D: excessive cow's milk intake displaces iron-rich foods from the diet and can cause occult gastrointestinal blood loss, both of which increase the risk of iron deficiency anemia in toddlers. Iron-fortified cereal, pureed lean meats, and green leafy vegetables are all good sources of iron and should be encouraged, not limited.
  8. The correct answer is A: uneven shoulder height with a rib hump noted on the forward bend test are classic signs of scoliosis and warrant referral to a provider. Equal leg lengths and symmetric hips, normal spinal curvature, and full shoulder range of motion are all expected findings that do not indicate scoliosis.
  9. The correct answer is B: installing grab bars near the toilet and in the shower provides stable support in high-risk areas and correctly reflects fall prevention teaching. Loose throw rugs are a tripping hazard, loose-fitting slippers increase the risk of slipping, and a waxed floor becomes more slippery, all increasing fall risk rather than reducing it.
  10. The correct answer is B: the newborn's mouth covering most of the areola with audible swallowing indicates an effective, deep latch that allows adequate milk transfer. Lips tucked inward instead of flanged outward, sharp pain throughout the feeding, and rapid sucking without pauses all indicate an ineffective, shallow latch that needs correction.
  11. The correct answer is A: the recombinant zoster vaccine is routinely recommended for adults beginning at age 50 to prevent shingles and is appropriate to discuss with this 55-year-old client. Rotavirus vaccine is given only in infancy, Haemophilus influenzae type b vaccine is a childhood vaccine, and a first dose of measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine is not an age-based recommendation for this client.
  12. The correct answer is D: school-age children are in Erikson's industry versus inferiority stage and develop a sense of competence through completing tasks successfully, so providing achievable school work and craft projects best supports this stage. Independent exploration reflects toddlerhood, trust through feeding reflects infancy, and simple daily choices reflect early childhood, not the school-age stage.
  13. The correct answer is C: beginning a folic acid supplement before conception is recommended to reduce the risk of neural tube defects, since these defects develop very early in pregnancy, often before a pregnancy is confirmed. Vitamin C intake, caffeine limits, and avoiding dairy products are all reasonable general health measures but do not specifically reduce neural tube defect risk.
  14. The correct answer is D: bone mineral density testing is recommended for postmenopausal women, including this 66-year-old client, to screen for osteoporosis. Prostate-specific antigen testing and testicular self-examination teaching apply to clients with male anatomy and are not appropriate for this client. Sweat chloride testing is used to diagnose cystic fibrosis and is unrelated to age-based screening.
  15. The correct answer is B: placing soft blankets and a pillow in the crib increases the risk of suffocation and sudden infant death syndrome and indicates a need for further teaching. Placing the infant supine to sleep, keeping the crib free of stuffed animals, and room sharing without bed sharing are all correct, recommended safe sleep practices.
  16. The correct answer is C: feelings of hopelessness combined with giving away a favorite possession are recognized warning signs of suicide risk and must be reported as a priority. Occasional disagreements with parents, changing friendships, and normal exam-related nervousness are all common, expected experiences during adolescence and are not warning signs requiring urgent reporting.
  17. The correct answer is A: because the rubella vaccine is a live attenuated vaccine, the client should avoid becoming pregnant for at least 4 weeks after receiving it to prevent potential harm to a future fetus. Immunity does not develop within 24 hours, breastfeeding does not need to be stopped after this vaccine, and a second dose the next day is not part of routine postpartum administration.
  18. The correct answer is A: low-fat fortified dairy products provide calcium and vitamin D, both of which support bone health in older adults. Foods high in sodium and processed meats, foods low in protein, and foods high in saturated fat do not support bone health and are generally discouraged as part of a health-promoting diet.
  19. The correct answer is C: stacking a tower of six to seven blocks is an expected fine motor milestone for a 2-year-old child. Tying shoelaces, riding a bicycle with training wheels, and printing a first and last name legibly are all milestones typically achieved several years later, in the preschool or early school-age years.
  20. The correct answer is B: applying sunscreen with an SPF of 30 or higher and reapplying every 2 hours provides ongoing protection against ultraviolet radiation and correctly reflects the teaching. Ultraviolet exposure occurs even on cloudy days, tanning beds also emit harmful ultraviolet radiation and are not a safer alternative, and all skin tones, including darker skin, benefit from sunscreen use.
  21. The correct answer is D: the ability to sit with support and maintain good head control shows the infant has the postural and neuromuscular development needed to safely begin solid foods. Doubling birth weight and sleeping through the night are not readiness indicators, a strong tongue-thrust reflex actually indicates the infant is not yet ready, and continued exclusive breastfeeding alone does not indicate readiness for solids.
  22. The correct answer is C: rechecking blood pressure at least once every 1 to 2 years is generally recommended for an adult with a normal reading, since hypertension is often asymptomatic and can develop silently over time. Waiting 5 or 10 years, or waiting for symptoms to appear, both delay detection of a condition that frequently produces no early warning signs.
  23. The correct answer is B: scrubbing the circumcision site with soap and a washcloth can disrupt healing and cause irritation, so this statement indicates a need for further teaching. Applying petroleum jelly with diaper changes, changing diapers frequently, and calling the provider for foul odor or pus are all correct, recommended aspects of circumcision site care.
  24. The correct answer is D: the human papillomavirus vaccine series is routinely recommended to begin at age 11 or 12. Pneumococcal conjugate vaccine and hepatitis A vaccine as a first-time dose are typically completed in early childhood, and inactivated poliovirus vaccine boosters follow a different schedule than the one appropriate for this visit.
  25. The correct answer is A: encouraging the client to bring all medications to each provider visit allows every prescriber to see the full medication list and helps identify duplications or interactions, which is especially important with multiple providers and pharmacies. Stopping medications independently, ignoring dosing instructions, and informing only one provider all increase the risk of medication errors and adverse effects.
  26. The correct answer is D: a sudden severe headache that does not resolve can be a sign of preeclampsia and should be reported immediately, correctly reflecting the teaching. Clear odorless discharge, at least ten fetal movements in 2 hours, and mild end-of-day foot swelling are all expected, reassuring findings in the third trimester and do not need to be reported.
  27. The correct answer is A: using simple words and a doll to demonstrate the procedure shortly before it occurs matches a preschooler's concrete, magical-thinking stage of development and reduces anxiety through hands-on, developmentally appropriate teaching. A written explanation assumes reading ability the child does not yet have, detailed anatomical terms are too abstract for this age, and avoiding all explanation can increase fear of the unknown.
  28. The correct answer is C: routine colorectal cancer screening is generally recommended to begin at age 45 for an average-risk client, making this the correct teaching point for a 46-year-old client. Waiting until age 65, screening only when blood is visible in the stool, and screening only with a family history all delay detection of a cancer that can be present without symptoms.
  29. The correct answer is B: a bulging anterior fontanel while the infant is calm and in an upright position can indicate increased intracranial pressure and requires further evaluation. A closed posterior fontanel with a flat, open anterior fontanel, a startle response to loud noise, and brief eye contact are all expected findings for a 2-month-old infant.
  30. The correct answer is B: limiting recreational screen time and encouraging active outdoor play increases physical activity and correctly reflects obesity prevention teaching. Offering sugary drinks, using dessert as a reward, and allowing unlimited portion sizes all promote excess calorie intake and reinforce eating patterns that increase the risk of childhood obesity.
  31. The correct answer is C: facing the client directly and speaking in a lower-pitched, clear voice supports lip reading and is easier to hear for clients with presbycusis, which typically affects high-frequency sounds first. Speaking loudly and quickly can distort speech and be harder to understand, standing behind the client prevents lip reading, and relying only on written communication is unnecessary and impractical for most interactions.
  32. The correct answer is A: breastfed newborn stools are typically soft, seedy, and mustard yellow, which is an accurate description for the nurse to teach. Hard, formed, brown stools describe formula-fed or older infant stool patterns rather than a breastfed newborn's. No stool for the first week is not expected, and stools every hour would suggest excessive frequency that should be evaluated, not dismissed.
  33. Irregular cycles in the first couple of years after menarche, tracking cycle length, and using a heating pad for mild cramping are all accurate, expected teaching points.
  34. The correct answer is A: setting a specific quit date and removing cigarettes from the home are concrete preparatory actions that indicate the client is in the preparation or action stage of readiness to change. Vague plans for the distant future, denial that smoking affects health, and conditional plans dependent on the provider's requirement all indicate a lower level of readiness.
  35. The correct answer is D: avoiding nighttime driving, when reduced vision and glare from headlights are most hazardous, along with regular vision reassessment, directly addresses this client's specific risks. Heavy traffic hours and increased highway driving both raise the demands on reaction time, and telling the client vision changes do not affect driving safety is inaccurate and unsafe.
  36. The correct answer is B: for a client with a prepregnancy body mass index in the normal range, a total weight gain of about 25 to 35 pounds is generally recommended and supports healthy fetal growth. Gaining no more than 10 pounds is too little, avoiding weight gain until the third trimester ignores steady growth needs earlier in pregnancy, and gaining at least 50 pounds exceeds the recommended range for this client.
  37. The correct answer is C: a brief, consistent time-out proportional to the child's age is an appropriate, developmentally suited discipline strategy for a 3-year-old. Spanking is not recommended, ignoring a child for an entire day is excessive and not developmentally appropriate, and detailed reasoning about complex consequences exceeds a 3-year-old's cognitive ability to understand.
  38. The correct answer is D: periodic lipid screening is generally recommended beginning in early adulthood, and the panel shown, with several values above the reference range, supports the value of this ongoing screening even without a family history. Waiting until age 65, screening weekly, and screening only with a family history are all inaccurate approaches to lipid screening intervals.
  39. The correct answer is C: jaundice that appears within the first 24 hours of life is considered pathologic, not normal, and requires prompt evaluation, so this statement indicates a need for further teaching. Watching for face-to-downward progression of yellowing, calling if the newborn is difficult to wake for feedings, and keeping the scheduled follow-up bilirubin check are all correct, accurate teaching points.
  40. The correct answer is A: regular physical activity and structured relaxation techniques are evidence-based strategies that help reduce stress and support overall well-being. Increasing caffeine intake can heighten anxiety and disrupt sleep, avoiding all social interaction removes a valuable source of support, and working extended hours tends to increase, rather than reduce, overall stress.
  41.   42. Vigorous crying when hungry and sleeping between feedings are both normal, expected newborn behaviors.
  43.   44.   46.   47.   49.   50. Regular weight-bearing exercise and adequate calcium and vitamin D intake both help protect bone density and are protective, not risk, factors.
  51. The correct answers are A, B, D, and F: making eye contact and talking softly, referring to the newborn by name, responding promptly to cues, and asking questions about newborn care are all findings that indicate effective parent-infant bonding. Immediately handing the newborn off without holding and avoiding touch during care both suggest limited engagement and would warrant further nursing attention.
  52. The correct answers are A, C, D, and F: gradual weight loss, at least 150 minutes of moderate physical activity per week, fiber-rich whole foods, and routine follow-up glucose screening are all evidence-based recommendations to help prevent progression from prediabetes to type 2 diabetes. Increasing sugar-sweetened beverage intake and skipping meals can both worsen blood glucose control and are not recommended.
  53. A consistent appetite and regular meal pattern and normal albumin and prealbumin levels would both be reassuring findings and are not present for this client.
  54. The correct answer is A: object permanence is the understanding that objects continue to exist even when they cannot be seen, and this ability typically develops between 8 and 12 months of age. Parallel play describes toddlers playing near but not with each other, egocentrism describes an inability to see another's perspective, and separation individuation describes a toddler's developing sense of self as distinct from a caregiver.
  55. The correct answer is B: the third stage of labor begins with the delivery of the newborn and ends with the delivery of the placenta. The first stage covers labor from the onset of true contractions to full cervical dilation, the second stage covers delivery of the newborn itself, and the fourth stage refers to the immediate recovery period after the placenta is delivered.
  56. The correct answer is D: a body mass index of 31 kg/m2 falls in the obese range, since obesity is generally classified as a body mass index of 30 kg/m2 or higher. A body mass index this high does not fall in the underweight, normal weight, or overweight categories, which are all classified at lower ranges.
  57. The correct answer is C: Erikson described the older adult stage of reflecting on life and achieving fulfillment or experiencing despair as ego integrity versus despair. Generativity versus stagnation describes middle adulthood, identity versus role confusion describes adolescence, and intimacy versus isolation describes young adulthood, all earlier stages in Erikson's model.
  58. The correct answers are Blank 1: B and Blank 2: B: a heart rate over 100 scores 2 points, slow irregular respirations score 1 point, some flexion scores 1 point, a grimace reflex scores 1 point, and a pink body with blue extremities (acrocyanosis) scores 1 point, for a total Apgar score of 6, which falls in the moderately depressed range of 4 to 6. A score of 3 would reflect a much more depressed newborn, and scores of 9 or 10 describe newborns in good condition with minimal or no depression.
  59.
