Crossing only one line or none at all would be less concerning, and this pattern does not reflect accelerated growth or a recent growth spurt.
  60. The correct answers are Blank 1: B and Blank 2: C: routine Papanicolaou screening for an average-risk client generally begins at age 21 and is repeated approximately every 3 years when results are normal. Starting at age 18 is earlier than recommended, and starting at 30 or 40 would delay screening. Repeating every 6 months or annually is more frequent than needed for a normal result, and every 10 years is too infrequent.
  61. The correct answers are Blank 1: C and Blank 2: A: aging is associated with a diminished sense of thirst, meaning older adults may not feel thirsty even when their body needs fluid, increasing their risk for dehydration if intake is not actively encouraged. Thirst sensation does not become heightened or remain unchanged with age, and the primary risk from reduced thirst sensation is dehydration rather than fluid overload, hyperglycemia, or hypertension.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D: infants typically roll from stomach to back around 4 months, sit without support around 6 months, crawl around 8 to 9 months, pull to stand around 9 to 10 months, and walk independently around 12 months, progressing from head and trunk control toward upright mobility. Option A places sitting before rolling. Option B places pulling to stand before sitting and crawling after walking. Option C places crawling before rolling and sitting.
  2. The correct answer is C: hand hygiene and sanitizing supplies come first to prevent contamination, followed by measuring the water, adding the correct number of scoops of powder, mixing until dissolved, and finally testing the temperature before feeding to prevent burns. Options A and B both sequence the preparation steps out of order and test the temperature at the wrong point. Option D tests the temperature before the formula has even been measured or mixed.
  3. The correct answer is A: the examination is performed monthly, ideally after a warm shower when the scrotal skin is relaxed, each testicle is gently rolled between the thumb and fingers to feel for lumps or changes compared with the last examination, and any new finding is reported promptly to a provider. Options B, C, and D all place the reporting step or the feeling step before the examination has actually been performed.
  4. The correct answer is B: the child is positioned at the correct marked distance first, then covers one eye at a time while reading the smallest line possible on the chart, the results for each eye are recorded, and referral is made only after the results have been obtained and compared with the expected range. Options A, C, and D each place recording or referral before the screening itself has been completed.
  5. The correct answers are A and B: bringing the hands to the mouth with rooting, and smacking or sucking sounds, are early hunger cues that should prompt the nurse to offer a feeding before the newborn becomes distressed. Loud crying with facial flushing is a late hunger cue that indicates the newborn is already distressed, not an early cue. Normal vital signs and acrocyanosis of the extremities are unrelated, expected newborn findings.
  6. The correct answers are A, B, and C: statements of hopelessness and social withdrawal, significant unintentional weight loss with loss of interest in cooking, and recent bereavement with withdrawal from a previously enjoyed community group together suggest depression and social isolation warranting further assessment. A clean, organized home, stable vital signs, and an organized medication routine are all reassuring findings that do not indicate a mental health concern.
  7. The correct answers are Row 1: Expected for age, Row 2: Expected for age, Row 3: Delayed for age, Row 4: Expected for age, Row 5: Delayed for age: two-word phrases, running steadily, and following simple one-step directions are all expected by 2 years of age. A 2-year-old who cannot yet walk independently or has no recognizable words is showing signs of delayed development that warrant further evaluation.
  8. The correct answers are Row 1: Normal finding in pregnancy, Row 2: Requires follow-up, Row 3: Normal finding in pregnancy, Row 4: Requires follow-up, Row 5: Normal finding in pregnancy: mild dependent ankle edema, regular fetal movement, and Braxton Hicks contractions that resolve with rest are all common and expected in later pregnancy. A blood pressure of 158/102 mmHg and a sudden increase in swelling with a severe headache are both signs of possible preeclampsia and require prompt follow-up.
  9. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: B, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: B: jitteriness, a weak cry, and a blood glucose of 32 mg/dL in a newborn of a mother with gestational diabetes indicate hypoglycemia, so the priority action is to initiate early feeding and notify the registered nurse rather than delaying or restricting feeding. A repeat glucose rising above 45 mg/dL and resolution of jitteriness with the newborn becoming easily consoled both confirm the hypoglycemia is improving.
  10. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: B, Priority Action: C, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: significant unintentional weight loss with meal skipping and excessive exercise, combined with an intense fear of gaining weight despite being underweight, are hallmark features of a possible eating disorder that require notifying the registered nurse or school counselor and referring the student for further evaluation. Engagement in follow-up care and the student's weight stabilizing toward a healthy range both indicate the referral and intervention are working.
Case Study CJ-B-PSYC-1

A 45-year-old client is admitted for an elective surgical procedure. During the admission interview, the client reports drinking 6 to 8 beers daily for the past several years and states the last drink was 10 hours ago. The client appears anxious, and the hands are visibly tremulous. Vital signs are temperature 37.6 degrees Celsius, heart rate 108/min, blood pressure 148/92 mmHg, and respiratory rate 20/min.

  1. The correct answer is C: a reported history of drinking 6 to 8 beers daily with the last drink 10 hours before admission places this client at significant risk for alcohol withdrawal syndrome, which can begin within hours of the last drink. The scheduled procedure, respiratory rate, and the client's age alone do not indicate withdrawal risk.
  2. The correct answer is A: tremulous hands, anxiety, and elevated heart rate and blood pressure together are classic early signs of alcohol withdrawal caused by central nervous system hyperactivity as the body adjusts to the absence of alcohol. Normal temperature and respiratory rate alone do not support this pattern, a history of drinking without physical findings is not yet corroborated, and elective surgery without vital sign changes provides no supporting evidence.
  3. The correct answer is B: given the history of heavy daily alcohol use and the emerging physical signs of tremors, anxiety, and elevated heart rate and blood pressure, risk for alcohol withdrawal syndrome is the priority concern, since untreated withdrawal can progress to seizures or delirium tremens. Surgical site infection, impaired skin integrity, and aspiration risk are not supported by the current findings and are not the immediate priority.
  4. The correct answer is D: the nurse should notify the registered nurse and anticipate orders for a standardized withdrawal assessment protocol along with frequent vital sign monitoring so early symptoms can be identified and treated before they progress. Proceeding without additional monitoring ignores a known risk, advising the client to resume drinking is unsafe and outside the nurse's role, and withholding this information from the surgical team could compromise the client's safety.
  5. The correct answer is C: while awaiting further orders, the nurse should reassess vital signs frequently to track the progression of withdrawal symptoms and maintain a calm, low-stimulation environment to reduce anxiety and prevent overstimulation. Restraining the client's hands does not address the underlying cause, unsupervised ambulation could be unsafe given the client's tremors and anxiety, and administering an opioid analgesic does not treat withdrawal and could mask worsening symptoms.
  6. The correct answer is D: a decrease in tremors along with vital signs trending back toward the client's baseline indicates the withdrawal symptoms are being effectively managed. A further increase in heart rate, increasing confusion and agitation, or a rise in blood pressure would all indicate the withdrawal is worsening rather than improving.
Case Study CJ-B-PSYC-2

A 42-year-old client is admitted to the behavioral health unit after telling a coworker they 'don't see the point in going on anymore' following a recent divorce and job loss. The client reports poor sleep, a 10-pound weight loss over the past month, and difficulty concentrating. The client appears withdrawn, avoids eye contact, and states, 'Everyone would be better off without me.' Vital signs are within normal limits, and the client denies a specific plan or access to a weapon at this time.

  1. The correct answer is A: a statement suggesting the client believes others would be better off without them reflects hopelessness and possible suicidal ideation, which requires immediate follow-up before any other finding. Weight loss and poor sleep are expected findings in major depressive disorder but are not immediately life-threatening. Avoiding eye contact reflects withdrawal but does not by itself signal danger to the client.
  2. The correct answer is B: language framing the client as a burden others would be relieved to lose reflects passive suicidal ideation layered on top of hopelessness, both established risk indicators for self-harm. Framing the statement as a normal, self-limiting reaction minimizes a genuine warning sign. Assuming attention-seeking dismisses the statement without further assessment, which is unsafe practice. Any statement referencing being better off dead always warrants further nursing assessment, not none.
  3. The correct answer is C: risk for suicide is the priority problem because the client has voiced hopelessness and a belief that others would be better off without them, both direct indicators of self-harm risk that outweigh coping, sleep, or self-esteem concerns. Ineffective coping and low self-esteem are relevant but secondary problems once immediate safety is addressed. Disturbed sleep pattern is a lower-priority physiological finding compared to a potential threat to life.
  4. The correct answer is A: directly assessing for a specific plan and means determines the client's actual level of suicide risk and guides the level of precautions needed. Journaling and group therapy are appropriate long-term interventions but do not address the immediate safety question. Waiting for the client to reintroduce the topic delays a needed safety assessment and is not an acceptable approach when hopelessness has already been expressed.
  5. The correct answer is B: asking a direct, nonjudgmental question about suicidal thoughts while staying with the client both clarifies risk and provides immediate one-to-one safety monitoring. False reassurance minimizes the client's feelings and can shut down further disclosure. Leaving the client alone removes needed supervision from a client with expressed hopelessness. Changing the subject avoids the safety issue rather than addressing it.
  6. The correct answer is A: a client who can verbalize a concrete safety plan and denies current suicidal thoughts demonstrates the most direct evidence that the identified risk for suicide has decreased. Improved sleep and eating are positive physical signs but do not confirm the client's current thoughts about self-harm. Continued reluctance to discuss feelings does not demonstrate improvement and may indicate ongoing risk that still needs assessment.
Case Study CJ-B-PSYC-3

A 55-year-old client is admitted to the medical unit for management of alcohol withdrawal after the last drink 18 hours ago. The client reports drinking approximately 10 standard drinks daily for the past 15 years. Current findings include hand tremors, diaphoresis, heart rate of 112/min, blood pressure of 152/94 mmHg, and reported anxiety. The client denies visual disturbances and is oriented to person, place, and time.

  1. The correct answer is A: a heart rate of 112 per minute combined with an elevated blood pressure reflects autonomic hyperactivity, the finding most predictive of worsening alcohol withdrawal and possible progression toward seizures or delirium tremens. Reported anxiety and hand tremors are expected early withdrawal symptoms but carry less immediate risk than significant vital sign changes. The time since the last drink provides context but is not itself a clinical finding requiring urgent action.
  2. The correct answer is B: tremors, diaphoresis, tachycardia, and hypertension occurring 18 hours after the last drink are the classic pattern of evolving alcohol withdrawal syndrome, which can progress further without close monitoring. Labeling the findings as unrelated anxiety ignores the clear temporal link to alcohol cessation. Involuntary commitment is not indicated based on a medical withdrawal presentation. Intoxication would present with different findings such as slurred speech and sedation, not autonomic hyperactivity after 18 hours of abstinence.
  3. The correct answer is C: risk for injury from a withdrawal seizure is the priority because untreated alcohol withdrawal can progress rapidly to generalized seizures, a life-threatening event. Deficient knowledge and ineffective coping are important long-term concerns but do not require the same immediate attention as physical safety. Disturbed body image is not supported by the data presented and is a lower priority even if present.
  4. The correct answer is D: notifying the registered nurse allows the ordered withdrawal protocol, which typically includes scheduled symptom scoring and medication, to begin promptly and reduce the risk of seizure. Referring the client to a support meeting is appropriate later in recovery, not during acute withdrawal. Restricting all visitors is not an evidence-based withdrawal intervention. Restraints are not indicated for a client who is currently cooperative and are never applied as a routine precaution.
  5. The correct answer is C: frequent monitoring of vital signs and withdrawal symptoms in a low-stimulation environment allows the care team to detect early signs of worsening withdrawal and intervene before a seizure occurs. Caffeine can worsen the client's existing tachycardia and anxiety. Allowing the client to leave the unit unsupervised removes needed monitoring during an unstable period. Leaving the client without checks for an extended time is unsafe given the current risk for seizure.
  6. The correct answer is B: a heart rate returning toward normal along with resolved tremors shows that autonomic hyperactivity is decreasing, the expected sign that the withdrawal protocol is working. A persistent elevated heart rate with new hallucinations suggests progression toward delirium tremens, not improvement. A further rise in blood pressure also reflects worsening autonomic instability. New reports of anxiety and visual hallucinations indicate the withdrawal syndrome is worsening, not resolving.
  7. The correct answer is D: directly assessing whether the client intends to act on the command hallucination is essential to determine immediate safety risk and guide the level of precautions needed. Asking the voices to describe their appearance does not address the safety concern raised by a command hallucination. Telling the client the voices are not real can increase distress and does not build trust. Changing the subject avoids a statement that requires urgent nursing assessment.
  8. The correct answer is C: reflecting the client's underlying feeling of being unsafe acknowledges the emotion without arguing about the content of the delusion, which is the recommended approach. Directly telling the client they are wrong can increase agitation and damage trust. Insisting something is impossible argues with the fixed false belief rather than addressing feelings. Offering proof rarely changes a delusional belief and can reinforce the client's focus on it.
  9. The correct answer is B: firm, consistent limits combined with redirection to a lower-stimulation area help reduce overstimulation and protect both the client and others from escalating behavior during acute mania. Leading a group activity increases stimulation and can worsen the client's agitation. Allowing unrestricted interaction risks disruptive or unsafe behavior toward other clients. Removing the other clients does not address the manic client's need for structure and limits.
  10. The correct answer is A: breaking tasks into small steps with available assistance matches the client's current low energy level while still promoting independence and a sense of accomplishment. Insisting on full independence can overwhelm a client with severe fatigue and lower self-esteem further. Postponing all self-care can worsen hygiene and physical health. A full day of structured activity is likely to exceed the client's current energy tolerance.
  11. The correct answer is D: new visual disturbances such as seeing shadows or shapes moving can signal progression toward alcohol withdrawal delirium, a medical emergency that requires prompt reporting. Mild nausea, restlessness, and a slight tremor are common, expected early withdrawal findings that are already being monitored under the withdrawal protocol. None of those three findings represents the same level of urgency as a new perceptual disturbance.
  12. The correct answer is C: an unresponsive client with severe respiratory depression and pinpoint pupils after an opioid dose is experiencing a probable opioid overdose, so the priority is to remain with the client and immediately call for the registered nurse, who can initiate emergency treatment such as naloxone. Waiting 30 minutes to reassess delays lifesaving care for a client in respiratory distress. An unresponsive client cannot follow a command to breathe deeply. Positioning and warming do not address the immediate threat of respiratory failure.
  13. The correct answer is A: a generalized shaking episode after abrupt discontinuation of a benzodiazepine may represent an actual seizure, the most dangerous complication of benzodiazepine withdrawal, and requires the most urgent follow-up. Insomnia, anxiety, and irritability are common, expected withdrawal symptoms that are uncomfortable but not immediately life-threatening. None of those three findings carries the same risk of physical harm as a possible seizure event.
  14. The correct answer is B: markedly elevated heart rate and blood pressure paired with chest tightness after stimulant use raise concern for cardiac ischemia, so the registered nurse must be notified immediately for prompt evaluation. Simply resting and reassessing later delays care for a potentially serious cardiac event. Caffeine would further increase heart rate and blood pressure and should be avoided. A detailed substance history is useful but is not the priority action when acute cardiac symptoms are present.
  15. The correct answer is D: consistent limits communicated across the whole care team prevent this common splitting behavior from creating conflict among staff and help the client learn more stable relationship patterns. Agreeing with the client's criticism reinforces splitting and undermines team cohesion. Spending extra individual time compared with other clients rewards the behavior and can worsen boundary difficulties. Keeping the comment from the team removes the consistency that this client's care plan depends on.
  16. The correct answer is B: verifying the claim with the care team before responding prevents the nurse from being manipulated by a client known to test limits and avoids rewarding manipulative behavior. Granting the request without verification risks reinforcing manipulation. Giving in to avoid conflict teaches the client that manipulation is effective. Directly accusing the client of lying is confrontational and is not necessary once the team confirms the actual plan.
  17. The correct answer is C: scheduling additional time for the ritual respects the client's current coping need while still allowing the client to eat, reducing anxiety that would otherwise worsen if the ritual is blocked. Physically stopping the ritual can sharply increase the client's anxiety without addressing the underlying disorder. Telling the client the ritual is unnecessary does not change compulsive behavior and can damage trust. Withholding meals punishes a symptom of the disorder rather than supporting the client.
  18. The correct answer is A: staying with the client and using a calm voice provides immediate reassurance and safety during a panic attack, which typically peaks and subsides within minutes. Leaving the client alone, even briefly, can increase fear during an acute episode. Asking for a detailed trigger history is better addressed once the acute symptoms have eased. Sending the client away isolates them during a frightening physical experience and removes needed support.
  19. The correct answer is D: calmly reorienting the client to the current time and place helps ground the client during a flashback and reduces the sense of reliving the traumatic event. Asking for detailed trauma content during an active flashback can retraumatize the client. Leaving the client unattended removes needed support during a distressing episode. Raising your voice can be perceived as threatening and may worsen the client's distress.
  20. The correct answer is A: allowing gradual, controlled exposure to the feared object, such as first viewing the packaged needle, reflects the stepwise principle of systematic desensitization and helps reduce anxiety before the actual procedure. Performing the draw without warning removes the client's sense of control and can increase fear. Dismissing the fear as irrational is invalidating and unhelpful. Full sedation is not an appropriate first-line response to a routine blood draw and phobia.
  21. The correct answer is B: slow, diaphragmatic breathing is a simple, evidence-based relaxation technique that activates the body's relaxation response and can be practiced independently at home. Avoiding all potentially anxious situations reinforces avoidance and can worsen anxiety over time. Taking extra medication without a prescriber's order is unsafe and outside client teaching about self-management. Staying constantly busy avoids addressing the anxiety directly and does not teach a coping skill.
  22. The correct answer is C: a heart rate this low combined with hypotension and dizziness signals a potentially life-threatening cardiac complication of severe malnutrition, so the registered nurse must be notified right away. Encouraging ambulation could increase the risk of fainting or a cardiac event. Waiting until the end of the shift to reassess delays care for an unstable finding. Offering food is appropriate for nutritional support but does not address the immediate cardiovascular concern.
  23. The correct answer is D: repeated vomiting causes significant loss of stomach acid and potassium, and a potassium level of 2.9 mEq/L is below normal and increases the risk for dangerous cardiac dysrhythmias. A sodium level of 140 mEq/L, a calcium level of 9.5 mg/dL, and a glucose level of 95 mg/dL are all within normal limits and do not require urgent reporting. Potassium is the electrolyte most consistently affected by this client's behavior.

Failing to document the disclosure leaves an incomplete and unsafe medical record.
  25. The correct answer is A: nurses are mandatory reporters, so suspected abuse of a vulnerable older adult must be reported to the appropriate protective services agency regardless of the client's or family's wishes. Discussing the observation with the suspected caregiver first could place the client at greater risk. Waiting for the client to directly confirm abuse is not required for reporting suspected abuse and can delay needed protection. Reporting duty does not depend on the client granting explicit permission.
  26. Accepting an inconsistent explanation without further action ignores a recognized warning sign.   27.   28. The correct answer is B: expressing sadness and beginning practical preparations before an expected death is a normal process known as anticipatory grief, which can help a person adjust gradually to an impending loss. Complicated grief refers to prolonged, impairing grief typically identified after the loss has occurred, not before. This behavior reflects healthy coping rather than poor coping and should be supported, not discouraged. Preparing for a known outcome is the opposite of denying it.
  29. The correct answer is A: an open-ended statement invites the client to share feelings in their own words and demonstrates active listening, a core therapeutic communication technique. False reassurance minimizes the client's concerns rather than exploring them. A why question can sound accusatory and puts the client on the defensive.   30. The correct answer is C: a why question can sound judgmental and puts the client on the defensive, making it a recognized nontherapeutic communication technique to avoid. Asking the client to elaborate, reflecting the underlying feeling, and offering to listen are all open, supportive techniques that encourage further disclosure. Avoiding why questions helps preserve trust and keeps the client engaged in the conversation rather than becoming defensive.
  31. Undoing involves performing an act to symbolically negate an unacceptable thought or feeling, not refusing to accept a diagnosis. Isolation involves separating a thought from its associated emotion, not denying the fact itself. Splitting involves viewing people or situations as all-good or all-bad, which is not described here.
  32. The correct answer is C: the client is attributing their own angry, uncaring feelings onto the nurse rather than acknowledging those feelings within themselves, which is the definition of projection. Denial involves refusing to accept a fact, not assigning one's own feelings to another person. Rationalization involves justifying behavior with a logical-sounding but false reason. Sublimation involves redirecting unacceptable impulses into a socially acceptable activity, which is not described here.
  33. The correct answer is B: redirecting angry feelings from the physician, the true source, onto a safer target such as a spouse is the definition of displacement. Regression involves returning to an earlier, less mature behavior pattern, which is not described here. Sublimation redirects an unacceptable impulse into a constructive activity, not toward another person. Reaction formation involves expressing the opposite of one's true feelings, which does not match this scenario.
  34. The correct answer is A: offering a logical-sounding but self-protective excuse to justify missing appointments is the definition of rationalization. Regression involves reverting to earlier, less mature coping behavior, not offering a justification.   69. The correct answer is D: a fixed false belief that persists despite clear contrary evidence is the definition of a delusion. A hallucination is a false sensory perception, such as hearing or seeing something that is not present, not a belief.   70. The correct answer is C: redirecting an unacceptable impulse, such as anger, into a socially acceptable and constructive activity, such as exercise, is the definition of sublimation. Regression involves reverting to an earlier, less mature behavior pattern, not channeling energy into a constructive outlet. Reaction formation involves expressing feelings that are the opposite of the true underlying feeling. Projection involves attributing one's own unacceptable feelings to another person rather than redirecting them into an activity.
  71. The correct answer is A: expressing frustration and a sense of unfairness about the diagnosis is characteristic of the anger stage in the Kubler-Ross model of grief.   72. The correct answer is B: restating the underlying feeling expressed by the client, in this case loneliness, back to the client is the technique of reflecting. Summarizing involves reviewing the main points of an entire conversation, not responding to a single feeling statement. Confronting involves pointing out a discrepancy in the client's behavior or statements, which also does not match this response.
  73. The correct answer is C: a failure to provide basic care such as hygiene, medications, and adequate hydration despite a caregiver being present defines neglect. Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D, the order that follows a standard de-escalation sequence of approach, acknowledge feelings, offer choices, monitor, and notify if escalation continues: this reflects starting with a calm, safe approach before assessing whether further support is needed. Option A notifies the registered nurse before monitoring the client's ongoing response, which skips a needed assessment step. Option B acknowledges feelings before approaching the client, which is not physically possible in sequence. Option C offers choices before the client has been approached and calmed, which is premature.
  2. The correct answer is A, the order that follows the standard crisis intervention sequence of assessing safety, establishing rapport, identifying resources, developing a plan, and following up: safety must be assessed first, and a plan cannot realistically be built until rapport and available resources are established. Option B assesses safety after attempting to build rapport, which delays a needed first step. Option C develops the plan before identifying the client's actual coping resources. Option D identifies resources before ever assessing the client's immediate safety, which is out of order.
  3. The correct answer is D, the order that follows safety first, then objective assessment, then documentation, then notifying the care team, and finally reporting: each step builds the accurate information needed for the next, ending with a report based on complete documented findings. Option A notifies the registered nurse before the physical assessment has even been performed, which is premature. Option B assesses the child before ensuring a safe location. Option C notifies the registered nurse before the findings have been documented, out of the expected sequence.
  4. The correct answer is B, the order that follows the standard four phases of the therapeutic relationship as preinteraction, orientation, working, and termination: the nurse prepares before meeting the client, builds trust and sets goals early in the relationship, addresses problems during the working phase, and formally ends the relationship last. Option A begins with orientation before the nurse has even reviewed available information. Option C moves into the working phase before trust and goals are established during orientation. Option D places termination before the working phase has occurred, which is out of order.
  5. The correct answer is C, the order that begins with immediate presence and calm reassurance, reduces stimulation, assesses the client's physical status, encourages controlled breathing once the client can participate, and ends with teaching a skill for future episodes: this sequence addresses the client's acute distress before moving to longer-term teaching. Option A assesses vital signs before the nurse has even approached and calmed the client. Option B encourages breathing exercises before the environment has been made calmer and before vital signs are assessed. Option D teaches a future relaxation technique before the acute episode has even been assessed, which is out of order.
  6. The correct answers are A and B: a statement describing a decided method and timing for suicide is a direct, specific plan requiring immediate reporting, and giving away a personal item while stating it will no longer be needed is a well-recognized warning sign of imminent suicide risk. A flat affect and minimal eye contact are consistent, ongoing findings of depression that should continue to be monitored but do not by themselves indicate the same immediate danger as a stated plan or giving away belongings.
  7. The correct answers are A, B, and C: controlling communication with others, restricting financial independence, and expressing fear of a partner's reaction are all recognized indicators of coercive control and possible intimate partner abuse that warrant further nursing assessment and a safety conversation. The statements about relocating for a job, enjoying reading, and having a good relationship with siblings are all neutral, unrelated background information that do not raise concern for abuse.
  8. The correct answers are A and B: a significant, rapid increase in heart rate signals worsening autonomic instability, and new tactile and visual hallucinations of insects indicate possible progression toward withdrawal delirium, both requiring immediate reporting. A fine resting tremor noted at the time of admission is an expected baseline finding already known to the care team and does not represent a new, worsening change requiring an urgent unplanned report.
  9. The correct answers are Row 1: Therapeutic, Row 2: Nontherapeutic, Row 3: Therapeutic, Row 4: Nontherapeutic, Row 5: Therapeutic: observing the client's mood and inviting conversation, reflecting an underlying feeling, and offering presence without pressure are all therapeutic techniques that encourage disclosure. Giving false reassurance minimizes the client's concerns rather than exploring them, and asking a why question can sound judgmental and place the client on the defensive, making both nontherapeutic.

Refusing to eat with a 20-pound weight loss and stating there is no reason to keep living are signs of complicated grief or possible depression and suicidal ideation that require further evaluation, not expected mourning.

False reassurance minimizes the client's real concerns, and asking 'why' puts the client on the defensive and implies judgment rather than support.
  12. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: a pounding heart with chest tightness, together with dizziness, diaphoresis, and a fear of dying, are the classic cluster of findings in an acute panic attack that the nurse must recognize immediately. The priority action is to stay with the client and use a calm, reassuring approach while checking vital signs, since leaving the client alone or ignoring the physical symptoms could worsen the episode, and success is measured by the heart rate and respiratory rate returning toward baseline along with the client reporting the fear and physical symptoms have eased.
