The priority action is to remain with the client, keep the environment safe, and notify the registered nurse right away rather than leaving the client alone or asking for a promise that does not remove the means, and effectiveness is shown by the client remaining safe and the plan and means being removed or secured.
Case Study CJ-B-BASI-1

An 82-year-old client is 2 days post-operative following open reduction internal fixation of a fractured left hip. The client has orders for bedrest with the head of the bed elevated no more than 30 degrees and has been reluctant to reposition because of pain. Skin assessment reveals a reddened area over the sacrum that does not blanch when pressure is applied. The client has a poor appetite and reports pain of 6 out of 10 at rest.

  1. The correct answer is B: nonblanchable redness over a bony prominence such as the sacrum is the earliest visible sign of skin breakdown and requires immediate attention before it progresses to an open pressure injury. Pain at rest reflects post-operative discomfort rather than a skin finding, the head-of-bed order alone is not itself a warning sign, and reluctance to reposition is a contributing behavior rather than the assessment finding itself.
  2. The correct answer is D: prolonged immobility combined with the client's reluctance to reposition because of pain is the direct cause of unrelieved pressure over the sacrum and the greatest contributor to further breakdown. Age alone is a risk factor but not the direct mechanism, the incision site is unrelated to sacral pressure, and the head-of-bed elevation is a precaution, not the driving cause of this client's skin risk.
  3. The correct answer is A: impaired skin integrity from ongoing unrelieved pressure is the most urgent and immediately actionable problem given the nonblanchable redness already present. Nutritional intake and anxiety are important but are not causing tissue damage in this moment, and a knowledge deficit about precautions does not explain the finding already on the skin, so skin integrity takes priority.
  4. The correct answer is C: a scheduled repositioning plan combined with a pressure-redistribution surface directly relieves and redistributes the pressure causing the sacral redness. Restricting the client to lying on the affected hip and encouraging the client to stay in one position would worsen the pressure injury risk, and heat application to compromised skin can increase tissue damage rather than protect it.
  5. The correct answer is A: the nurse must immediately relieve pressure on the affected area by repositioning the client and report the finding so the registered nurse can determine further treatment. Massaging a reddened area over a bony prominence can worsen underlying tissue damage, applying a dressing without further assessment does not address the cause, and waiting 8 hours allows the injury to progress.
  6. The correct answer is B: skin that blanches with pressure and shows no further breakdown demonstrates that circulation has been restored and the interventions successfully protected the tissue. A continued decline in appetite, an unchanged position for the full shift, and worsening pain would all indicate the plan of care has not been effective and needs to be revised.
Case Study CJ-B-BASI-2

A 70-year-old client is admitted 3 days after a left-sided ischemic stroke. The client has residual right-sided facial droop and is alert but has a weak, wet-sounding voice after swallowing water. The speech-language pathologist has not yet evaluated the client, and the client's family has brought in a favorite sandwich from home.

  1. The correct answer is D: a wet, weak voice quality after swallowing is a classic sign that liquid has entered the airway and strongly suggests dysphagia after a stroke. Facial droop reflects the neurological deficit but is not itself evidence of unsafe swallowing, alertness and orientation are reassuring findings unrelated to swallow safety, and the sandwich the family brought is a risk to manage, not an assessment finding.
  2. The correct answer is C: impaired swallowing from the recent stroke, which has not yet been formally evaluated by a speech-language pathologist, is the direct cause of this client's aspiration risk. The client's age alone does not create the risk, the family's visit is unrelated to swallowing safety, and the client's alertness is a reassuring finding rather than a contributing risk factor.
  3. The correct answer is B: risk for aspiration related to impaired swallowing is the priority because an unrecognized swallowing deficit can lead to airway compromise, a life-threatening complication. Mobility, body image, and anxiety are all valid concerns after a stroke, but none carries the same immediate risk to airway and breathing as unsafe swallowing.
  4. The correct answer is A: withholding food and oral fluids until a formal swallow evaluation is completed protects the client from aspirating while the true extent of the swallowing impairment is still unknown. Offering the sandwich or any food or thin liquids before the evaluation, or allowing the client to eat lying flat, all increase the risk of aspiration given the wet voice already noted.
  5. The correct answer is D: the nurse should explain to the family that the client must remain NPO until the swallow evaluation confirms it is safe to eat, since any oral intake before that point risks aspiration. Allowing the client to eat the sandwich, cutting it into smaller pieces, or having the family feed the client slowly all still involve unsafe oral intake before swallowing has been assessed.
  6. The correct answer is C: tolerating a modified diet without coughing or a wet voice demonstrates that the swallowing evaluation identified a safe consistency and the plan of care protected the client from aspiration. Resolution of facial droop, a request for the sandwich, or family satisfaction do not reflect whether the swallowing problem itself has been safely managed.
  7. The correct answer is C: a diet higher in fiber from sources such as bran and fresh fruit adds bulk to the stool and stimulates peristalsis, helping to prevent constipation. Limiting fluids, choosing low-fiber processed foods, and decreasing activity all slow bowel transit and worsen constipation risk rather than prevent it.
  8. The correct answer is A: high-Fowler's position leaning slightly forward with the arms supported, often called the tripod position, maximizes lung expansion and uses accessory muscles most effectively for a client with COPD and dyspnea. A flat supine position, Trendelenburg, and a knees-drawn-up lateral position all reduce diaphragmatic excursion and would worsen the client's breathing comfort.
  9. The correct answer is B: elevating the heels off the mattress with a pillow placed under the calves floats the heels and removes direct pressure, the most effective way to prevent a heel pressure injury. Vigorous massage and tight wraps can damage fragile tissue and impair circulation, and allowing the heels to rest directly on the mattress leaves them under continuous pressure.
  10. The correct answer is C: passive range-of-motion exercises should be performed slowly and gently, stopping if the client reports pain, to move the joint without causing injury. Forcing a joint quickly can tear tissue, performing the exercises only weekly allows contractures to develop, and stopping at any mild resistance rather than actual pain limits the therapeutic benefit of the exercise.
  11. The correct answer is D: a back rub combined with dimmed lighting is a well-established non-pharmacologic comfort measure that promotes relaxation and helps the client fall asleep. Bright lighting, caffeine, and television all stimulate the client and interfere with the ability to settle and rest.
  12. The correct answer is C: positioning the head to the side or placing the client in a side-lying position allows fluid used during oral care to pool and drain out of the mouth rather than into the airway, which is essential for an unconscious client. A flat supine position increases aspiration risk, infrequent oral care allows secretions and bacteria to accumulate, and large volumes of liquid mouthwash increase the amount of fluid that could be aspirated.
  13. The correct answer is A: assessing the client's weight-bearing ability and mobility level before attempting the transfer allows the nurse to determine how much assistance and what equipment are needed to keep both the client and nurse safe. Transferring the client alone without this assessment, rushing the client to stand without support, or removing footwear before standing all increase the risk of a fall or injury.
  14. The correct answer is A: scrambled eggs and mashed potatoes are soft, easily chewed foods appropriate for a mechanical soft diet after oral surgery. Raw carrots, whole nuts, a crisp salad with croutons, and a tough steak all require significant chewing and could injure the healing oral tissue.
  15. The correct answer is C: dry mucous membranes together with dark, concentrated urine are classic early signs that fluid intake is inadequate and the body is conserving water. Moist mucous membranes, pale clear urine, and skin that returns quickly after pinching are all findings of normal hydration status, not dehydration.
  16. The correct answer is D: attempting a bowel movement at a consistent time each day, such as after breakfast, takes advantage of the body's natural gastrocolic reflex and helps retrain the bowel. A random schedule, suppressing the urge to defecate, and limiting activity all work against establishing a predictable, effective bowel pattern.
  17. The correct answer is C: repositioning a client who cannot move independently at least every 2 hours is the standard interval that relieves pressure before tissue damage begins. Waiting 8 or 12 hours, or repositioning only when the client requests it, all leave tissue under prolonged, unrelieved pressure and significantly increase the risk of a pressure injury.
  18. The correct answer is B: leaning full body weight onto a moving walker is unsafe and indicates a need for further teaching, since the walker should be fully placed on the floor and stationary before any weight is applied to it. Moving the walker forward before stepping into it, ensuring all four legs are on the floor, and wearing nonskid shoes are all correct, safe techniques.
  19. The correct answer is A: ice chips and hard candy relieve the sensation of thirst using only a small amount of fluid, helping the client tolerate a fluid restriction more comfortably. Encouraging extra fluids in the morning only still exceeds the restriction, removing containers without explanation is not therapeutic communication, and telling the client the restriction will be lifted once symptoms improve may not be accurate and can build false expectations.
  20. The correct answer is D: repositioning the client onto the side allows pooled secretions to drain by gravity and reduces the noisy, labored breathing sometimes called a death rattle, which is more comfortable for both the client and family. Increasing intravenous fluids can worsen secretions, aggressive suctioning is uncomfortable and often ineffective at end of life, and keeping the client flat allows secretions to pool in the airway.
  21. The correct answer is B: supporting painful joints in a neutral, slightly extended position with pillows reduces strain on the joint while helping to prevent contractures. Prolonged flexion promotes contracture formation, avoiding all pillow support leaves joints unsupported and uncomfortable, and leaving joints uncovered does nothing to relieve the pain associated with rheumatoid arthritis.
  22. The correct answer is C: sequential compression device sleeves should fit smoothly and snugly, with about two fingers of space, to promote venous return without restricting circulation. Applying the sleeves over clothing prevents proper fit and function, leaving them off defeats the purpose of preventing venous stasis, and applying them to only one leg when both are prescribed leaves the untreated leg at continued risk.
  23. The correct answer is D: daily inspection of the feet allows early detection of cuts, blisters, or redness before they progress to a serious wound, which is essential given the reduced sensation and healing common in diabetes. Hot water soaks can cause burns that go unfelt, walking barefoot increases the risk of unnoticed injury, and toenails should be trimmed straight across rather than rounded to avoid ingrown nails.
  24. The correct answer is A: confirming the client's hearing aid and eyeglasses are in place and working before communicating ensures the client can fully participate in their own care and preserves independence and dignity. Speaking loudly from across the room without facing the client, removing the hearing aid during the day, and rushing through care without waiting for a response all reduce the client's ability to communicate and participate.
  25. The correct answer is B: a client with peripheral vascular disease is at high risk for poor wound healing and infection from even a minor nail injury, so any abnormality should be reported to the registered nurse rather than trimmed by the practical nurse. Trimming the nails, soaking the feet in hot water, or cutting the nails very short all risk causing a skin break in a client whose circulation cannot support safe healing.
  26. The correct answer is D: wiping from front to back with a clean area of the cloth for each stroke prevents transferring bacteria from the rectal area toward the urethra and reduces the risk of urinary tract infection. Wiping from back to front, reusing the same soiled area of the cloth, and using firm, vigorous pressure all increase the risk of contamination or skin irritation.
  27. The correct answer is B: engaging the client in conversation or guided imagery during a brief, mildly painful procedure is an effective non-pharmacologic distraction technique that reduces the client's focus on pain. Performing the procedure silently and quickly, asking the client to hold their breath, or leaving the client alone in the dark do nothing to reduce the perception of pain and may increase anxiety.
  28. The correct answer is C: keeping the head of the bed elevated at least 30 to 45 degrees during a continuous tube feeding uses gravity to keep formula in the stomach and significantly reduces the risk of aspiration. A flat supine position, Trendelenburg, and a lowered head with lateral positioning all allow formula to move more easily toward the airway.
  29. The correct answer is A: emptying the ostomy pouch when it is one-third to one-half full prevents the weight of the contents from pulling on the skin barrier and reduces the risk of leakage and skin breakdown. Waiting until the pouch is completely full increases the chance of leakage, changing the entire system with every emptying is unnecessary and irritates the skin, and waiting until irritation appears means the skin barrier has already failed.
  30. The correct answer is A: keeping the bed in its lowest position with the call light within reach reduces the distance and effort involved if the client attempts to get up, lowering fall risk while promoting comfort and a sense of safety. A dim room during the day can increase confusion and disorientation, placing items out of reach encourages risky reaching or unsupported movement, and a raised bed increases the distance and risk of a fall.
  31. The correct answer is D: allowing extra time and encouraging the client to perform hygiene tasks within their current ability promotes independence, self-esteem, and continued use of remaining function after a stroke. Completing all tasks for the client or discouraging self-care fosters dependence, and changing the order of tasks each day can be confusing for a client relearning routines.
  32. The correct answer is B: dimming the lights, reducing noise, and clustering nursing care activities limits interruptions and creates an environment that supports uninterrupted rest. Hourly vital sign checks, an open hallway door, and a caffeinated beverage before bedtime all disrupt or interfere with the client's ability to fall and stay asleep.
  33. The correct answer is D: plain, thin water does not meet a nectar-thickened liquid prescription and must be removed from the tray to prevent aspiration in a client with an identified swallowing risk. Thickened juice, pureed vegetables, and a thickened milkshake are all appropriate consistencies for this diet order.
  34. The correct answer is B: checking the skin every 5 to 10 minutes during a warm application allows the nurse to detect excessive redness or early signs of a burn before tissue damage occurs. Applying heat directly to unprotected skin, leaving it in place for 2 hours unchecked, and using the hottest available setting all significantly increase the risk of a thermal injury.
  35. The correct answer is B: applying an ice pack for 15 to 20 minutes at a time with breaks between applications reduces swelling effectively while preventing cold-related tissue injury such as frostbite. Continuous application for hours, placing ice directly on bare skin, and waiting until swelling has already resolved all either injure the skin or fail to provide timely relief.
  36. The correct answer is C: cleansing the skin promptly after each incontinence episode and applying a moisture barrier protects the skin from prolonged contact with urine, which can quickly cause breakdown. Allowing moisture to air dry on the skin, applying powder to already broken skin, and restricting fluids all fail to protect skin integrity and can create additional problems.
  37. The correct answer is B: cleansing the perineal area and the catheter itself from the meatus outward, moving away from the point of entry, prevents introducing bacteria into the urinary tract. Disconnecting the catheter from the tubing breaks a closed drainage system and increases infection risk, the drainage bag should always be kept below the level of the bladder to prevent backflow, and catheter care should be performed at least daily, not weekly.
  38. The correct answer is C: trying less restrictive alternatives, such as moving the client closer to the nurses' station for closer observation and covering the IV site from view, respects the client's rights and is required before a physical restraint is considered. Applying restraints immediately, sedating the client heavily, or leaving the client unattended with the site exposed either skip required alternatives or create additional safety risks.
  39. The correct answer is D: going to bed and waking at the same time each day helps regulate the body's internal sleep-wake cycle and is a foundational principle of good sleep hygiene. Vigorous exercise right before bed, a long afternoon nap, and evening caffeine all interfere with the ability to fall asleep and stay asleep.
  40. The correct answer is A: small, frequent sips of clear, cool fluids along with fresh air are simple, well-tolerated first-line comfort measures for mild nausea. Lying flat immediately after eating, offering a large heavy meal, and remaining in a warm, poorly ventilated room can all worsen nausea.
  41. The correct answer is D: having the client sit and dangle at the bedside first allows the nurse to assess for orthostatic dizziness before attempting to stand, reducing the risk of a fall during the first attempt at ambulation. Standing and walking immediately, walking the full hallway right away, or focusing on footwear before assessing tolerance all skip this important safety step.
  42. The correct answer is B: splinting the abdominal incision with a pillow during coughing supports the surgical site and reduces the discomfort and strain associated with an effective cough. Coughing forcefully without support increases pain and strain on the incision, avoiding coughing altogether risks retained secretions and pneumonia, and lying flat without support does not protect the incision.
  43. The correct answer is C: applying the gait belt snugly around the client's waist over clothing provides a secure handhold the nurse can use to support and guide the client while ambulating. A loose belt will not provide adequate support, placing it over a surgical incision causes discomfort and risks injury, and holding the client's arm alone does not offer the same control or safety as a properly applied gait belt.
  44. The correct answer is A: smooth pureed applesauce matches the ordered pureed diet consistency and is safe for a client transitioning from nothing-by-mouth status. A whole chicken breast, crunchy crackers, and a fresh salad all require chewing and are inconsistent with a pureed diet order.
  45. The correct answer is A: clustering nursing care activities allows the nurse to complete necessary tasks together, leaving longer uninterrupted periods for the client to rest. Checking vital signs every single hour, waking the client for a full bath overnight, and scheduling assessments in the middle of the night all fragment the client's rest unnecessarily.
  46. The correct answer is D: a footboard or high-top athletic shoes maintain the foot in a neutral, dorsiflexed position and help prevent the Achilles tendon from shortening, which causes foot drop. Leaving the feet unsupported, positioning them in plantar flexion, or removing footwear without any supportive device all fail to prevent this complication of prolonged bedrest.
  47. The correct answers are A, C, D, and F: repositioning at least every 2 hours, using a pressure-redistribution mattress, keeping the skin clean and dry, and minimizing friction and shearing during moves are all evidence-based interventions that reduce pressure injury risk. Keeping the head of the bed elevated above 30 degrees actually increases shearing force on the sacrum and should be minimized, and vigorously massaging reddened bony areas can cause further tissue damage rather than prevent it.
  48. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: keeping the bed low, ensuring the call light is reachable, maintaining a clutter-free and well-lit floor, and providing nonskid footwear are all standard fall-prevention interventions. Encouraging an older adult at high fall risk to ambulate alone at night without assistance directly increases fall risk, and removing eyeglasses at bedtime does not prevent falls and could increase confusion if the client needs to see clearly overnight.
  49. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: adequate urine output, moist mucous membranes, quickly returning skin turgor, and a stable body weight are all findings consistent with normal hydration status. Dark amber urine and dry, cracked lips are both signs of dehydration, not adequate hydration.
  50. The correct answers are A, B, C, E, and F: dimming the lights, clustering care to reduce interruptions, offering a caffeine-free warm beverage, reducing hallway noise, and encouraging a consistent bedtime routine all support better sleep. Performing a complete bath at 2 a.m. unnecessarily interrupts the client's sleep and works directly against the goal of promoting rest.
  51. The correct answers are A, B, D, and F: a nonblanchable reddened area, an open stage 2 pressure injury, a dark blister-like area suggesting deep tissue injury, and new drainage on a dressing are all abnormal findings that must be reported to the registered nurse. Warm, dry, intact skin with no redness and skin that blanches normally with light pressure are both normal findings that do not require reporting.
  52. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: bending at the knees with a straight back, keeping the load close to the body, using assistive devices when available, and widening the base of support all protect the caregiver and client from injury during repositioning. Twisting at the waist while lifting places dangerous strain on the spine, and lifting a heavy load alone without help increases the risk of injury to both the caregiver and the client.
  53. The correct answers are A, C, E, and F: refined breads and pastas, well-cooked and peeled vegetables, tender well-cooked poultry, and white rice are all low-fiber foods appropriate for a low-residue diet. Raw vegetables with the skin left on and whole bran cereal are both high in fiber and residue and should be avoided on this diet.
  54. The correct answers are A, C, E, and F: small, frequent sips of cool clear fluids, fresh air, slow deep breathing, and a quiet environment with minimal strong odors are all effective non-pharmacologic measures for nausea. A large, high-fat meal and strong perfumes or scented lotions are both likely to trigger or worsen nausea.
  55. The correct answers are A, C, D, and E: checking the skin frequently, limiting the application to the prescribed time, using a covering to maintain temperature, and assessing for pain or excessive redness all promote safe use of a warm compress. Applying a compress directly to an open wound without a barrier or using the hottest available temperature both risk burning or further damaging the tissue.
  56. The correct answers are A, C, E, and F: a soft, formed stool without straining, a consistent daily time for bowel movements, active bowel sounds, and recognizing and acting on the urge to defecate all indicate the bowel training program is working. Abdominal cramping and bloating, along with straining and incomplete emptying, indicate the plan has not yet been effective.
  57. The correct answers are A, C, and E: dressing the weaker arm first, undressing the stronger side first, and allowing the client to participate using the stronger arm all follow the standard principle of dressing the affected side first and undressing it last to make the process easier for the client. Dressing the stronger arm first and undressing the weaker side first reverse this principle and make dressing more difficult, and completing every task for the client without allowing participation reduces independence unnecessarily.
  58. The correct answers are A, B, D, and F: removing the dentures before cleaning, cleaning them over a padded or water-filled basin to prevent breakage, brushing the client's gums and tongue, and handling the dentures carefully all promote safe and thorough denture care. Hot or boiling water can warp dentures, and storing dentures dry overnight can cause them to lose their shape, so they should be stored in water or a denture solution.
  59. The correct answers are A, C, D, and E: range-of-motion exercises, trochanter rolls for leg alignment, a regular repositioning schedule, and a footboard to support the feet all help prevent contractures in a client with limited mobility. Keeping joints in the same flexed position at all times and avoiding all repositioning both directly promote the shortening of muscles and tendons that leads to contractures.
  60. The correct answers are A, C, D, and F: explaining each step beforehand, offering distraction, giving analgesics with enough time to take effect, and allowing the client to ask questions all reduce the client's anxiety and perceived pain during the procedure. Rushing through the procedure without explanation and ignoring the client's reports of discomfort both increase anxiety and fail to address the client's comfort needs.
  61. The correct answer is D: a diet strained and blended to a smooth, pudding-like consistency with no lumps is defined as a pureed diet. A clear liquid diet contains only transparent fluids, a mechanical soft diet includes foods that are soft but not necessarily blended smooth, and a full liquid diet includes any liquid or food that becomes liquid at room or body temperature, neither of which describes a blended, lump-free consistency.
  62. The correct answer is A: positioning a client on their side with support along the back and one leg forward describes the lateral, or side-lying, position. The supine position has the client lying flat on the back, the prone position has the client lying face down, and high-Fowler's position has the head of the bed raised to a near-sitting angle, none of which match the description given.
  63. The correct answer is C: a footboard is the device placed at the foot of the bed to keep the feet in a neutral, dorsiflexed position and prevent foot drop from prolonged plantar flexion. A trochanter roll supports the hip and leg in proper alignment, a trapeze bar assists the client with repositioning and transfers, and an abductor pillow keeps the hips separated after certain surgeries, none of which support the feet in this way.
  64. The correct answer is B: continuous, unrelieved pressure over a bony area places the client at risk for a pressure injury, in which reduced blood flow leads to tissue damage. A contracture results from prolonged joint immobility rather than pressure alone, a muscle strain results from overstretching or overuse, and a deep vein thrombosis results from blood clot formation related to venous stasis, not directly from skin pressure.
  65. The correct answers are Blank 1: A and Blank 2: A: after a total hip arthroplasty, the client must avoid flexing the operative hip beyond 90 degrees and avoid crossing the legs, since both movements can cause the new hip joint to dislocate. Extending or externally rotating the hip and elevating or exercising the legs are not the specific movements associated with the standard hip precautions taught after this surgery.
  66. The correct answers are Blank 1: A and Blank 2: A: increasing dietary fiber adds bulk to the stool while increasing physical activity stimulates intestinal motility, and together these are first-line, non-pharmacologic measures to prevent constipation. Refined sugar, saturated fat, and sodium do not add stool bulk, and sedative use, extra caffeine, and sleep alone do not stimulate bowel motility the way physical activity does.
  67. The correct answers are Blank 1: B and Blank 2: A: keeping the head of the bed elevated at least 30 degrees during a continuous tube feeding uses gravity to reduce the risk of aspiration, and gastric residual volume should always be checked according to facility policy to ensure consistent, evidence-based practice. Elevating the head to only 10 degrees does not adequately reduce aspiration risk, elevating to 60 or 90 degrees is unnecessary and uncomfortable for a client on continuous feeding, and residual checks should never be based on personal preference or convenience rather than established protocol.
  68. The correct answers are Blank 1: A and Blank 2: A: cleansing the skin promptly after each incontinence episode removes irritating moisture quickly, and applying a moisture barrier product protects the skin from ongoing exposure to urine. Cleansing only once daily or once weekly allows prolonged contact with moisture, and a tight wrap, powder alone, or a hot compress do not provide the same protective barrier function.
  69. The correct answer is B: the procedure should begin by explaining it to the client and gathering needed help, then lowering the head of the bed to reduce friction, then positioning the slide sheet under the client, then repositioning the client, and finally removing or adjusting the slide sheet once the client is settled. Option A skips explaining the procedure before beginning, option C attempts to reposition the client before the slide sheet is even in place, and option D removes the slide sheet before the client has actually been repositioned.
  70. The correct answer is C: correct hand hygiene begins by turning on the water and adjusting the temperature, then wetting the hands, then applying soap and lathering for at least 20 seconds, then rinsing thoroughly, and finally drying the hands and turning off the faucet with the towel to avoid recontamination. Option A applies soap before wetting the hands is out of order relative to turning on the water, option B places multiple steps out of sequence including drying before rinsing, and option D applies soap before the hands have been wetted.
  71. The correct answer is A: the nurse should first assess the client's vital signs before getting out of bed, then assist the client to sit and dangle, then have the client stand with support, then walk a short distance using a gait belt, and finally assist the client back to bed and reassess tolerance. Option B skips the vital sign assessment before getting out of bed, and options C and D both attempt to have the client stand or walk before completing the earlier safety steps in the correct order.
  72. The correct answer is D: the nurse should first trim any excess pubic hair away from the shaft, then cleanse and dry the penis thoroughly, then apply the condom sheath leaving a small space at the tip, then secure it with the supplied strip without creating a tight seal, and finally connect the drainage tubing. Options A, B, and C each place cleansing, hair trimming, or sheath application out of the correct order, which can lead to skin irritation, poor adhesion, or a compromised seal.
  73. The correct answers are A, B, and C: nonblanchable redness over the sacrum, remaining in the same position for 6 hours, and new tingling in the heels are all findings that suggest early tissue compromise from unrelieved pressure and require the nurse to reposition the client and complete a full skin assessment right away. A comfortable room temperature and an improved appetite are reassuring findings unrelated to skin breakdown risk and do not require this action.
  74. The correct answers are A and B: a pain rating that dropped from 6 to 2 out of 10 and a client who appears comfortable with a relaxed facial expression both demonstrate that the back rub and repositioning successfully reduced the client's pain. Continued guarding of the lower back and refusal to reposition indicate the pain is still poorly controlled and the interventions have not yet been effective.
  75. The correct answers are A, B, and C: dry, cracked oral mucosa, dark and reduced urine output, and skin that remains tented after being pinched are all classic signs of dehydration in an older adult. A newly noted change in orientation supports the concern but was not one of the bracketed findings, and an unchanged weight this morning does not by itself indicate dehydration since acute fluid loss may not yet be reflected on the scale.
  76. The correct answers are Row 1: Expected, Row 2: Unexpected, Row 3: Expected, Row 4: Unexpected, Row 5: Expected: mild pinkness, a warm and more comfortable sensation, and slightly improved swelling are all expected responses to a properly applied warm compress. A burning sensation with blistering, or numb and pale skin, both indicate tissue injury from the heat and mean the compress must be discontinued immediately.
  77. The correct answers are Row 1: Correct, Row 2: Incorrect, Row 3: Correct, Row 4: Incorrect, Row 5: Correct: moving the walker forward before stepping into it, checking that all four legs are on the floor before stepping, and wearing nonskid shoes are all correct, safe walker techniques. Leaning heavily onto a walker that is still moving and stepping well past the walker with each step are both unsafe and increase the client's risk of falling.
  78. The correct answers are Row 1: Expected, Row 2: Notify the RN, Row 3: Expected, Row 4: Notify the RN, Row 5: Expected: a soft, formed stool every 1 to 2 days, mild transient gas and bloating, and an improved ability to recognize the urge to defecate are all expected responses to a high-fiber, high-fluid bowel program. Severe pain with no bowel movement in 5 days suggests possible obstruction or impaction, and bright red blood in the stool suggests bleeding, both of which must be reported to the registered nurse rather than treated as expected findings.
  79. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: 6 days without a bowel movement combined with abdominal distention, along with liquid stool leaking around a sensation of continued rectal fullness, are classic findings of fecal impaction that the nurse must recognize. The priority action is to notify the registered nurse and prepare to assist with further assessment rather than encouraging straining or giving a laxative without evaluation, and success is shown by the client passing a soft, formed stool with decreased distention and resolution of the liquid stool leakage.
  80. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: forceful coughing with the client still able to speak, along with a red face and watery eyes, indicate a partial rather than a complete airway obstruction that the nurse must recognize correctly. The priority action is to stay with the client, encourage the client's own forceful cough, and stop the meal, since abdominal thrusts are reserved for a complete obstruction when the client cannot cough, speak, or breathe, and success is shown by the airway clearing with the client's cough and respiratory effort returning to baseline.
Case Study CJ-B-PHAR-1

A client is receiving morphine via patient-controlled analgesia (PCA) pump following abdominal surgery performed 6 hours ago. The practical nurse notes the client is difficult to arouse, with a respiratory rate of 8 breaths/min and shallow respirations. Oxygen saturation is 89% on room air. The client's spouse states the client pressed the PCA button frequently overnight because of pain.

  1. The correct answer is C: a respiratory rate of 8 breaths/min in a client who is difficult to arouse is the priority finding, since it directly signals opioid-induced respiratory depression, a life-threatening complication. Frequent PCA button use, recent surgery, and the spouse's presence are all contextual information but do not by themselves indicate the client's current respiratory status is compromised.
  2. The correct answer is D: excess opioid administration from frequent PCA button use overnight is the direct cause of the client's current sedation and slowed, shallow respirations. The surgery itself, the spouse's presence, and the client's pain level before surgery are all part of the clinical picture but are not the direct physiologic cause of the current respiratory depression.
  3. The correct answer is A: risk for respiratory depression related to opioid administration is the priority problem because untreated respiratory depression can rapidly progress to respiratory arrest. Anxiety, infection risk, and impaired mobility are all appropriate post-operative concerns, but none carries the same immediate, life-threatening urgency as a compromised respiratory status.
  4. The correct answer is B: stopping the PCA pump and notifying the registered nurse immediately prevents further opioid administration and allows prompt evaluation and treatment of the client's respiratory depression. Increasing the dose or encouraging more frequent button use would worsen the client's condition, and simply documenting and waiting 4 hours delays care the client needs right now.
  5. The correct answer is A: the nurse should first attempt to stimulate the client, apply supplemental oxygen, and stop the PCA pump while notifying the registered nurse, since these actions address the airway and breathing emergency immediately. Allowing the client to continue sleeping, giving another dose of morphine, or asking the spouse to leave before assessing the client all delay the urgent intervention this situation requires.
  6. The correct answer is C: a respiratory rate returning to 14 breaths/min with oxygen saturation above 94% and the client easily aroused shows that the opioid effect has been reversed or has resolved and the client's respiratory status has stabilized. A further decrease in respiratory rate, the spouse leaving the hospital, or the client pressing the PCA button again immediately would all indicate the situation has not improved or that the intervention has failed.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

Case Study CJ-B-PHAR-2

A 78-year-old client with a history of heart failure is admitted for increasing shortness of breath and bilateral ankle swelling. Home medications include digoxin 0.125 mg daily and furosemide 40 mg twice daily. The client reports nausea, poor appetite, and seeing yellow-green halos around lights. Vital signs show a heart rate of 52/min that is irregular, blood pressure 108/64 mmHg, and oxygen saturation 94% on room air. The most recent potassium level is 3.0 mEq/L.

  1. The correct answer is C: a heart rate of 52/min that is irregular is the most concerning finding given this client's digoxin and furosemide regimen, since it raises suspicion for digoxin toxicity and needs to be recognized first. Blood pressure of 108/64 mmHg is a mildly low but not immediately alarming value in a client with heart failure. Bilateral ankle swelling is an expected finding related to the client's heart failure diagnosis. Oxygen saturation of 94% is only slightly below normal and is less urgent than the new bradycardia.
  2. The correct answer is D: a potassium level of 3.0 mEq/L reflects hypokalemia, which enhances digoxin's effect on the heart and best explains the client's new bradycardia and visual disturbances. Blood pressure of 108/64 mmHg does not explain a cardiac rhythm change or visual symptoms. Oxygen saturation of 94% does not account for the specific pattern of bradycardia and visual halos described. Respiratory rate of 20/min is within a normal range and is not linked to this presentation.
  3. The correct answer is A: the combination of digoxin therapy, hypokalemia, bradycardia, nausea, and visual halos should lead the nurse to prioritize digoxin toxicity related to hypokalemia as the primary concern. Community-acquired pneumonia is not supported by the assessment findings described, which do not include respiratory infection signs. Anxiety related to hospitalization does not explain the cardiac and visual findings. Dehydration from poor oral intake does not account for the specific pattern of bradycardia and visual disturbance seen here.
  4. The correct answer is B: requesting an order to hold the next digoxin dose and notifying the registered nurse directly addresses the suspected digoxin toxicity, the client's priority problem. Encouraging increased fluid intake does not address the medication-related cause of the client's symptoms. Repositioning to high Fowler's position may help with breathing but does not resolve the suspected toxicity. Applying oxygen addresses the mildly low saturation but does not address the more urgent cardiac and electrolyte concern.
  5. The correct answer is D: withholding the scheduled digoxin dose and reporting the low potassium level to the registered nurse is the priority first action to prevent worsening digoxin toxicity. Administering the scheduled furosemide dose could further lower potassium and worsen the risk. Documenting and reassessing in four hours delays action on findings that need prompt intervention. Asking the client to describe the visual disturbance further is reasonable assessment but is not the priority action given the safety concern already identified.
  6. The correct answer is C: a regular heart rate of 76/min along with a potassium level of 4.2 mEq/L, both within normal limits, indicates the client's digoxin toxicity risk has resolved and the condition has improved. A heart rate of 50/min with a potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L shows both findings are still abnormal. Worsening nausea suggests the client's condition has not improved. Continued visual halos are a sign that toxicity risk persists rather than resolving.
Case Study CJ-B-PHAR-3

A 65-year-old client taking warfarin for atrial fibrillation began a 7-day course of an oral antibiotic 4 days ago for a urinary tract infection. The client now reports two nosebleeds today and new bruising on both forearms. Vital signs are heart rate 92/min, blood pressure 132/84 mmHg, and temperature 37.1 degrees Celsius. The client's international normalized ratio (INR) this morning is 5.8, compared with 2.4 two weeks ago.

  1. The correct answer is C: an INR of 5.8, far above the therapeutic range, is the priority finding because it places this client at significant risk for serious bleeding. A heart rate of 92/min is only mildly elevated and less urgent than the markedly abnormal INR. A blood pressure of 132/84 mmHg is a common, unremarkable finding. A temperature of 37.1 degrees Celsius is within the normal range and not a priority concern.
  2. The correct answer is A: many oral antibiotics interact with warfarin by altering its metabolism or gut flora that produces vitamin K, which can significantly raise the INR, best explaining this client's rise from 2.4 to 5.8. The client's diagnosis of atrial fibrillation is the reason warfarin was prescribed but does not explain a sudden change in INR. A blood pressure of 132/84 mmHg has no established effect on INR. The length of the antibiotic course alone, without the interaction itself, does not explain the change in INR.
  3. The correct answer is B: risk for bleeding related to supratherapeutic anticoagulation is the priority problem, given the markedly elevated INR and active bleeding signs such as nosebleeds and bruising. Risk for infection related to an incomplete antibiotic course is not supported, since the client is still within the prescribed course. Anxiety related to a new diagnosis is not indicated by the findings presented. Risk for falls related to hypotension is not supported, since the client's blood pressure is not low.
  4. The correct answer is C: notifying the registered nurse or provider of the elevated INR and active bleeding signs is the appropriate action so the anticoagulation can be reassessed and managed safely. Encouraging leafy green vegetable intake does not address the client's current bleeding risk and would not be an immediate priority. Continuing the antibiotic course with no further action ignores a significant, reportable change in the client's condition. Instructing the client to resume normal activity without restriction could increase injury and bleeding risk while the INR remains elevated.
  5. The correct answer is A: applying direct pressure to any active bleeding and holding the next warfarin dose pending further orders addresses the client's immediate safety needs first. Documenting and continuing the shift assessment delays action on an active bleeding risk that needs immediate attention. Having the client walk in the hallway is not an appropriate priority action when active bleeding and a markedly elevated INR are present. Administering the next scheduled warfarin dose would further increase the client's bleeding risk and is contraindicated in this situation.
  6. The correct answer is A: an INR decreasing to 2.6, a therapeutic level, with no new bruising or bleeding indicates the client's excess anticoagulation and bleeding risk have improved. An INR remaining at 5.8 with a new nosebleed shows the bleeding risk is unchanged or worsening. An additional episode of gum bleeding also indicates the problem has not resolved. Increasing bruising on the forearms is a sign the condition is worsening rather than improving.
  7. The correct answer is B: opioids depress the central nervous system and can cause life-threatening respiratory depression, so respiratory rate and sedation level are the priority assessments after administration. Blood pressure is monitored routinely but is not the most urgent concern with opioid therapy. Bowel sounds relate to opioid-induced constipation, a longer-term effect, not an immediate safety priority. Skin turgor reflects hydration status and is unrelated to opioid safety monitoring.
  8. The correct answer is D: protamine sulfate binds to heparin and neutralizes its anticoagulant effect, making it the antidote used for heparin-induced bleeding. Vitamin K reverses the effect of warfarin, a different anticoagulant with a different mechanism of action. Naloxone reverses opioid effects and has no role in anticoagulant reversal. Flumazenil reverses benzodiazepine sedation and is unrelated to heparin therapy.
  9. The correct answer is A: a persistent dry cough is a well-documented effect of angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors caused by increased bradykinin levels, and it should be reported so the provider can decide whether to switch medication classes. The cough is not a sign of pneumonia, and there is no fever or other infectious symptom described. Doubling the dose would not resolve a class-related cough and could worsen hypotension. Because this cough is a recognized medication effect, telling the client it needs no follow-up is inaccurate.
  10. The correct answer is C: regular insulin peaks 2 to 4 hours after administration, so a 0700 dose places the highest hypoglycemia risk between approximately 0900 and 1100, and the nurse should monitor closely during this window. The period right after the injection, 0700 to 0730, falls before the insulin has reached peak effect. Four to six hours later is past the typical peak for regular insulin. Waiting until just before the next meal would miss the actual peak window entirely.
  11. The correct answer is B: clindamycin carries a well-known risk of disrupting normal intestinal flora and allowing Clostridioides difficile to overgrow, which presents as frequent watery diarrhea and requires prompt reporting. Mild fatigue at the end of the day is a nonspecific finding not linked to this antibiotic. Occasional dry mouth is a minor, common complaint unrelated to a serious antibiotic complication. A slight decrease in appetite alone is not the hallmark warning sign associated with this medication.
  12. The correct answer is A: prolonged corticosteroid use suppresses the adrenal glands' own cortisol production, so stopping the drug abruptly can cause a life-threatening adrenal crisis, and the dose must be tapered gradually under provider guidance. Stopping now because symptoms have resolved ignores this suppression risk. Doubling the dose before stopping is not a recognized tapering method and could worsen steroid side effects. Describing abrupt discontinuation as only mild and temporary understates a genuinely dangerous risk.
  13. The correct answer is D: rapid intravenous administration of furosemide, a loop diuretic, has been linked to ototoxicity, including transient or permanent hearing loss and tinnitus, so it should be given slowly over the recommended time. A persistent dry cough is associated with angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors, not loop diuretics. Gingival hyperplasia is a known effect of phenytoin, an anticonvulsant. Peripheral neuropathy is more commonly linked to isoniazid, not to furosemide.
  14. The correct answer is C: phenytoin is well known to cause gingival hyperplasia, and consistent oral hygiene along with regular dental follow-up helps minimize gum overgrowth and its complications. Vitamin K intake relates to warfarin therapy and clotting, not to phenytoin's gum effects. Avoiding sunlight relates to photosensitizing medications such as tetracycline, not phenytoin. An antifungal mouth rinse addresses oral thrush, a different problem unrelated to gingival hyperplasia.
  15. The correct answer is D: a coarse tremor, persistent vomiting, and ataxia together suggest lithium toxicity, which requires prompt notification of the registered nurse and holding the next dose until the client is evaluated. These findings are not expected, mild effects of lithium and should not be dismissed with reassurance. Increasing sodium intake is unrelated and would not address a toxic lithium level. Taking the next dose with food does not resolve the underlying toxicity and delays needed intervention.
  16. The correct answer is B: a digoxin level of 1.2 ng/mL falls within the generally accepted therapeutic range of about 0.5 to 2.0 ng/mL, and the client has no signs of toxicity, so the scheduled dose should be given as prescribed. Withholding the dose and notifying the registered nurse is unnecessary when the level and assessment are both reassuring. Doubling the dose is unsafe and could push the level into a toxic range. Delaying the dose for a repeat level is not indicated when the current result is already within range.
  17. The correct answer is C: beta blockers such as metoprolol slow the heart rate further, and an apical pulse of 52 beats per minute is generally below the safe threshold to administer the dose, so it should be withheld and the registered nurse notified. Giving the full dose or documenting without action ignores this safety parameter. Giving half the dose without a prescriber order is not within the nurse's authority. Having the client walk to raise the heart rate before administering the medication is not an appropriate way to bypass a low pulse parameter.
  18. The correct answer is A: combining a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as ibuprofen with a corticosteroid such as prednisone significantly increases the risk of gastrointestinal irritation and bleeding, so the client should check with the provider before combining them. Stating there is no interaction is inaccurate and could place the client at risk. Ibuprofen does not eliminate the therapeutic effect of prednisone. A persistent dry cough is a known effect of angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors, not a result of this combination.
  19. The correct answer is D: ondansetron can further prolong the QT interval, increasing the risk of a dangerous arrhythmia in a client with a known prolonged QT interval, so the nurse should notify the registered nurse or provider before administering it. Giving the medication without addressing this risk could endanger the client. Withholding all antiemetics is an overly broad response when the concern is specific to this one medication's cardiac effect. Giving the medication with water does not reduce its cardiac risk, since ondansetron is administered intravenously in this scenario.
  20. The correct answer is A: albuterol is a beta agonist bronchodilator, and mild tachycardia and tremor are common, expected effects related to its stimulation of beta receptors, though the client should still be monitored. Instructing the client to stop the inhaler permanently is unwarranted for an expected, mild effect during an asthma exacerbation. These findings do not indicate a severe allergic reaction, which would include symptoms such as facial swelling or difficulty breathing beyond the asthma itself. Doubling the dose would worsen the tachycardia and tremor rather than address the underlying asthma safely.
  21. The correct answer is B: oxybutynin has anticholinergic properties that can paradoxically cause urinary retention, and difficulty starting a stream with incomplete emptying should be recognized and reported rather than dismissed. This is not the intended therapeutic response, which is a reduction in urgency and frequency, not new retention. It is not a sign of an underdose, since retention is a known adverse effect rather than a dosing problem. Because urinary retention can lead to complications such as infection, it is not a finding to ignore.
  22. The correct answer is C: sudden muscle rigidity, neck stiffness, and involuntary eye deviation shortly after starting an antipsychotic such as haloperidol are classic signs of acute dystonia, an extrapyramidal symptom requiring prompt reporting and treatment. This is not an expected calming effect and represents a distressing, involuntary muscular reaction. The presentation does not match a typical seizure, which involves altered consciousness and generalized convulsive movement. It is not a skin reaction, since the findings described involve muscle tone and eye movement, not the skin.
  23. The correct answer is A: muscle aches, weakness, and dark urine in a client taking a statin such as atorvastatin can indicate myopathy or rhabdomyolysis, a serious muscle breakdown that requires prompt reporting and further evaluation. These findings are not simply expected mild soreness and should not be dismissed without assessment. Increasing physical activity could worsen muscle injury if rhabdomyolysis is occurring. Changing the timing of the dose does not address the possible underlying muscle toxicity described.
  24. The correct answer is D: prolonged use of proton pump inhibitors such as omeprazole has been associated with an increased risk of bone fractures and vitamin B12 deficiency, and clients should be informed of these risks. Long-term use does not eliminate the need for bone density monitoring, it increases the reason for it. Proton pump inhibitors are not commonly linked to irreversible liver damage. Stating there are no risks beyond the first year is inaccurate, since these risks are specifically tied to prolonged, ongoing use.
  25. The correct answer is B: vitamin C enhances iron absorption, and clients should be told that darker or black stools are an expected, harmless effect of oral iron rather than a sign of bleeding. Calcium-rich dairy products actually reduce iron absorption and should be avoided close to the dose. Stopping the supplement for darker stools is unnecessary, since this color change is expected. Black tea contains tannins that can reduce iron absorption, so it is not recommended to take with the dose.
  26. The correct answer is C: aminoglycosides such as gentamicin carry known risks of nephrotoxicity and ototoxicity, so serum creatinine and any reports of hearing changes or tinnitus should be monitored closely throughout therapy. Blood glucose is not a primary concern with this antibiotic class. Liver enzymes and abdominal girth are not the primary monitoring parameters for aminoglycoside toxicity.   27. The correct answer is D: enoxaparin should be injected subcutaneously at a 90 degree angle without aspirating, and the site should not be massaged afterward, since both aspirating and massaging increase the risk of bruising and hematoma formation.   28.   30.   31.   33.   34. The correct answers are A, C, and E: a pain rating of 2 out of 10, active participation in physical therapy without hesitation, and the client reporting that pain interferes less with movement all indicate effective pain control. A respiratory rate of 8 breaths per minute with difficulty arousing the client is a sign of oversedation and respiratory depression, not effective pain relief. Sleeping only 30 minutes before being disturbed and having pinpoint, nonreactive pupils are also concerning findings rather than signs that pain is well controlled.
  70. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: tinnitus or hearing loss, a rising serum creatinine level, decreased urine output, and dizziness or unsteady gait are all recognized signs of aminoglycoside-related ototoxicity or nephrotoxicity that should be reported. An improved appetite is unrelated to this medication's known toxicities. Clear, pale yellow urine is a normal, reassuring finding rather than a sign of toxicity.
  71. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: taking the medication on an empty stomach in the morning, separating it from calcium or iron supplements by at least four hours, reporting palpitations, tremor, or unintended weight loss, and never discontinuing the medication without provider guidance are all appropriate teaching points. Full symptom relief is not expected within the first two days, since thyroid hormone replacement typically takes weeks to reach full effect, so D is incorrect.   72. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: increased thirst, increased urination, blurred vision, and a fingerstick blood glucose of 220 mg/dL are all findings consistent with corticosteroid-induced hyperglycemia and should be reported. Decreased appetite is not a typical sign of hyperglycemia. Cool, clammy skin is more consistent with hypoglycemia, the opposite condition, rather than an elevated blood glucose.
  73.   74.   75. The correct answer is C: atorvastatin, a statin medication, carries a known risk of myopathy and rhabdomyolysis, so new muscle pain, tenderness, or dark urine should be reported promptly.   76. The correct answer is D: nausea, visual disturbances, bradycardia, and an elevated digoxin level together are the classic presentation of digoxin toxicity, and the finding should prompt further evaluation and reporting. Hypoglycemia would present with different findings, such as shakiness, diaphoresis, and confusion, and is not linked to digoxin level. Serotonin syndrome results from serotonergic medications, not digoxin. Anaphylaxis is an acute allergic reaction and does not match this gradual presentation tied to a rising digoxin level.
  77. The correct answer is B: vitamin K helps restore the body's ability to produce clotting factors that warfarin suppresses, making it the appropriate treatment to help reverse warfarin's anticoagulant effect. Protamine sulfate is the antidote for heparin, a different anticoagulant that works through a different mechanism. Naloxone reverses opioid effects and has no role in anticoagulant reversal. Flumazenil reverses benzodiazepine sedation and is also unrelated to warfarin.
  78. The correct answer is A: protamine sulfate binds to heparin and neutralizes its anticoagulant effect, making it the specific antidote used for heparin-induced bleeding. Vitamin K reverses warfarin, not heparin, since the two anticoagulants work through entirely different mechanisms. Naloxone reverses opioid effects and has no role in reversing heparin. Activated charcoal is used for certain oral overdoses and would not reverse an intravenous heparin infusion already in the bloodstream.
  79. The correct answer is C: naloxone is an opioid antagonist that reverses respiratory depression and sedation caused by opioid overdose, matching this client's presentation. Flumazenil reverses benzodiazepine sedation, not opioid effects, and is not the correct choice here. Atropine treats symptomatic bradycardia and does not reverse opioid-induced respiratory depression. Protamine sulfate reverses heparin's anticoagulant effect and has no role in opioid toxicity.
  80. The correct answer is B: angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors are well known to cause a persistent dry cough due to increased bradykinin levels, and this effect is a common reason clients request a different medication class. A rash is not the hallmark effect associated with this medication class. A tremor is more commonly linked to medications such as bronchodilators or lithium, not to this drug class. A headache can occur with many antihypertensives but is not the specific, well-documented effect unique to this class.
  81. Practice Test 9 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A: in suspected anaphylaxis, the priority is to stop the causative infusion immediately, keep the IV line open with normal saline for emergency access, call for help, and continuously monitor the client's airway, breathing, and vital signs. Option B delays stopping the causative antibiotic, allowing continued allergen exposure. Option C delays calling for help until after monitoring has begun. Option D delays stopping the offending medication until after monitoring, prolonging exposure to the allergen.
  2. The correct answer is C: verifying the prescribed insulin type, dose, and recent glucose result first, then attaching and priming a new needle, dialing the prescribed dose, cleansing the site, and inserting the needle follows safe technique for a subcutaneous insulin pen injection. Option A dials the dose before the needle has even been attached and primed. Option B attaches and primes the needle before the order has been verified. Option D cleanses the site before the correct dose has been dialed, an inefficient and less safe sequence.
  3. The correct answer is C: obtaining a complete home medication list first, comparing it against the admission orders, clarifying any discrepancies with the prescriber or pharmacist, and then documenting the reconciled list follows the correct medication reconciliation process. Option A compares the lists before the home medication list has even been obtained. Option B attempts to clarify discrepancies before the two lists have been compared, so no discrepancies would yet be identified. Option D documents the reconciled list before discrepancies have been clarified, risking an inaccurate record.
  4. The correct answers are A, B, and C: a respiratory rate of 8 breaths per minute that is shallow, an oxygen saturation of 89%, and pinpoint pupils that react minimally to light together indicate opioid-induced respiratory depression and central nervous system sedation severe enough to require immediate reporting and likely naloxone administration. Drowsiness that is arousable, a pain rating of 2 out of 10, and present bowel sounds are expected or reassuring findings that do not indicate toxicity.
  5. The correct answers are A, B, and C: visual disturbances such as yellow-green halos, a resting apical pulse below 60 beats per minute, and new nausea with vomiting are all classic early signs of digoxin toxicity and must be reported before another dose is given. An apical pulse of 96 beats per minute, warm dry skin, and independent ambulation are all normal or unrelated findings that do not suggest toxicity.
  6. The correct answers are A, B, and C: a coarse worsening tremor, new confusion with slurred speech, and muscle weakness with an unsteady gait are classic signs of lithium toxicity, which is more likely now because vomiting and diarrhea have caused fluid loss that raises the serum lithium level. Mild fatigue, a decreased appetite from the viral illness, and a normal temperature are expected findings during an intercurrent illness and do not by themselves indicate toxicity.
  7. The correct answers are Row 1: Hold and Notify RN, Row 2: Hold and Notify RN, Row 3: Administer, Row 4: Hold and Notify RN, Row 5: Administer: an apical pulse of 52/min is below the threshold for safely giving a beta-blocker, so the nurse should hold the metoprolol and notify the registered nurse. A potassium of 3.1 mEq/L is already low, so giving a diuretic that further lowers potassium would be unsafe without notifying the registered nurse first. Levothyroxine has no contraindication described and should be administered as scheduled. A blood glucose of 58 mg/dL is hypoglycemic, so the sliding-scale insulin should be held and the low glucose treated and reported instead of administering more insulin. Acetaminophen for a temperature of 38.1 degrees Celsius and pain of 5 out of 10 is an appropriate, safe medication to administer.
  8. The correct answers are Row 1: Expected side effect, Row 2: Adverse effect - report, Row 3: Expected side effect, Row 4: Adverse effect - report, Row 5: Expected side effect: a dry cough, mild orthostatic dizziness, and a slight headache are common, expected effects of an ACE inhibitor like lisinopril and do not usually require the medication to be stopped. Facial and lip swelling with difficulty breathing describes angioedema, a rare but life-threatening reaction that must be reported immediately, and a potassium of 6.4 mEq/L is dangerously elevated hyperkalemia, a known adverse effect of ACE inhibitors that also requires immediate reporting.
  9. The correct answers are Row 1: Within Therapeutic Range, Row 2: Notify RN, Row 3: Notify RN, Row 4: Within Therapeutic Range, Row 5: Notify RN: the therapeutic INR range for a client on warfarin for atrial fibrillation is generally 2.0 to 3.0, so results of 2.3 and 2.9 fall within that range. An INR of 5.8 is far above therapeutic range and increases bleeding risk, and an INR of 1.1 is subtherapeutic and increases clotting risk, so both must be reported. An INR of 4.5 is already above range, and blood in the stool is an active bleeding sign, making this the most urgent finding to report.
  10. The correct answers are Condition/Cue 1: A, Condition/Cue 2: A, Priority Action: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 1: A, Expected Outcome/Parameter 2: A: a respiratory rate of 6 breaths per minute and pinpoint pupils together indicate opioid-induced respiratory depression severe enough to require reversal, so the priority action is to administer naloxone per protocol and be ready to support the client's breathing. The two parameters that show whether the reversal worked are the respiratory rate rising above 12 breaths per minute and the client's level of consciousness returning to baseline alertness, since both directly reflect whether the opioid effect has been reversed.
  11.
